E_s[X(t_1) X(t_2)] = P(\left \{ X(t_1)=1 \text{ and } X(t_2)=1 \right \}) = \frac{1}{2}.
\]

Finally, 
\[
\begin{split}
    E\left [ X(t_1) X(t_2) \right ] & = P(\text{same interval})E_s[X(t_1) X(t_2)] + P(\text{different interval})E_d[X(t_1) X(t_2)]\\
        & = \frac{1}{2}\left ( 1-\frac{|t_2-t_1|}{T} \right) + \frac{1}{4}\left (\frac{|t_2-t_1|}{T} \right)\\
        & = \frac{1}{4}\left ( 1-\frac{|t_2-t_1|}{T} \right) + \frac{1}{4}
\end{split}
\]

Now, \( \tau = t_2 - t_1\) and
\[ 
E[X(t_1) X(t_2)] = \begin{cases}
\frac{1}{4}\left ( 1-\frac{|\tau|}{T} \right) + \frac{1}{4} & \text{for } |\tau|<T \\
\frac{1}{4} & \text{for } |\tau| \ge T
\end{cases}
\]

This is depicted in Figure \ref{fig:autocorr_binary}.


\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.4]{figures/Binary_Interval.png}
\caption{A random binary interval with \(t_1\) and \(t_2\) falling in different (top) and the same (bottom) bit.}
\label{fig:random_binary_interval}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.5]{figures/Autocorr_binary.png}
\caption{The autocorrelation function of a random binary transmission.}
\label{fig:autocorr_binary}
\end{figure}

Note that:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \( \phi_\mathrm{xx}(0) = E[X(t_i)X(t_i)] = MSV = \sigma_\mathrm{x}^2 + \mu_\mathrm{y}^2 = \frac{1}{2} \)
    \item \( \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\infty) =  \mu_\mathrm{y}^2(t) = \frac{1}{4}\)
\end{itemize}
\bigskip

\begin{tcolorbox}
    \begin{exmp}{Random binary transmission (zero mean).}\\
Suppose we have a stochastic process $X(t)$ representing the binary transmission, as illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:random_binary-zm}. Assume that $X(t)$ can take values -1 or 1 with equal probabilities. That is:
\[P(-1) = P(1) = \frac{1}{2}\]
This is true at any time \(t\). \\

Find the mean and the autocorrelation function of this random process.

\end{exmp}
\end{tcolorbox}



\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.4]{figures/Random_binary_zm.png}
\caption{A random binary transmission (zero mean).}
\label{fig:random_binary-zm}
\end{figure}

Now consider the mean of the process. 
\[
\begin{split}
    E[X(t)] & = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} x f_\mathrm{x}(x,t) \, dx \\
            & = (-1)P(-1) + (1)P(1)\\
            & = 0
\end{split}
\]

The variance of the process is:
\[
\begin{split}
    \sigma_\mathrm{x}^2(t) & = E \left [ (X(t)-\mu_\mathrm{x}(t))^2 \right ]\\
            & = E \left [ X^2(t) \right ] - E\left [ X(t) \right ]^2 \\
            & = (1)^2P(-1) + (1^2)P(1) - (0)^2 \\
            & = 1
\end{split}
\]

Computing the autocorrelation function is a bit more involved. Recall the definition:

\[
    \phi_\mathrm{x_1 X_2}(t_1,t_2) = E\left [ X(t_1) X(t_2) \right ]
\]

We must consider two separate cases:\\

\textbf{Case 1:} \( |t_2-t_1|>T\)\\
Here, \(X(t_1)\) and \(X(t_2)\) are uncorrelated, and so
\[
E\left [ X(t_1) X(t_2) \right ] = E\left [ X(t_1) \right ] E\left [X(t_2)\right ] = 0
\]

\textbf{Case 2:} \( |t_2-t_1|\le T\)\\
Here, \(X(t_1)\) and \(X(t_2)\) are uncorrelated if \(t_1\) and \(t_2\) fall outside of the same bit (as illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:random_binary_interval}, and \emph{correlated} if they fall inside the same bit.\\

Therefore, the expected value is;
\[
\begin{split}
    E\left [ X(t_1) X(t_2) \right ] & = P(\text{same interval})E_s[X(t_1) X(t_2)] + P(\text{different interval})E_d[X(t_1) X(t_2)]\\
        & = \left ( 1-\frac{|t_2-t_1|}{T} \right)E_s[X(t_1) X(t_2)] + \left (\frac{|t_2-t_1|}{T} \right)E_d[X(t_1) X(t_2)]
\end{split}
\]

Here, \(E_s[X(t_1) X(t_2)]\) and \(E_d[X(t_1) X(t_2)]\) denote the expected value in the same bit and different bit, respectively. For a random binary transmission, \(E_d[(X_1 X_2]=0\), as in case 1.\\

For \(X(t_1)\) and \(X(t_2)\) in the same interval, the product \(X_1 X_2\) is always equal to 1.
 
\[ 
E_s[X(t_1) X(t_2)] = 1.
\]

Finally, 
\[
\begin{split}
    E\left [ X(t_1) X(t_2) \right ] & = P(\text{same interval})E_s[X(t_1) X(t_2)] + P(\text{different interval})E_d[X(t_1) X(t_2)]\\
        & = \left( 1-\frac{|t_2-t_1|}{T} \right )
\end{split}
\]

Since \(X(t)\) is stationary, then \( \tau = t_2 - t_1\) and
\[ 
E[X(t_1) X(t_2)] = \begin{cases}
\left ( 1-\frac{|\tau|}{T} \right)  & \text{for } |\tau|<T \\
0 & \text{for } |\tau| \ge T
\end{cases}
\]

This is depicted in Figure \ref{fig:autocorr_binary_zero}.


\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.5]{figures/Autocorr_binary_zero.png}
\caption{The autocorrelation function of a random binary transmission (zero mean).}
\label{fig:autocorr_binary_zero}
\end{figure}

It is trivial to extend the result of the zero mean case to the non-zero mean case since for \(Y = aX + \mu\):

\[
\phi_\mathrm{yy}(\tau) = a^2\phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) + \mu^2
\]

The non-zero mean process has 1/2 the amplitude of the zero mean process, and is offset by 1/2. This yields
\[ 
\phi_\mathrm{yy}(\tau) = \begin{cases}
\frac{1}{4}\left ( 1-\frac{|\tau|}{T} \right) + \frac{1}{4} & \text{for } |\tau|<T \\
\frac{1}{4} & \text{for } |\tau| \ge T
\end{cases}
\]

which is the result of the previous example.\\

\begin{tcolorbox}
    \begin{exmp}{Random telegraph signal (zero mean).}
    
Suppose we have a stochastic process $X(t)$ where at any instant, with equal probability, the value either $+A$ or $-A$, as depicted in Figure \ref{fig:random_telegraph}. \\

The probability that $n$ sign changes occur in a time interval \((t_1, t_2)\) is given by the given Poisson distribution:

\[
P(n, t_1, t_2) = \frac{\lambda (t_2 - t_1)^n}{n!} \exp \left (-\lambda \cdot (t_2 - t_1) \right )
\]

where \(\lambda\) is the average rate of sign changes per unit time.\\

Find the mean and the autocorrelation function of this random process.
    
    \end{exmp}
\end{tcolorbox}




\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.5]{figures/Random_telegraph.png}
\caption{A random telegraph signal.}
\label{fig:random_telegraph}
\end{figure}

Now, let \(X_1 = X(t') \) and \(X_2 = X(t'+\tau)\).

\[ 
E[X(t_1) X(t_2)] = \begin{cases}
A^2 & \text{for } X_1 = X_2 \\
-A^2 & \text{for } X_1 = -X_2
\end{cases}
\]

\[
\begin{split}
    \phi_\mathrm{xx}(t_1,t_2) & = A^2 P(A^2) - A^2 P(-A^2)\\
                    & = A^2 P(X_1=X_2) - A^2P(X_1=-X_2)
\end{split}
\]

If \(n\) sign changes occur between \(t_1\) and \(t_2\), then
\[
P(X_1=X_2) = P(n \text{ even})
\]
\[
P(X_1=-X_2) = P(n \text{ odd})
\]

\[
\begin{split}
P(n \text{ even}) & = P(n = 0, 2, 4, \ldots)\\
    & = \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \frac{(\lambda \tau) ^n}{n!} \exp(\lambda \tau) \quad \text{for \emph{n} even}\\
    & = \exp(-\lambda \tau) \cosh(\lambda \tau)
\end{split}
\]

\[
\begin{split}
P(n \text{ odd}) & = P(n = 1, 3, 5, \ldots)\\
    & = \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \frac{(\lambda \tau) ^n}{n!} \exp(\lambda \tau) \quad \text{for \emph{n} odd}\\
    & = \exp(-\lambda \tau) \sinh{}h(\lambda \tau)
\end{split}
\]

\[
\begin{split}
\phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) & = A^2 \exp(-\lambda \tau) \left \{ \cosh(\lambda \tau) - \sinh(\lambda \tau)\right \} \\
        & =  A^2 \exp(-\lambda \tau)  \exp(-\lambda \tau)\\
        & = A^2 \exp(- 2 \lambda \tau) \quad \text{for } t_2>t_1\\
    & = \exp(-2 \lambda |\tau|) \quad \text{for all } t_2, t_1
\end{split}
\]

This is illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:random_telegraph_AC}.


\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.3]{figures/Random_telegraph_AC.png}
\caption{The autocorrelation function of a random telegraph signal.}
\label{fig:random_telegraph_AC}
\end{figure}


\begin{tcolorbox}
    \begin{exmp}{Sinusiod with random phase offset.}
    
Suppose we have a stochastic process \(X(t) = a \cos(\omega_0 t + \theta)\), where \(a\) and \(\omega_0\) are constants, and \(\theta\) is a random variable uniformly distributed over the interval \([0, 2\pi]\) as depicted in Figure \ref{fig:random_cosine}. \\

Find the mean and autocorrelation function of this random process.

\end{exmp}
\end{tcolorbox}


\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.6]{figures/Random_cosine.png}
\includegraphics[scale=0.4]{figures/theta_dist.png}
\caption{Top: A cosine process with random phase shift. Bottom: Density function of a uniformly distributed phase shift \(\theta\).}
\label{fig:random_cosine}
\end{figure}

\textbf{Mean:}
\[
\begin{split}
\mu_\mathrm{x}(t) = E[X(t)] & = E\left[a\cos(\omega_0 t + \theta)\right] \\
    & = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} a\cos(\omega_0 t + \theta) f_{\theta}(\theta) d\theta \\
    & = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} a\cos(\omega_0 t + \theta) \cdot \frac{1}{2\pi} d\theta \\
    & = \frac{a}{2\pi} \int_0^{2\pi} \cos(\omega_0 t + \theta) d\theta \\
    & = 0
\end{split}
\]

\textbf{Variance:}
\[
\begin{split}
\sigma_\mathrm{x}(t)^2 = E[(X(t)-\mu_\mathrm{x}(t))^2] & = E\left[a\cos(\omega_0 t + \theta)\right] \\
    & = E[(X(t)^2] \\
    & = \frac{a^2}{2}
\end{split}
\]

\textbf{Autocorrelation:}
\[
\begin{split}
\phi_\mathrm{xx}(t) = E[X(t_1)X(t_2)] & = E\left[a\cos(\omega_0 t_1 + \theta) a\cos(\omega_0 t_2 + \theta) \right] \\
    & = E\left[\frac{a^2}{2}\cos(\omega_0 (t_2-t_1) ) \right] + E\left[\frac{a^2}{2}\cos(\omega_0 (t_2+t_1) + 2\theta) \right] \\
    & = \frac{a^2}{2}\cos(\omega_0 (t_2-t_1) ) \\
    & = \frac{a^2}{2}\cos(\omega_0 \tau ) 
\end{split}
\]

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.4]{figures/cosine_AC.png}
\caption{The autocorrelation function of a cosine process with random phase shift.}
\label{fig:cosine_ac}
\end{figure}


\subsection{Cross-Correlation Function}

 The cross-correlation function between two random processes \(X(t) \) and \(Y(t) \) is a measure of similarity between them as a function of the displacement of one relative to the other. It is defined as:

\[ \phi_\mathrm{xy}(t_1, t_2) = E[X(t_1)Y(t_2)] \]

This is illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:cross_corr_ac}.

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.35]{figures/Cross_correlation.png}
\caption{The cross-correlation function of processes \(X(t)\) and \(Y(t)\).}
\label{fig:cross_corr_ac}
\end{figure}

Now, \( X(t) \) and \(Y(t) \) are \emph{uncorrelated} random processes if

\begin{itemize}
    \item \( \phi_\mathrm{xy}(t_1,t_2) = E[X(t_1)]E[Y(t_2)] = \mu_\mathrm{x}(t_1) \mu_\mathrm{y}(t_2)\) \quad \quad \quad for all \(t_1, t_2\)
    \item \( C_\mathrm{xy}(t_1,t_2) = E[X(t_1)]E[Y(t_2)] - \mu_\mathrm{x}(t_1) \mu_\mathrm{y}(t_2)\) = 0 \quad cross-covariance
\end{itemize}

They are \emph{orthogonal} if \( \phi_\mathrm{xy}(t_1,t_2) = 0\).\\

To be \emph{independent} processes:

\[
f_\mathrm{x_1 x_2 \cdots y_1 y_2 \cdots}(x_1, x_2, \ldots, y_1, y_2, \ldots) = f_\mathrm{x_1 x_2 \cdots}(x_1, x_2, \ldots) f_\mathrm{y_1 y_2} \cdots(y_1, y_2, \ldots)
\]\\

In general, all statistics and density functions may depend on time. If so, the process is \emph{non-stationary}.

\section{Stationary Processes}

\textbf{Strict-Sense Stationary (SSS)}: All statistics and density functions must be time-invariant.\\

\textbf{Wide-Sense Stationary (WSS)}: Up to \(2^{nd}\) order statistics (moments) must be time-invariant.

\[
\begin{split}
\mu_\mathrm{x}(t) & \rightarrow \mu_\mathrm{x} \\
\sigma_\mathrm{x}^2(t) & \rightarrow \sigma_\mathrm{x}^2 \\
\phi_\mathrm{xx}(t_1,t_2) & \rightarrow \phi_\mathrm{xx}(t_2-t_1) = \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau)
\end{split}
\]
This is a valid criterion for stationarity in many engineering applications.

\subsection{Ensemble  and Time Averages}
\textbf{Ensemble Averages:}

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Definition:} Ensemble averages involve taking the average of a statistical property (e.g., mean, variance) over an ensemble of multiple realizations (sample functions) of a stochastic process.
    \item \textbf{Calculation:} To calculate ensemble averages, you consider a collection of independent realizations of the stochastic process and average the corresponding values of the property of interest over these realizations.
    \item \textbf{Interpretation:} Ensemble averages provide statistical insights into the behavior of the stochastic process as a whole. They describe how the process behaves on average across different realizations.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Time Averages:}

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Definition:} Time averages involve taking the average of a single realization (sample function) of a stochastic process over a specified time interval.
    \item \textbf{Calculation:} For a given time series, one calculates time averages by sampling values of the process at different time points and then averaging them over the chosen time interval (within which the process must be ergodic).
    \item \textbf{Interpretation:} Time averages provide insights into the behavior of a specific realization of a stochastic process. 
\end{itemize}

An illustration of ensemble and time averages is given in Figure \ref{fig:time_ensemble}. 

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.3]{figures/time_ensemble.png}
\caption{The calculation of an ensemble and time average mean. The ensemble average is computed across all (preferably infinite) sample functions at a specified time. Here, at \(t=t_1\),  \(\mu_\mathrm{x}(t_1) = E[X(t_1)]\). The time average mean computed on the \(3^{rd}\) sample function is  \(M_x = \frac{1}{T} \int_{0}^{T} x_3(t) dt \). Ideally, \(T\rightarrow \infty\) but clearly, this is not practical.}
\label{fig:time_ensemble}
\end{figure}


With unlimited time to compute the time average statistics of \(X(t)\) we define the mean as:
\[
M_\mathrm{x} = \lim_{T \to \infty} \frac{1}{T} \int_{0}^T X(t) dt
\]

The \emph{time-average autocorrelation function} is 

\[
R_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) = \lim_{T \to \infty} \frac{1}{2T} \int_{-T}^{T} X(t)X(t+\tau) dt
\]


\(M_\mathrm{x}\) and \( R_\mathrm{xx}(\tau)\) are RV because the random process assigns a sample function from which they are computed.

In general:

\[
\mu_\mathrm{x} \neq M_\mathrm{x}
\]

\[
\phi_\mathrm{xx}(T) \neq R_\mathrm{xx}(\tau)
\]

for since the ensemble values are constants/vectors for a WSS process, and the time-average values are random variables.


\subsection{Ergodic Stochastic Processes}

If `time averages' are equal to `ensemble averages,' then $X(t)$ is said to be ergodic. Implications of ergodicity are as follows:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item The time average can be determined from \emph{any} individual sample functions (\emph{i.e.}, each sample function must yield the same result, since \( M_\mathrm{x}=\mu_\mathrm{x}, R_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) = \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau)\)).
    \item Ergodicity implies stationarity, since $\mu_\mathrm{x}(t)$, $\sigma_\mathrm{x}(t)$, and $\phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau)$ must be time-invariant.

\end{enumerate}

\begin{tcolorbox}
    
\begin{exmp}{A stationary random process that is not ergodic.}\\

    Consider a stationary random process, denoted as \(X(t)\), which alternates between two constant values, \(A\) and \(-A\) amongst sample functions, with equal probability. Mathematically, it can be represented as:

\[
X(t) = \begin{cases} 
A, & \text{with probability } 0.5 \\
-A, & \text{with probability } 0.5 
\end{cases}
\]
\end{exmp}
\end{tcolorbox}

This process is stationary because its statistical properties (mean, variance, autocorrelation) do not change with time. However, it is not ergodic because the time averages and ensemble averages are not equal.\\

\begin{itemize}
    \item Time Averages: 
    \begin{itemize}
        \item If you observe a single realization of this process (a single sample function), you will see that it takes the value of either \(A\) and \(-A\) for all time.
        \item The time average of this single realization will be either A or -A.
    \end{itemize}
    \item Ensemble Averages:
    \begin{itemize}
        \item When considering the ensemble of all possible realizations, you will have an equal number of realizations where the time average is \(A\) and where it is \(-A\).
        \item So, the ensemble average will be zero.
    \end{itemize}

\end{itemize}


\subsection{Wide-Sense Ergodicity}

Wide-sense ergodicty requires ergodicty of the mean and autocorrelation function. For the process $X(t)$:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item $\mu_\mathrm{x} = M_\mathrm{x}$ (time-average mean)
    \item $\phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) = R_\mathrm{xx}(\tau)$ (time-average autocorrelation).
\end{enumerate}


\textbf{Ergodicity of the mean:}

For infinite averaging time \(T\):
\[
M_\mathrm{x} = \lim_{T \to \infty} \frac{1}{2} \int_{0}^{T} x(t) dt
\]

For finite averaging time \(T\):

\[
M_T =  \frac{1}{T} \int_{0}^{T} x(t) dt
\]

Since \(M_T\) is a random variable, then to be ergodic:
\[
E[M_T] = \frac{1}{T} \int_{0}^{T} E[X(t)] dt = \mu_\mathrm{x}.
\]

The variance of the estimate \(M_T\) decreases as the window size \(T\) increases, as illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:time_avg_mean}.

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.3]{figures/time_avg_mean.png}
\caption{The estimate of the time average mean \(M_T\), extracted for a few sample functions \(x_i(t)\), as the window size \(T\) increases. If the process is ergodic, then \(E[M_T] = \mu_\mathrm{x}  \forall     T\) and \(\text{Var} [M_T] \rightarrow 0 \) as  \(T \rightarrow \infty\).}
\label{fig:time_avg_mean}
\end{figure}

The conditions for ergodicity of the mean are:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \(E[M_T] = \mu_\mathrm{x}\) \quad for all \(T\)
    \item \(\text{Var} [M_T] = E[M_T^2] - E[M_T]^2 \rightarrow 0 \) \quad as  \(T \rightarrow \infty\)
\end{itemize}

\bigbreak

\textbf{Ergodicity of the autocorrelation function:}
\[
R_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) = \lim_{T \to \infty} \frac{1}{2T} \int_{-T}^{T} x(t)x(t+\tau) dt
\]
(a random variable).

Now, define

\[
R_T(\tau) = \frac{1}{2T} \int_{-T}^{T} x(t)x(t+\tau) dt
\]
(also a random variable). \\

Conditions for ergodicty of the autocorrelation function:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \(E[R_T(\tau)] = \frac{1}{2T} \int_{-T}^{T} x(t)x(t+\tau) dt = \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau)\) \quad for all \(T\)
    \item \(\text{Var} [R_T(\tau)] \rightarrow 0 \) \quad as  \(T \rightarrow \infty\)
\end{itemize}

The variability of \(R_T\) as the window size \(T\) increases from \(T_1\) to \(T_2\) is illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:time_avg_mean}.

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.3]{figures/time_avg_ac.png}
\caption{The estimate of \(R_T\) as the window size increases from \(T_1\) to \(T_2\).}
\label{fig:time_avg_ac}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Properties of \(\phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau)\) and \(\phi_\mathrm{xy}(\tau)\) (Stationary Processes)}

\begin{enumerate}
    \item \(\boldsymbol{\phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau)}\) \textbf{ is an even function.}\\
    We know that 
     \[ 
    \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) = E[x(t)x(t+\tau)]
    \]
    
    Consider that 
    \[ 
    \phi_\mathrm{xx}(-\tau) = E[x(t)x(t-\tau)]
    \]
    

   
Now, let \(\alpha = t - \tau\), which yields
 \[ 
 \begin{split}
         \phi_\mathrm{xx}(-\tau) & = E[x(\alpha+\tau)x(\alpha)] \\
         \phi_\mathrm{xx}(-\tau) & = \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau)
\end{split}
\]

    \item \textbf{The value of }\(\phi_\mathrm{xx}(0)\) \textbf{is the mean squared value.}
    \[
    \phi_\mathrm{xx}(0)\ = E[X^2(t)] = MSV
    \]

    \item \textbf{The value of } \(\phi_\mathrm{xx}(0)\) \textbf{is greater than or equal to all other values of} \(\phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau)\).\\
    A typical non-periodic autocorrelation function (\emph{e.g.} the autocorrelation function of a random telegraph signal) is depicted in Figure \ref{fig:random_telegraph_AC} which has its peak at \(\tau=0\).   

\bigskip
\textbf{Proof:}
Consider 
\[
E \left [ \left \{ X(t) \pm X(t + \tau) \right \} ^2 \right ] \ge 0
\]

\[
\phi_\mathrm{xx}(0) +  \phi_\mathrm{xx}(0) \pm 2\phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau)\ge 0
\]

\[
2 \left \{ \phi_\mathrm{xx}(0) \pm \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) \right \} \ge 0
\]

Therefore,

\[
\phi_\mathrm{xx}(0) \ge | \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) \quad \text{ for all } \tau \ge 0.
\]

This is illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:MSV_peak}. A periodic process may have peaks recurring at multiples of the period, as with the cosine process with random phase shift (Figure \ref{fig:cosine_ac}). 


\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.5]{figures/MSV_Peak.png}
\caption{The value of \( \phi_\mathrm{xx}(0) = MSV \) is the peak for a non-periodic process.}
\label{fig:MSV_peak}
\end{figure}

\item \textbf{The value of }\(\phi_\mathrm{xx} (\tau) \rightarrow \mu_\mathrm{x}^2 \) \textbf{ as }  \( \tau \rightarrow \infty\).

\bigskip
\textbf{Proof:}

\[
\begin{split}
    \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) & = E[X(t) X(t+ \tau)]\\
                    & = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}x_1 x_2 f_\mathrm{x_1 x_2}(x_1,x_2) \, dx_1 \, dx_2
\end{split}
\]

If, as \(\tau \rightarrow \infty\), \(X_1\) and \(X_2\) are independent, then 
\[
\begin{split}
    \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) & = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}x_1  f_\mathrm{x_1}(x_1) \, dx_1 \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}x_2 f_\mathrm{x_2}(x_2) \, dx_2 \\
                    & = \mu_\mathrm{x_1}\mu_\mathrm{x_2} \\
                    & = \mu_\mathrm{x}^2
\end{split}
\]

Again, this is illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:MSV_peak}.

\item \textbf{The cross-correlation has reciprocity}.\\

The reciprocity property of cross-correlation (also known as the symmetry property) states that swapping the order of the two signals in a cross-correlation calculation is equivalent to reversing the time-lag (or index) of the resulting correlation function. 

\bigskip
\textbf{Proof:}

\[
\begin{split}
    \phi_\mathrm{xy}(\tau) & = E[X(t)Y(t + \tau)]\\
    \phi_\mathrm{xy}(-\tau) & = E[X(t)Y(t - \tau)] \quad \text{let } \alpha=t-\tau \\
                    & = E[X(\alpha + \tau)Y(\alpha)]\\
                    & = E[Y(\alpha)X(\alpha + \tau)]\\
     \therefore \phi_\mathrm{xy}(-\tau)               & = \phi_\mathrm{yx}(\tau)
\end{split}
\]
\end{enumerate}

\bigskip

\begin{tcolorbox}
    
\begin{exmp}{A sinusoidal process with random phase shift, corrupted by bandlimited white noise.}\\

Consider a stochastic process represented by the equation:
\[Y(t) = X(t) + A \sin(\omega_0 t + \alpha) \]
where:
\begin{itemize}
  \item \(X(t)\) is a noise process.
  \item \(A\) and \(\omega_0\) are constants.
  \item \(\alpha\) is a random variable uniformly distributed over the interval \([0, 2\pi]\) and is independent of \(X(t)\).
\end{itemize}

Find the autocorrelation function $\phi_\mathrm{yy}(\tau)$.
\end{exmp}
\end{tcolorbox}

Now,
\[
\begin{split}    
\phi_\mathrm{yy}(\tau) & = E\left [ \{X(t) + A \sin (\omega_0 t + \alpha)\}\{X(t+\tau) + A \sin (\omega_0 (t + \tau)+ \alpha)\}\ \right ] \\
    & = \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) +  E\left [X(t)A \sin (\omega_0 (t+\tau) + \alpha) \right ] \\
    & \quad  + E\left [X(t+\tau)A \sin (\omega_0 t + \alpha) \right ] \\
    & \quad  + E\left [A^2 \sin (\omega_0 t + \alpha) \sin (\omega_0 (t+\tau) + \alpha)\right ]  \quad  \\
\end{split}
\]

Now using \( \sin A \sin B = 1/2 \{\cos (A-B) - \cos (A+B)\}\),\\

\[
\begin{split}    
\phi_\mathrm{yy}(\tau) & = \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) + \frac{A^2}{2}E\left [ \cos(\omega_0 \tau) - \cos(2 \omega_0 t + \omega_0 \tau + 2 \alpha)\right ] \\
    & = \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) + \frac{A^2}{2} \cos(\omega_0 \tau)
\end{split}
\]

This is illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:Noisy_ACF}. If \(X(t)\) is bandlimited white noise, \(\phi_\mathrm{yy}(\tau)\) becomes equal to that of the periodic signal as the noise becomes uncorrelated with itself as \(\tau \rightarrow \) large. 

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.5]{figures/Noisy_ACF.png}
\caption{The autocorrelation function of a sinusoidal process with random phase shift, corrupted by bandlimited white noise. For the autocorrelation function of bandlimited white noise, please see Section \ref{BLWN}.}
\label{fig:Noisy_ACF}
\end{figure}




\subsection{Autocovariance function}

If \(X(t)\) is a stationary process:

\begin{itemize}
    \item \( \phi_\mathrm{xx}(t) \rightarrow \) autocorrelation function
    \item \( C_\mathrm{xx}(t) \rightarrow \) autocovariance function
\end{itemize}

   \[
\begin{split}
   C_\mathrm{xx}(t) & = E\left[(X(t) - \mu_\mathrm{x})(X(t + \tau) - \mu_\mathrm{x})\right] \\
                & = \phi_\mathrm{xx}(t) - \mu_\mathrm{x} E[X(t) - \mu_\mathrm{x}] E[X(t + \tau)] + \mu_\mathrm{x}^2\\
                & = \phi_\mathrm{xx}(t) - \mu_\mathrm{x}^2
\end{split}
\]

Note:
\[
C_\mathrm{xx}(0) = \sigma_\mathrm{x}^2 = MSV - \mu_\mathrm{x}^2 \quad \text{(variance).}
\]

\section{Power Density Spectrum}

The power density spectrum, or power spectral density (PSD) of a stationary process \(X(t)\) is related to its autocorrelation function \(\phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau)\) by the Fourier transform. The PSD, denoted by \(\Phi_\mathrm{xx}(f)\), is given by:

\[ \Phi_\mathrm{xx}(f) = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) e^{-j2\pi f \tau} d\tau \]

This relationship provides a frequency-domain representation of the energy distribution in the process. The PSD is an important tool in understanding the frequency content of a stochastic process.

\[
\phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) \Leftrightarrow \Phi_\mathrm{xx}(\omega)
\]

\subsubsection{Interpretation of the PSD as a density function}

Consider a random process \(X(t)\) (in volts) driving a resistive circuit, as illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:power_circuit}.
\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.4]{figures/Power_circuit.png}
\caption{Power dissipated in a resistive circuit}
\label{fig:power_circuit}
\end{figure}




The instantaneous power \(P(t)\) dissipated in \(R\) is a random process
\[
P(t) = \frac{X^2(t)}{R} \quad \text{watts}
\]

without loss of generality, let \(R=1 \Omega\). Therefore, \(P(t) = X^2(t) \text{ volts}^2 \).\\

The average power \(\overline{P} \) is also a random variable:
\[
\begin{split}
\overline{P} & = \lim_{T \to \infty} \frac{1}{2T} \int_{-T}^{T} P(t) \, dt\\
                & = \lim_{T \to \infty} \frac{1}{2T} \int_{-T}^{T} X^2(t) \, dt
\end{split}
\]

Now,
\[
E[\overline{P}] = E \left [ \lim_{T \to \infty} \frac{1}{2T} \int_{-T}^{T} X^2(t) \, dt \right ] = MSV = \phi_\mathrm{xx}(0) \quad \text{volts}^2
\]

But we know that
\[
\phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \Phi_\mathrm{xx}(f) e^{-j2\pi f \tau} \, df \quad \text{volts}^2
\]

and therefore
\[
\phi_\mathrm{xx}(0) = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \Phi_\mathrm{xx}(f) \, df \quad \text{volts}^2
\]

Therefore, \(\Phi_\mathrm{xx}(f)\) has units of \(V^2/Hz\) and is a density function in the frequency domain.

\subsection{Properties of \(\Phi_\mathrm{xx}(f)\)}

\begin{enumerate}
    \item Since \(\phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau)\) is an even function, \(\Phi_\mathrm{xx}(f)\) is \emph{real} and \emph{even}.
    \item \(\phi_\mathrm{xx}(0) = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \Phi_\mathrm{xx}(f) \, df \) is \emph{the average power} of \(X(t)\) - the MSV.
    \item \(\Phi_\mathrm{xy}(f)\) is a \emph{complex} function since \(\phi_\mathrm{xy}(\tau)\) is an \emph{odd} function.
    \item Since \(\phi_\mathrm{xy}(\tau) = \phi_\mathrm{yx}(-\tau)\) it follows that \( P_\mathrm{xy}(f) = P_\mathrm{yx}^*(f)\) - the complex conjugate.

\end{enumerate}


\subsection{White noise process.}
Pure white noise is not a realizable process (infinite frequencies!), but it is a useful theoretical reference. It has equal power density at all (infinite) frequencies, and an autocorrelation function equal to a Dirac delta function at \(\tau=0\), as illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:PSD_WN}.  

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.4]{figures/PSD_WN.png}
\caption{The power spectral density and the autocorrelation function of white noise.}
\label{fig:PSD_WN}
\end{figure}



\subsection{Bandlimited white noise process.}\label{BLWN}

Bandlimited white noise contains equal power at all frequencies within a specific frequency range (\(-B\) to \(B\) in the double-sided PSD), but it's limited to that range. Unlike standard white noise, which extends infinitely across all frequencies, bandlimited white noise is confined to a certain bandwidth, as illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:PSD_BLWN}.\\

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.4]{figures/PSD_BLWN.png}
\caption{The power spectral density and the autocorrelation function of bandlimited white noise.}
\label{fig:PSD_BLWN}
\end{figure}


The autocorrelation function is expressed as the \emph{sinc} function:

\[
\phi_\mathrm{xx} (\tau) = 2BK\frac{\sin(2 \pi B \tau)}{2 \pi B \tau }
\]
Note the reciprocal relationship between bandwidth in the frequency domain and the width of the autocorrelation function in the time domain. Reducing bandwidth has the effect of imposed more correlation in the time domain (broadening the autocorrelation function).   





\chapter{Estimation}

\section{Introduction}
In this chapter, we are interested in finding a function of random  variables used to estimate an unknown parameter of an underlying  distribution. Such a function is called a \emph{statistic}, and it generally reduces the number of random variables, called samples, to a lower dimension without loss of information about the parameter. The scenario can be summarized:

\begin{itemize}
    \item Let \(X(t)\) be a random process with fixed probability density function that is parameterized by a scalar \(\theta\). Assume that \(\theta\) is not observable: it cannot be sampled directly and information about \(\theta\) is obtained only through samples of \(X(t)\).
    \item The random process is sampled \(N\) times, yielding \(N\) independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) random variables \(\{X_1, X_2 \ldots X_N\}\). Although the RVs \(\{X_N\}\) contain identical information about the parameter \(\theta\), the specific samples \(\{x_N\}\) generally vary.
    
\end{itemize}

The \(\{X_N\}\) could also be derived from samples of a random sequence \(X[k]\) or they may be just be \(N\) samples of a particular random variable with respect to time. We will use a subscript on \(X_n\) to indicate that it is a sample (random variable) of either the process \(X(t)\), sequence \(X[k]\) or random variable \(X\). The samples need not be collected from the process/sequence with uniform spacing in time; we can simply view \(\{X_N\}\) as a set of i.i.d. random variables with parameter \(\theta\).\\

In order to simplify the equations, we use \(\mathbf{X} \triangleq \{X_1, X_2 \ldots X_N\}\) to represent the \(N\) samples and \(\mathbf{x} \triangleq \{x_1, x_2 \ldots x_N\}\) to represent their specific outcomes.\\

This chapter explores various estimation techniques, ranging from classical frequentist approaches to Bayesian methodologies\footnote{The primary difference between frequentist 
 and Bayesian methods is that the parameter is a considered a fixed quantity versus a random variable, respectively. Refer to \hyperlink{https://towardsdatascience.com/statistics-are-you-bayesian-or-frequentist-4943f953f21b}{this article}{ for theoretical and practical differences between the two approaches.}}, each offering unique insights into parameter estimation. Four key methods that will be examined in detail are Bayesian Estimation, Maximum A Posteriori (MAP) Estimation,  Maximum Likelihood (ML) Estimation, and Linear Minimum Mean Squared Error (LMMSE) Estimation.


\section{Estimator Performance}
To evaluate the efficacy of an estimator, it is important to establish metrics of estimator performance.\\


\subsubsection*{Bias}
    \begin{itemize}
        \item \emph{Definition: }The bias of an estimator \(\hat{\theta}\) with respect to the true parameter \(\theta\) is given by 
        \[
        Bias(\hat{\theta}) = E[\hat{\theta}] - \theta
        \]
        \item \emph{Interpretation: } An estimator is unbiased if \(E[\hat{\theta}] = \theta\), meaning that, on average, the estimator's expected value equals the true parameter value.
    \end{itemize}
   
\subsubsection*{Variance}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \emph{Definition: } The variance of an estimator \(\hat{\theta}\) is given by 
    \[
    \begin{aligned}
        Var(\hat{\theta}) & = E[(\hat{\theta} - E[\hat{\theta}])^2]\\
            & = E[\hat{\theta}^2] - E[\hat{\theta}]^2
    \end{aligned}
    \]
    
   \item \emph{Interpretation: } Variance measures the spread or variability of the estimator around its expected value.
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection*{Mean Squared Error (MSE)}
 \begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Definition: } MSE of an estimator \(\hat{\theta}\) is given by 
    \[
    MSE(\hat{\theta}) = E[(\hat{\theta} - \theta)^2]
    \]
    
   \item \textbf{Interpretation: } MSE combines both bias and variance to quantify the average squared difference between the estimated values and the true parameter value. That is:
   \[
    \begin{aligned}
        MSE(\hat{\theta}) &=  E[(\hat{\theta} - \theta)^2]\\
                            & = E[\hat{\theta}^2  - 2 \hat{\theta} \theta + \theta^2] \\
                            & = E[\theta^2] - 2 E[\hat{\theta}] \theta + E[\hat{\theta}]^2 +  E[\hat{\theta}^2] - E[\hat{\theta}]^2 \\
                            & = Bias^2(\hat{\theta}) + Var(\hat{\theta})
    \end{aligned} 
   \]
   \item An estimator is said to be \emph{consistent} if $\text{MSE} \rightarrow$ as \(T\rightarrow \infty\).
\end{itemize}

 

\subsubsection*{Relationships}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \emph{Consistency and Bias:} Consistent estimators are typically asymptotically unbiased, meaning they become unbiased as the sample size increases.
    \item \emph{Bias and Variance in MSE: } MSE can be decomposed into bias squared and variance. Minimizing MSE involves finding a balnce between reducing bias and variance.
\end{itemize}




\section{Bayesian Estimation}
\label{sec:Bayesian_Estimation}

Bayesian estimation is a statistical framework for estimating parameters of a model using Bayes' theorem. In Bayesian statistics, the parameter of interest (\( \theta\)) is treated as a random variable, and its uncertainty is quantified using probability distributions. The estimation process involves updating prior beliefs about the parameter with observed data \(X\) to obtain a posterior distribution.\\

We may think of \(\theta\) and \(X\) as events, in which case we can use probabilities to describe the elements of Bayes estimation. If \(\theta\) and \(X\) are continuous random variables, we can also express the elements of Bayes' theorem in terms of their probability density functions.\\

Here are the key components of Bayesian estimation:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Prior Distribution (Prior):} This is the initial belief or probability distribution about the parameter before observing any data. The prior represents existing knowledge or beliefs about the parameter and is denoted as \( P(\theta) \) (probability of $\theta$) or \(f_\theta(\theta)\) (probability density function of $\theta$).
    \item \textbf{Likelihood Function (Likelihood):} This function represents the probability of observing the data \(X\) given a specific parameter value $\theta$. It is denoted as \( P(X | \theta) \) or \( f_\mathrm{{x}|\theta}({x})\).
    \item \textbf{Posterior Distribution (Posterior):} The posterior distribution is the updated probability distribution of the parameter after taking into account the observed data. It is calculated using Bayes' theorem:

   \[ P(\theta | X) = \frac{P(X | \theta) \cdot P(\theta)}{P(X)} \]

    Here, \( P(X) \) is the marginal likelihood: the probability of observing the data irrespective of the parameter. When \(\theta\) and \(X\) are continuous random variables it is common to use probability density functions:

       \[ f_\mathrm{\theta | x} (\theta) = \frac{f_\mathrm{x | \theta} (x) \cdot f_\theta (\theta)}{f_\mathrm{x}(x)} \]

The posterior distribution summarizes our updated beliefs about the parameter of interest after incorporating the observed data. It combines our prior beliefs with the new evidence provided by the data, using the likelihood function to assess how well different values of the parameter(s) explain the observed data. As more data is collected, the posterior distribution can be continually updated to refine our estimates of the parameter.

\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Vector Notation of the Data}\\
Recall that, in general, the random process my be sampled \(N\) times, yielding \(N\) independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) random variables such that \(\mathbf{X} \triangleq \{X_1, X_2 \ldots X_N\}\). In this case:
\[
f_\mathrm{x}({x}) = \prod_{n=1}^N f_\mathrm{x_n}(x_n)
\]

and

\[
f_\mathrm{{x}|\theta}({x}) = \prod_{n=1}^N f_\mathrm{x_n|\theta}(x_n)
\]

In the following sections the simpler notation for the data will be \(X\) with the acknowledgement that it can be used interchangeably with the vector \(\mathbf{X}\).\\

\textbf{Posterior Estimation}\\ The parameter estimate in Bayesian statistics is often summarized using the posterior distribution. The point estimate can be the mean, median, or mode of the posterior distribution, and credible intervals (analogous to confidence intervals in frequentist statistics) can be derived to express the uncertainty around the estimate.\\

\textbf{Advantages of Bayesian Estimation}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \emph{Incorporation of Prior Information:} Bayes' theorem allows the incorporation of prior knowledge or beliefs into the estimation process.
    \item \emph{Quantification of Uncertainty:} Provides a full probability distribution for the parameter, allowing for a more comprehensive understanding of uncertainty.
    \item \emph{Flexibility:} Can be applied to a wide range of problems, especially when dealing with complex models or small sample sizes.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Challenges of Bayesian Estimation}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \emph{Subjectivity of Priors:} The choice of prior can impact the results, and different analysts may have different priors.
    \item \emph{Computational Complexity:} In some cases, obtaining the exact posterior distribution may be computationally challenging, leading to the use of approximation methods like Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC).
\end{itemize}

\bigskip

\begin{tcolorbox}
    \begin{exmp}{Bayesian estimation.}

    Consider using a Bayesian estimator to estimate the mean $\mu$ of a Gaussian process \(X \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma)\). Assume  the standard deviation $\sigma$ of the distribution is known.
    \end{exmp}
\end{tcolorbox}

Using a Bayesian approach to estimate the mean \(\mu\) of a Gaussian process \(X \sim N(\mu, \sigma^2)\) involves updating our beliefs about \(\mu\) based on observed data. We'll start with a prior distribution for \(\mu\), then update it using Bayes' theorem after observing data points from the process.\\

Let's denote:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \(X = \{x_1, x_2, ..., x_n\}\) as the observed data points.
    \item \(N\) as the total number of observations.
    \item \(\sigma^2\) as the known variance of the process.
\end{itemize}

After observing data \(X\), the posterior distribution for \(\mu\) can be calculated using Bayes' theorem:

\[ f(\mu | X) = \frac{f(X | \mu) \cdot f(\mu)}{f(X)} \]

Where:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \( f(\mu | X) \) is the posterior distribution for \(\mu\) given the observed data \(X\)
    \item \( f(X | \mu) \) is the likelihood function, representing the likelihood of observing data \(X\) given \(\mu\), which is given by the product of \(N\) Gaussian probability density functions
    \item \( f(\mu) \) is the prior distribution for \(\mu\), assumed to be normal
    \item \( f(X) \) is the marginal likelihood or evidence, which can be calculated by integrating the likelihood function over all possible values of \(\mu\).
\end{itemize}


Once we have the posterior distribution \( f(\mu | X) \), we can summarize it by calculating its mean, median, mode, or credible intervals, which provide a range of plausible values for \(\mu\) given the observed data. \\


First, let's define our prior knowledge about $\mu$. We'll assume that $\mu$ itself follows a Gaussian distribution with known mean $\mu_0$ and standard deviation $\sigma_0$. This is our prior distribution \(f(\mu) = \mathcal{N}\ (\mu_0, \sigma_0)\).\\

After observing some data $X = \{x_1, x_2, ..., x_n\}$, we can calculate the likelihood function $\mu$, representing the likelihood of observing data $X$ given $f(\mu)$. Recall that this is given by the product of $n$ Gaussian probability density functions with known variance $\sigma$. This is computed as the sample estimates of mean and variance from the Gaussian process \(X\) with:
\[
\begin{aligned}
 \hat{\mu} & = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n x_i\\
 \hat{\sigma} & =  \frac{1}{n}\sigma
 \end{aligned}
\]

We can then apply Bayes' theorem to calculate the posterior distribution $P(\mu|X)$, which is proportional to the product of the likelihood and the prior:

$$ f(\mu|X) \propto f(X|\mu)f(\mu) $$

The posterior distribution is also Gaussian, since it is the product of two Gaussian distributions. 

We multiply the Prior kernel by the Likelihood kernel:

$$f(\mu | x) \propto \underbrace{\exp\left( -\frac{(\mu - \mu_0)^2}{2\sigma_0^2} \right)}_{\text{Prior}} \times \underbrace{\exp\left( -\frac{(x - \mu)^2}{2\sigma^2} \right)}_{\text{Likelihood}}$$

Since we are multiplying exponentials, we add the terms inside the brackets:$$f(\mu | x) \propto \exp\left( -\frac{1}{2} \left[ \frac{(\mu - \mu_0)^2}{\sigma_0^2} + \frac{(x - \mu)^2}{\sigma^2} \right] \right)$$


Before we evaluate the mean and variance of the  the product of two exponential functions, let's define \emph{precision} as the inverse of the variance, so that
\[
p = \frac{1}{\sigma^2}
\]

The precision of the posterior distribution is then
\[
\begin{aligned}
    p_{\text{posterior}} & = \frac{1} {\sigma^2_{\text{prior}}} + \frac{1}{\hat{\sigma}} \\
        & = \frac{1} {\sigma^2_0} + \frac{n}{\sigma^2}
\end{aligned}
\]
And,

\[
\sigma^2_{p} = \frac{1}{p_{p}}
\]

The mean of the posterior distribution. After completing the square and normalizing, the new mean $\mu_p$ emerges as a weighted sum:

$$\mu_p = \frac{\text{Prior Precision} \cdot \mu_0 + \text{Data Precision} \cdot \hat{\mu}}{\text{Total Precision}}$$Mathematically, this looks like:
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mu_{p} & = \frac{\frac{\mu_0}{\sigma_0^2} + \frac{\hat{\mu}}{\sigma^2}}{\frac{1}{\sigma_0^2} + \frac{n}{\sigma^2}}
 \\
\end{aligned}
\]

This can be simplified to give
\[ 
\mu_{p} = \frac{\sigma^2\mu_0 + n\sigma_0^2\hat{\mu}}{\sigma^2 + n\sigma_0^2}
\]


The Bayesian estimate is a weighted average of the prior mean $\mu_0$ and the sample mean $\hat{\mu}$, with more weight given to whichever has smaller variance. This reflects the trade-off between prior knowledge and observed data in Bayesian estimation. If we have a lot of data (large $n$), the sample mean will be more influential. If we have strong prior knowledge (small $\sigma_0$), the prior mean will be more influential. \\

This example is evaluated in numerical form in the code \href{https://www.ece.unb.ca/kengleha/Textbook/Code/Bayes_estimate_mu.py}{\texttt{Bayes\_estimate\_mu.py}}. Figure \ref{fig:Bayes_estimate_mu} uses this code to evaluate the posterior density and compute the resulting mean. Here, the prior mean is $\mu_0=0$, and the example uses a prior standard deviation that is large ($\sigma_0=2.0$) and small $\sigma_0=0.5$.  \\

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.5]{figures/Bayes_estimate_mu_large_sigma.png}
\includegraphics[scale=0.5]{figures/Bayes_estimate_mu_small_sigma.png}
\caption{Bayesian estimate of the mean of a process. After an update using four new data samples,  the resulting updated posterior density is more affected by the new data for the large prior $\sigma_0=2.0$ than for $\sigma_0=0.5$.}
\label{fig:Bayes_estimate_mu}
\end{figure}

\begin{tcolorbox}
\begin{exmp}{Bayesian estimation of mean response time of a new drug.}

      Let's consider an example involving the estimation of the mean response time of a new drug in a clinical trial. Suppose a new drug is designed to reduce the response time of patients to a specific stimulus. The response times are assumed to follow a normal distribution, and we want to estimate the mean response time (\(\mu\)) of the population. We can use Bayesian estimation to update our belief about \(\mu\) based on observed data. In this case, the response time is updated from a an observed average response time, after adding \(n\) new patients to a study.
      \end{exmp}
\end{tcolorbox}

     

\begin{enumerate}
    \item Prior Distribution:
   We start with a prior distribution representing our initial belief about the mean response time. Let's assume a normal distribution with a mean \(\mu_0\) and a relatively wide standard deviation (\(\sigma_0\)) to reflect our uncertainty before any data is collected.

   \[ \text{Prior: } f(\mu) \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_0, \sigma_0) \]

    \item Likelihood Function:
   After conducting a clinical trial, we collect response time data from patients. We model the likelihood of the observed data given the parameter \(\mu\), assuming that the response times are normally distributed.

   \[ \text{Likelihood: } f(\text{Data} | \mu) \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu, \sigma) \]

    \item Posterior Distribution:
   Using Bayes' theorem, we update our belief about \(\mu\) based on the prior and likelihood. The posterior distribution reflects our updated knowledge after considering the observed data.

   \[ \text{Posterior: } f(\mu | \text{Data}) \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_p, \sigma_p) \]

   The posterior parameters (\(\mu_p, \sigma_p\)) are calculated based on the prior parameters (\(\mu_0, \sigma_0^2\)) and the observed data.

    \item Bayesian Estimation:
   We can calculate the posterior mean (\(\mu_p\)) as our Bayesian estimate of the mean response time. The posterior standard deviation (\(\sigma_p\)) provides information about the uncertainty of our estimate.

\end{enumerate}

This example is evaluated in numerical form in the code \href{https://www.ece.unb.ca/kengleha/Textbook/Code/Bayes_clinical_trial.py}{\texttt{Bayes\_clinical\_trial.py}}. Before adding new patients to the study, the response time is \( \sim \mathcal{N}(50,20) \).  Thirty new patients enter the study, drawn from a \( \sim \mathcal{N}(45,15) \) likelihood distribution. Figure \ref{fig:Bayes_clinical_trial} shows the prior density, the new data and likelihood distribution, and the resulting updated posterior density. It is clear that the updated response time is less than the prior.

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.4]{figures/Bayes_clinical_trial.png}
\caption{Bayesian estimate of the response time of a new drug, before and after adding 30 new patients to the study.}
\label{fig:Bayes_clinical_trial}
\end{figure}


\subsection{Maximum a Posteriori Estimation }
    

Maximum a Posteriori (MAP) estimation is a Bayesian point estimate of the most probable value of a parameter (the mode of the probability density function) given observed data and prior knowledge. 


Recall that Bayes' theorem relates the posterior \(P(\theta | X)\), likelihood \(P(X | \theta)\), prior \(P(\theta)\), and evidence \(P(X)\):

\[ P(\theta | X) = \frac{P(X | \theta) \cdot P(\theta)}{P(X)} \]

Generally, it is assumed that the  likelihood \(P(X | \theta)\) and the prior \(P(\theta)\) are known or can be estimated. The estimate of the parameter \(\theta\) does not depend directly on the evidence \(P(X)\); consequently it does not affect the maximum and can be ignored. The MAP estimator for \(\theta\) is the value maximizing the posterior pdf \footnote{Note that if the prior is uniform or does not significantly affect the result, the MAP estimate becomes equivalent to Maximum Likelihood Estimation \( \theta_{\text{MLE}} = \arg \max_\theta P(X | \theta) \). This is discussed in the next section.}:
 
\[ \theta_{\text{MAP}} = \arg \max_\theta \left[ P(X | \theta) \cdot P(\theta) \right] \]



Often, to simplify the expression, one can take the logarithm of both sides:

\[ \log P(\theta | X) = \log P(X | \theta) + \log P(\theta) - \log P(X) \]


Maximizing the posterior probability is equivalent to finding the maximum of the logarithm of the posterior:

\[ \theta_{\text{MAP}} = \arg \max_\theta \left[ \log P(X | \theta) + \log P(\theta) \right] \]

The MAP estimation provides a principled way to incorporate prior knowledge into the parameter estimation process, making it particularly useful when dealing with limited data.

\begin{tcolorbox}
    
\begin{exmp}{Example with Gaussian Likelihood and Prior.}

For a Gaussian likelihood \(P(X | \theta) = \mathcal{N}(\theta, \sigma)\) and a Gaussian prior \(P(\theta) = \mathcal{N}(\mu_0, \sigma_0)\), find the MAP estimate of $\theta$.

\end{exmp}
\end{tcolorbox}

The map estimate of $\theta$ is given by:
\[ \log P(\theta | X) = \log P(X | \theta) + \log P(\theta)\]

where \( X = \{x_1, x_2, \ldots, x_n\} \) is a vector of data points. Let's consider first the probability density functions: 

   \[ f(X | \theta) = \prod_{i=1}^{n} \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^2}} \exp\left(-\frac{(x_i - \theta)^2}{2\sigma^2}\right) \]


   \[ f(\theta) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma_0^2}} \exp\left(-\frac{(\theta - \mu_0)^2}{2\sigma_0^2}\right)\]


The joint posterior distribution is given by:

\[ f(\theta | X) \propto f(X | \theta) \cdot f(\theta) \]

Substituting the expressions for the joint likelihood and the joint prior, we have:

\[ f(\theta | X) \propto \prod_{i=1}^{n} \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^2}} \exp\left(-\frac{(x_i - \theta)^2}{2\sigma^2}\right) \cdot \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma_0^2}} \exp\left(-\frac{(\theta - \mu_0)^2}{2\sigma_0^2}\right) \]

Taking the logarithm of this expression makes it easier to work with:

\[ \ln f(\theta | X) \propto \sum_{i=1}^{n} \left( -\frac{(x_i - \theta)^2}{2\sigma^2} \right) - \frac{(\theta - \mu_0)^2}{2\sigma_0^2} + C \]

Where \( C \) is a constant term.\\

To find the MAP estimate of \( \theta \), we need to maximize \( \log f(\theta | X) \) with respect to \( \theta \). We can do this by taking the derivative of \( \log f(\theta | X) \) with respect to \( \theta \), setting it to zero, and solving for \( \theta \).

\[ \frac{d}{d\theta} \ln f(\theta | X) = \sum_{i=1}^{n} \frac{x_i - \theta}{\sigma^2} - \frac{\theta - \mu_0}{\sigma_0^2} = 0 \]

Solving this equation for \( \theta \), we get the MAP estimate:

\[ \hat{\theta}_\text{MAP} = \frac{\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n} x_i}{\sigma^2} + \frac{\mu_0}{\sigma_0^2}}{\frac{n}{\sigma^2} + \frac{1}{\sigma_0^2}} \]

This is illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:MAP1_example}.


\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.6]{figures/MAP1_example.png}
\caption{The prior distribution (green), posterior distribution (red), and a histogram of the observed data (orange). The Maximum A Posteriori (MAP) estimate of the parameter $\theta$
 is indicated by the dashed gray line. The data histogram represents the observed dataset $X$.}
\label{fig:MAP1_example}
\end{figure}

This expression provides the MAP estimate of \( \theta \) given the dataset \( X \), the known standard deviation \( \sigma \) of the likelihood, the prior mean \( \mu_0 \), and the prior standard deviation \( \sigma_0 \).

\bigskip

\newpage
\begin{tcolorbox}
    \begin{exmp}{Bit Error Rate in a Communications System.}

    Let's consider a communication system where we want to estimate the probability of bit errors (\(p\)) in a digital communication channel. 
    \end{exmp}
\end{tcolorbox}

We have some prior information about the bit error probability $p$, which we model as a Beta distribution with parameters \(\alpha\) and \(\beta\). \\

\[ P(p) = \frac{p^{\alpha - 1} (1-p)^{\beta - 1}}{B(\alpha, \beta)} \]
where \( B(\alpha, \beta) \) is the Beta function, which serves as a normalization constant to ensure that the total probability integrates to 1.\\


Now, we conduct a series of experiments to measure the bit error rate in the communication channel. Let \(k\) be the number of observed errors and \(n\) be the total number of bits transmitted in the experiments. The likelihood function for \(p\) given the observed data is given by the Binomial distribution:

\[ P(\text{data} | p) = \text{Binomial}(k | n, p) \]

Now, our goal is to find the Maximum A Posteriori (MAP) estimate for the bit error probability (\(p\)), given the observed data and the prior information. According to Bayes' Theorem, the posterior distribution is proportional to the product of the likelihood and the prior:

\[ P(p | \text{data}) \propto P(\text{data} | p) \cdot P(p) \]

For a Beta prior and a Binomial likelihood, the posterior distribution is also a Beta distribution. This posterior distribution is given by:

\[ P(p | \text{data}) = \text{Beta}(\alpha + k, \beta + n - k) \]

The likelihood function for \( p \) given the observed data (number of observed errors \( k \) and total number of bits transmitted \( n \)) is given by the Binomial distribution:

\[ P(\text{data} | p) = \binom{n}{k} p^k (1-p)^{n-k} \]


The posterior distribution is the product of the likelihood function and the prior distribution (ignoring the terms that simply scale the magnitude in the expression of proportionality):
\[ P(p | \text{data}) \propto p^k (1-p)^{n-k} \cdot p^{\alpha - 1} (1-p)^{\beta - 1} \]

This simplifies to:
\[ P(p | \text{data}) \propto p^{k + \alpha - 1} (1-p)^{n - k + \beta - 1} \]
The mode of this distribution, which is the MAP estimate, is found by taking the derivative of the log of the posterior with respect to \( p \), setting it to zero, and solving for \( p \). This yields:
\[ \hat{p}_\text{MAP} = \frac{k + \alpha - 1}{n + \alpha + \beta - 2} \]




Here, \(\hat{p}_{\text{MAP}}\) represents the MAP estimate of the bit error probability based on the observed data and the prior information.\\

A numerical example is provided in \href{https://www.ece.unb.ca/kengleha/Textbook/Code/MAP_example.py}{\texttt{MAP\_example.py}}, and the results shown in Figure \ref{fig:MAP_example}.

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.5]{figures/MAP_example.png}
\caption{MAP example: The prior and posterior distributions of the bit error probability \(p\) in a digital communication channel are shown. The data were simulated using a binomial distribution with a true bit error probability of \(p=0.2\). The prior distribution, modeled as a Beta distribution with parameters \(\alpha = 10000\) and \(\beta = 41000\), and the posterior distribution, obtained using a hybrid Beta-Binomial model, are shown. The vertical dashed line indicates the true bit error probability, while the green dashed line represents the MAP estimate of \(p\).}
\label{fig:MAP_example}
\end{figure}


\subsubsection*{Comparison between Bayesian and MAP Estimation}
Bayesian estimation and MAP estimation are both methods used in statistics for parameter estimation, and they both incorporate prior knowledge about the parameters. However, they differ in how they use this information:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item \emph{Estimate: } The MAP estimate is the mode (peak) of the posterior distribution, which is the value of the parameter that maximizes the posterior probability. On the other hand, the Bayesian estimate is typically the expected value (mean) of the posterior distribution, which takes into account all possible values of the parameter, weighted by their posterior probabilities.

    \item \emph{Uncertainty: } Bayesian estimation provides a full posterior distribution for the parameter, which gives a sense of the uncertainty or variability in the estimate. It tells us how plausible each parameter value is given the data. In contrast, MAP provides a single value without a direct measure of uncertainty.

    \item \emph{Influence of Prior: } In MAP estimation, the prior can have a strong influence on the estimate when there's little data, but its influence diminishes with more data. In Bayesian estimation, the prior always influences the estimate to some extent because it affects the shape of the entire posterior distribution.
\end{enumerate}


In summary, if you're interested in a point estimate and have a good idea of what the parameter should be, MAP might be a good choice. If you want to quantify uncertainty and incorporate prior information in a more nuanced way, Bayesian estimation would be more appropriate.

\section{Maximum Likelihood Estimation}
Consider estimating parameter \(\theta\) where its prior distribution \(f_\theta (\theta)\) is not known, and consequently cannot be used in MAP estimation. In this situation, we can use the MLE estimator which does not require information about the prior distribution. MLE can also be viewed as the MAP point estimator in which the a prior density is assumed uniform over parameter range.\\



The likelihood function, denoted as \( \mathcal{L}( x | \theta ) \), represents the probability of observing the given data \( x \) for different values of the model parameters \( \theta \).

\[
\mathcal{L}(x| \theta) = f_\mathrm{x|\theta}(x)
\]

For \(N\) i.i.d data samples \(\mathbf{X} = \{X_1, X_2, \ldots X_n \} \) this is 
\[
\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{x} | \theta ) = \prod_{n=1}^N f_\mathrm{x_n|\theta}(x_n)
\]

Due to mathematical convenience, the log-likelihood function (log-likelihood) is often used instead of the likelihood function.
 \[
 \ell(\mathbf{x} | \theta ) =  \sum_{n=1}^N \ln \left (f_\mathrm{x_n|\theta}(x_n) \right )\]

MLE seeks to find the parameter values that maximize the likelihood (or log-likelihood) function. Mathematically, it involves solving the optimization problem: 

\[ \hat{\theta}_{\text{MLE}} = \arg\max_{\theta} \ell(\theta | \mathbf{x}) 
\]

\textbf{Steps in MLE}\\

\begin{enumerate}
    \item \emph{Formulate Likelihood Function:} Write down the likelihood function based on the assumed statistical model and the observed data.
    \item \emph{Take Logarithm:} Simplify the likelihood function by taking the natural logarithm, yielding the log-likelihood.
    \item \emph{Differentiate and Set to Zero: } Differentiate the log-likelihood with respect to each parameter and set the derivatives equal to zero to find the critical points.
    \item \emph{Solve for Parameters:} Solve the system of equations to find the parameter values that maximize the log-likelihood.

\end{enumerate}

\begin{tcolorbox}
    \begin{exmp}{ML Estimation of the Mean of a Normal Distribution.}

    Let's consider a simple analytical example to illustrate MLE: estimating the mean of a normal distribution.

\end{exmp}
\end{tcolorbox}



Suppose you have a random sample \(X_1, X_2, \ldots, X_n\) from a normal distribution with an unknown mean \(\mu\) and a known standard deviation \(\sigma\). The likelihood function for this scenario is given by the product of the probability density functions of the normal distribution:

\[ \mathcal{L}( x_1, x_2, \ldots, x_n | \mu ) = \prod_{i=1}^{n} \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma} e^{-\frac{(x_i - \mu)^2}{2\sigma^2}} \]

The log-likelihood function is:

\[ \ell( x_1, x_2, \ldots, x_n | \mu) = -\frac{n}{2} \log(2\pi\sigma^2) - \frac{1}{2\sigma^2} \sum_{i=1}^{n} (x_i - \mu)^2 \]

To find the maximum likelihood estimate \(\hat{\mu}_{\text{MLE}}\), we differentiate the log-likelihood with respect to \(\mu\) and set the result equal to zero:

\[ \frac{d\ell}{d\mu} = \frac{1}{\sigma^2} \sum_{i=1}^{n} (x_i - \hat{\mu}_{\text{MLE}}) = 0 \]

Solving this equation gives the MLE for \(\mu\):

\[ \hat{\mu}_{\text{MLE}} = \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^{n} x_i \]

This is the sample mean, which is a well-known result for the MLE of the mean in a normal distribution.\\

\begin{tcolorbox}
    \begin{exmp}{ML Estimation of Regression Parameters.}
    
    Let's consider a more complex example of MLE: estimating the parameters of a simple linear regression model.
    \end{exmp}
\end{tcolorbox}


Suppose we have a dataset \((x_1, y_1), (x_2, y_2), \ldots, (x_n, y_n)\) and assume that the relationship between \(x\) and \(y\) follows a simple linear regression model:

\[ y_i = \beta_0 + \beta_1 x_i + \epsilon_i \]

where \(\epsilon_i\) is a random error term. We assume that \(\epsilon_i \sim N(0, \sigma^2)\) with constant variance.

The likelihood function for the observed data is given by the product of the normal distributions:

\[ \mathcal{L}(x_1, y_1, \ldots, x_n, y_n | \beta_0, \beta_1, \sigma ) = \prod_{i=1}^{n} \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma^2}} e^{-\frac{(y_i - (\beta_0 + \beta_1 x_i))^2}{2\sigma^2}} \]

The log-likelihood function is:

\[ \ell(x_1, y_1, \ldots, x_n, y_n | \beta_0, \beta_1, \sigma) = -\frac{n}{2} \log(2\pi\sigma^2) - \frac{1}{2\sigma^2} \sum_{i=1}^{n} (y_i - (\beta_0 + \beta_1 x_i))^2 \]

To find the maximum likelihood estimates \(\hat{\beta}_0\), \(\hat{\beta}_1\), and \(\hat{\sigma}\), we might consider differentiating the log-likelihood with respect to the parameters and set the results equal to zero. The solutions involve solving a system of equations. In practice, this is very difficult if not impossible, and so numerical optimization methods are often used.\\

 
In routine \href{https://www.ece.unb.ca/kengleha/Textbook/Code/MLE_example.py}{\texttt{MLE\_example.py}} we generate synthetic data from a linear regression model, define the log-likelihood function, and use the `minimize` function from SciPy to find the maximum likelihood estimates. Figure  \ref{fig:MLE_example} shows the true regression line, observed data, and the MLE regression line.

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.4]{figures/MLE_regression.png}
\caption{The MLE estimate of the regression parameters \(\hat{\beta}_0\), \(\hat{\beta}_1\), and \(\hat{\sigma}\).}
\label{fig:MLE_example}
\end{figure}


\section{Minimum Mean Square Estimation}
Given a random variable \(X\), we wish to estimate the RV \(Y\) from \(X\) is the MMSE sense. Define
\[
\hat{Y} = g(X)
\]
where \(\hat{Y}\) is the MMSE estimate of \(Y\). In order to minimize the mean square error:

\[
\begin{aligned}
MSE &= E[ (Y - \hat{Y})^2]\\
&= \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} (Y-g(X))^2 f_\mathrm{xy}
(x,y) \, \, dx \, dy \\
&= \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \underbrace{\left [ \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} (Y-g(X))^2 f_\mathrm{y}(y|x) \, dy \right ]}_{\text{minimize this quantity}} f_\mathrm{x}(x) \, dx 
\end{aligned}
\]
Denote

\[
G = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} (Y-g(x))^2 f_\mathrm{y}(y|x) \, dy
\]

Minimize with respect to \(\hat{Y}=g(X)\) using the chain rule. Note that \(\frac{d(y-\hat{Y})^2}{d \hat{Y}} = -2(y-\hat{Y})\).

\[
\frac{dG}{d\hat{Y}} = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} -2y f_\mathrm{y}(y|x) \, dy + \hat{Y}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}2 f_\mathrm{y}(y|x) \, dy = 0
\]

so that,
\[
2 \hat{Y} - 2 \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} y f_\mathrm{y}(y|x) \, dy = 0
\]

And finally,
\[
\boxed{\hat{Y} =  \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} y f_\mathrm{y}(y|x) \, dy = E[Y|X]}
\]

This is the minimum mean square solution, the conditional mean of the random variable  \(Y\) conditioned upon \(X\).

\subsection{Linear MMSE Estimation}

Let's derive the MMSE solution for the linear estimator \(\hat{Y} = aX + b\).\\

Given the Mean Squared Error (MSE) as:

\begin{align*}
\text{MSE} &= E[(Y - (\hat{Y}))^2] \\
&= E[Y^2 - 2aXY - 2bY + (\hat{Y})^2] \\
&= E[Y^2] - 2aE[XY] - 2bE[Y] + E[a^2X^2 + 2abX + b^2] \\
&= E[Y^2] - 2aE[XY] - 2bE[Y] + a^2E[X^2] + 2abE[X] + b^2 \\
\end{align*}

Now, take the partial derivatives with respect to \(a\) and \(b\) and set them equal to zero:


\begin{align*}
\frac{\partial \text{MSE}}{\partial a} &= -2E[XY] + 2aE[X^2] + 2bE[X] = 0 \\
\frac{\partial \text{MSE}}{\partial b} &= -2E[Y] + 2aE[X] + 2b = 0
\end{align*}

Solve these two equations simultaneously for \(a\) and \(b\). The solutions are:

\begin{align*}
a &= \frac{\text{Cov}(X, Y)}{\text{Var}(X)} \\
b &= E[Y] - aE[X]
\end{align*}

These are the values for \(a\) and \(b\), providing the MMSE estimator for \(Y\):

\[ \hat{Y}_{\text{MMSE}} = aX + b \]


\textbf{Remark:} If $X$ and $Y$ are uncorrelated, i.e., $\text{Cov}(X, Y) = 0$, then setting $a = 0$ solves this equation. In this case, we have $Y = g(x) = b$. The implication is that the guess for $Y$ does not include any information from $X$. Therefore, linear estimation cannot extract any information about $Y$ that can minimize the Mean Squared Error $MSE = E[(Y - b)^2]$.\\


\begin{tcolorbox}
    \begin{exmp}{Signal + Random Noise with MMSE}
    
Consider an observed signal $X(t)$ that consists of a `signal' $Z(t)$ which is a a zero-mean random process with variance $\sigma_z^2$, and additive zero-mean noise $W(t)$ with variance $\sigma_w^2$.

\[ X(t) = Z(t) + W(t) \]

We wish to find the linear MMSE solution \(Z_\text{MMSE}\) from the noisy signal \(X\) using the linear estimator

\[
\hat{Z} = aX + b
\]
    
    \end{exmp}
\end{tcolorbox}

We calculate $a$ using the formula:
\[ a = \frac{\text{Cov}(Z, X)}{\text{Var}(X)} \]

\[
\begin{aligned}
\text{Cov}(Z, X) & = E[(Z-\mu_z)(X-\mu_\mathrm{x})]\\
                & = E[ZX]\\
                & = E[Z(Z+W)]\\
                & = E[ZZ] + E[ZW]
\end{aligned}
\]

If we assume that the signal and the noise are uncorrelated, then

\[
\text{Cov}(Z, X) = \sigma_z^2
\]

Given that $\text{Var}(X) = \sigma_z^2 + \sigma_w^2$, this becomes
\[ a = \frac{\sigma_z^2}{\sigma_z^2 + \sigma_w^2} \]

Since all processes have zero mean, \(b=0\).\\

The linear MMSE estimate of the signal $Z$ is :
\[Z = \frac{\sigma_z^2}{\sigma_z^2 + \sigma_w^2} X(t) \]


The following Matlab code illustrates this example:

\begin{lstlisting}[language=Matlab, caption={MATLAB Code Example}]
% Parameters
sigma_z = 1;  % Variance of Z
sigma_w = 0.5;  % Variance of W
sigma_\mathrm{x} = 0.8;  % Variance of X

% Generate a time vector
t = 0:0.1:10;

% Generate the random process Z
Z = randn(size(t)) * sqrt(sigma_z);

% Generate white Gaussian noise W
W = randn(size(t)) * sqrt(sigma_w);

% Form the observed signal X
X = Z + W;

% Calculate the optimal coefficient A
A = sigma_z^2 / (sigma_z^2 + sigma_w^2);

% Compute the MMSE estimate of Z
Z_hat = A * X;

% Plot the signals
figure;
plot(t, Z, 'b', 'LineWidth', 2, 'DisplayName', 'Z(t)');
hold on;
plot(t, X, 'r', 'LineWidth', 1.5, 'DisplayName', 'X(t)');
plot(t, Z_hat, 'g--', 'LineWidth', 1.5, 'DisplayName', 'Estimated Z(t)');
legend();
xlabel('Time');
ylabel('Amplitude');
title('MMSE Estimation Example');
grid on;
\end{lstlisting}

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.4]{figures/MMSE_example.png}
\caption{The MMSE estimate of \(Z\) given noisy \(X\).}
\label{fig:MMSE_example}
\end{figure}



\subsection{Orthogonality Principle}

The orthogonality principle in LMMSE states that the error is orthogonal to the data:
\[
E[(Y-\hat{Y})X] = 0
\]

\bigskip

For example, for \(\hat{Y}=aX + b\) and without loss of generality, let \(\mu_\mathrm{x} = 0\) so that \(b = 0\).

\[
\begin{aligned}
    E[(Y-aX)X] & = E[YX] - E[aX^2]\\
                & = E[YX] -a E[X^2]
\end{aligned}
\]

We know that the LMMSE solution is:

\[
\begin{aligned}
a &= \frac{\text{Cov}(X, Y)}{\text{Var}(X)} \\
    & = \frac{E[XY]}{\sigma_\mathrm{x}^2}
\end{aligned}
\]

Therefore,
\[
E[(Y-aX)X] = E[YX] - \frac{E[XY]}{\sigma_\mathrm{x}^2}(\sigma_\mathrm{x}^2 + \mu_\mathrm{x}^2)
\]
since \(\mu_\mathrm{x}=0\) this becomes
\[
\begin{aligned}
    E[(Y-aX)X] & = E[YX] - \frac{E[XY]}{\sigma_\mathrm{x}^2}\sigma_\mathrm{x}^2 \\
                & = 0
\end{aligned}
\]

Therefore, the error is orthogonal to the data in the LMMSE.\\

The orthogonality of the error to the data is an extremely important result in estimation. In general, given an LMMSE estimate \( Y \) in terms of data \( \bar{X} \):

\[
Y = a_1 X_1 + a_2 X_2 + \ldots + a_n X_n
\]

Then, from the orthogonality principle:

\[
{E}[(Y - \hat{Y}) X_i] = 0 \quad \text{for all } i
\]



\begin{tcolorbox}
\begin{exmp}{Multivariate LMMSE}
Suppose we have a system where the observed data vector is \(X = [X_1, X_2, X_3]\) and you want to estimate a target variable \(Y\). We'll assume a linear relationship between \(X\) and \(Y\) with some noise.

The relationship is given by:

\[ Y = w_1 X_1 + w_2 X_2 + w_3 X_3 + \epsilon \]

where:
\begin{itemize}
    \item \(w_1, w_2, w_3\) are the weights or coefficients
    \item \(\epsilon\) is a random variable representing the noise
\end{itemize}


We want to find the weights \(w_1, w_2, w_3\) that minimize the mean square error.


\end{exmp}
\end{tcolorbox}

\textbf{Steps:}
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Define the Linear Model}
   \[ \hat{Y} = w_1 X_1 + w_2 X_2 + w_3 X_3 \]
    \item \textbf{Define the Error}
   \[ \epsilon = Y - \hat{Y} \]

    \item \textbf{Minimize Mean Square Error (MSE)}
   \[ \text{MSE} = {E}[(Y - \hat{Y})^2] \]
   Minimize MSE with respect to \(w_1, w_2, w_3\) to find the optimal weights.

    \item \textbf{Optimal Weights (LMMSE Estimators)}
   \[ w_1^* = \frac{\text{Cov}(X_1, Y)}{\text{Var}(X_1)} \]
   \[ w_2^* = \frac{\text{Cov}(X_2, Y)}{\text{Var}(X_2)} \]
   \[ w_3^* = \frac{\text{Cov}(X_3, Y)}{\text{Var}(X_3)} \]
   
\end{enumerate}

These optimal weights minimize the mean square error, and the linear combination \(\hat{Y} = w_1^* X_1 + w_2^* X_2 + w_3^* X_3\) gives the linear MMSE estimate of \(Y\), shown in Figure \ref{fig:LMMSE_MV}. The code for this example is in \href{https://www.ece.unb.ca/kengleha/Textbook/Code/LMMSE_multivariate.py}{\texttt{LMMSE\_multivariate.py}}.

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.4]{figures/LMMSE_MV.png}
\caption{The linear estimate using \(\hat{Y} = w_1 X_1 + w_2 X_2 + w_3 X_3 \).}
\label{fig:LMMSE_MV}
\end{figure}

Note: This example assumes that the variance of the noise term \(\epsilon\) is known, and the covariance and variance terms are finite. In practical applications, you might need to estimate these values from data.





\section{Summary}

\textbf{Bayesian Estimation}

Bayesian estimation is a framework that incorporates prior knowledge about the parameters along with observed data to make statistical inferences. It involves updating prior beliefs using Bayes' theorem.
The posterior distribution, which is the distribution of the parameters given the observed data, is central to Bayesian estimation. The mean of the posterior distribution is a Bayesian point estimate, and this is often used as a Bayesian counterpart to MLE.\\

\textbf{Maximum A Posteriori (MAP) Estimation}

MAP estimation is a specific form of Bayesian estimation where the goal is to find the parameter values that maximize the posterior distribution. Mathematically, it involves maximizing the product of the likelihood and the prior.
MAP estimation can be seen as a compromise between MLE and Bayesian estimation, where it incorporates prior information while still providing a point estimate.\\

\textbf{Maximum Likelihood Estimation}

MLE is a classical frequentist approach to estimation. It seeks to find the parameter values that maximize the likelihood function, which measures how well the observed data fit the assumed statistical model.
MLE is often used when there is no prior information available about the parameters, and it provides point estimates for the parameters.\\




\textbf{Linear Minimum Mean Squared Error Estimation}

LMMSE estimation is a particular form of estimation that minimizes the mean squared error between the true parameter value and the estimated value, given the observed data.
LMMSE estimation is often used in the context of linear models and is particularly relevant when there is a linear relationship between the observed data and the parameters.\\

\textbf{Relationships}

\begin{itemize}
    \item MLE is a special case of Bayesian estimation where the prior distribution is assumed to be non-informative or uniform.
    \item MAP estimation incorporates prior information and reduces to MLE when the prior distribution is non-informative.
    \item LMMSE is not inherently Bayesian because it does not involve specifying prior distributions or updating beliefs based on observed data in the formal Bayesian sense. Instead, it focuses solely on minimizing the mean squared error without considering probabilistic models or uncertainties explicitly.
\end{itemize}

\chapter{Detection}

This chapter develops the fundamentals of detection theory. It follows the chapter on estimation theory as often plays a role in designing detection algorithms by estimating parameters of signals or noise distributions. \\

The basic components of a detection (or decision theory) problem are depicted in Figure \ref{fig:decision_components}.

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.3]{figures/decision_components.png}
\caption{The components of a decision theory problem.}
\label{fig:decision_components}
\end{figure}

The first component is a \emph{source} that generates a signal. In the simplest case, this signal is one of two choices. We can refer to these are \emph{hypotheses} and label them $H_0$ and $H_1$, which is a \emph{binary} problem. More generally, the signal might be one of $M$ possible choices which is referred to as an $M$-ary problem. Some typical sources include:
\begin{itemize}
    \item A digital communication system that transmits information by sending zeros ($H_0$) and ones ($H_1$).
    \item An electrocardiogram is examined to determine if a patient has had a heart attack. Here, a feature (or features) of the electrocardiogram will suggest the presence $H_1$ or absence $H_0$ of a heart attack. 
\end{itemize}

The second component is a probabilistic transition mechanism and the third is the observation space. The transition mechanism captures the dynamics of the system and therefore governs how the input will map to the observation space. Generally, with noisy or uncertain inputs (hypotheses) it may be viewed as a device that generates a point in observation space according to some probability law.



\section {Binary Detection}
\label{sec:Binary_Detection}

The binary detection problem often involves making a decision between two possible hypotheses in the presence of \emph{additive white noise}. This problem is often encountered in signal processing, communications, and statistical signal detection. The hypotheses are formulated as follows:

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Hypothesis \(H_0\):} The observed signal is purely noise (null hypothesis).
    \item \textbf{Hypothesis \(H_1\):} The observed signal contains a signal of interest embedded in white noise (alternative hypothesis).
\end{itemize}


The goal is to design a detector that can make a decision based on the observed signal to determine which hypothesis is more likely to be true. A Bayes detector is a type of detector that minimizes the probability of error by considering the probability of each hypothesis given the observed data.\\

The Bayes detector uses Bayes' rule to compute the posterior probabilities of the hypotheses given the observed data. The decision rule is then based on comparing these posterior probabilities. Mathematically, the Bayes detector can be described as follows:\\

\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Prior Probabilities:} Assign prior probabilities \(P(H_0)\) and \(P(H_1)\) to the hypotheses, indicating the initial belief in the likelihood of each hypothesis before observing the data.
    \item \textbf{Likelihood Function:} Specify the likelihood function \(P(\mathbf{X} | H_0)\) and \(P(\mathbf{X} | H_1)\), which describe the probability distribution of the observed data \(\mathbf{X}\) given each hypothesis. In general, \(\mathbf{X}\) is a vector of data samples. In the case of white noise, this distribution is typically a normal distribution.
    \item \textbf{Posterior Probabilities:} Use Bayes' rule to compute the posterior probabilities:
   \[ P(H_0 | \mathbf{X}) = \frac{P(\mathbf{X} | H_0) \cdot P(H_0)}{P(\mathbf{X})} \]
   \[ P(H_1 | \mathbf{X}) = \frac{P(\mathbf{X} | H_1) \cdot P(H_1)}{P(\mathbf{X})} \]
   where \(P(\mathbf{X})\) is the probability of the data \(\mathbf{X}\).
    \item \textbf{Decision Rule:} Make a decision by comparing the posterior probabilities:
   \begin{itemize}
       \item If \(P(H_0 | \mathbf{X}) > P(H_1 | \mathbf{X})\), decide in favor of \(H_0\).
       \item If \(P(H_1 | \mathbf{X}) > P(H_0 | \mathbf{X})\), decide in favor of \(H_1\).
      \end{itemize}
   This is equivalent to choosing \(H_1\) \emph{iff} \(P(H_1)P(\mathbf{X}|H_1) > P(H_0)P(\mathbf{X}|H_0)\) and \(H_0\) otherwise.

\end{enumerate}

\subsection{{The Bayesian Binary Detector}}


Consider the components of the  Bayesian binary detector:

\begin{itemize}
    \item The prior probabilities: \( P(H_0) \), \( P(H_1) \)
    \item \( \mathbf{R} \): The received signal; in general, a data vector.
    \item \( Z_0 \): The region for which we conclude \( H_0 \) is true when we receive \( \mathbf{R} \) in the observation space.
    \item \( Z_1 \): The region for which we conclude \( H_1 \) is true when we receive \( \mathbf{R} \) in the observation space.
    \item The channel transition probabilities (or likelihood functions). These are $f(\mathbf{R} | H_0)$ and $f(\mathbf{R} | H_1)$, or equivalently, by their probabilities $P(\mathbf{R} | H_0)$ and $P(\mathbf{R} | H_1)$.
\end{itemize}

The Bayes' binary detector determines a decision rule that divides the observation space into two parts, $Z_0$ and $Z_1$ as shown in Figure \ref{fig:Z0_Z1_area}. Whenever an observation falls in $Z_0$ we say $H_0$ and whenever an observation falls in $Z_1$ we say $H_1$.\\

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.3]{figures/Z0_Z1_area.png}
\caption{The decision space of $Z_0$ and $Z_1$.}
\label{fig:Z0_Z1_area}
\end{figure}

The error probability is given by:

\[ P(E) = P(E | H_0)P(H_0) + P(E | H_1)P(H_1) \]

Let's express \( P(E | H_0) \) and \( P(E | H_1) \) in terms of the likelihoods \( f(\mathbf{R} | H_1) \) and \(f(\mathbf{R} | H_0) \):



\[ P(E | H_0) = \int_{Z_1} f(\mathbf{R} | H_0) \, dR \]
\[ P(E | H_1) = \int_{Z_0} f(\mathbf{R} | H_1) \, dR \]
Note that if  \(\mathbf{R} = \{r_1, r_2, \ldots r_N\}\), then \( f(\mathbf{R} | H_i) \) is an \(N^{th}\) order joint density function.\\\\
Consider the decision rule which assigns the regions \( Z_0 \) and \( Z_1 \). The probability of error is given by:

\[ P(E) = P(H_0) \int_{Z_1} f(\mathbf{R} | H_0) \, dR + P(H_1) \int_{Z_0} f(\mathbf{R} | H_1) \, dR \]



This can also be written as:

\[ P(E) = P(H_0) \left \{ 1- \int_{Z_0} f(\mathbf{R} | H_0) \, dR  \right \} + P(H_1) \int_{Z_0} f(\mathbf{R} | H_1) \, dR \]

Which is:

\[ P(E) = 
\begin{cases}
    P(H_0) +  \int_{Z_0} \left \{ P(H_1) f(\mathbf{R} | H_1) 
 - P(H_0) f(\mathbf{R} | H_0) \right \} \, dR  \\
 \text{or} \\
 P(H_1) +  \int_{Z_1} \left \{ P(H_0) f(\mathbf{R} | H_0) 
 - P(H_1) f(\mathbf{R} | H_1) \right \} \, dR
\end{cases}
\]

A decision rule to minimize \(P(E)\) is to assign \(\mathbf{R}\) to \(Z_0\) \emph{iff}:
\[
\int_{Z_0} P(H_1)f(\mathbf{R} | H_1) < \int_{Z_0} P(H_0)f(\mathbf{R} | H_0)
\]

which can also be written as:
\[
P(H_1)P(\mathbf{R}|H_1) < P(H_0)P(\mathbf{R}|H_0)
\]
This can be written to establish an assignment to both $H_0$ and $H_1$ as 

\[
\frac{P(\mathbf{R}|H_1)}{P(\mathbf{R}|H_0)}  
\begin{tabular}{c @{\hspace{2\tabcolsep}} *{5}{c}}

     \(H_1\) \\
     $>$ \\
    $<$ \\
    \(H_0\)
\end{tabular}
\frac{P(H_0)}{P(H_1)} 
\]


We refer to the left side as the \emph{likelihood ratio} (for obvious reasons), denoted as 
\[
\Lambda(\mathbf{R}) = \frac{P(\mathbf{R}|H_1)}{P(\mathbf{R}|H_0)}  
\begin{tabular}{c @{\hspace{2\tabcolsep}} *{5}{c}}

     \(H_1\) \\
     $>$ \\
    $<$ \\
    \(H_0\)
\end{tabular}
\frac{P(H_0)}{P(H_1)} 
\]


This conclusion aligns with the idea that the likelihood ratio test is optimal for minimizing the probability of error in a Bayesian framework. It's also worth noting that this result is consistent with the Neyman-Pearson criterion for hypothesis testing.

\subsection{Binary Detection in  Gaussian White Noise}

The binary detection problem is the task of deciding whether a signal \(\mathbf{S}\) in the presence of white Gaussian noise is present or not in a given time interval \([0,T]\). This signal \(\mathbf{S}\) may be any waveform such as a sinusoidal pulse or a binary (ones and zeros) waveform.  For simplicity, we consider a random binary waveform, as  illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:Binary_Wave_Noisey}.

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.7]{figures/Binary_Wave_Noisey.png}
