\caption{Sampling a random binary waveform. Here, the binary waveform has a period of \(T=100 ms\) and is sampled every \(10ms.\)}
\label{fig:Binary_Wave_Noisey}
\end{figure}

We denote the received binary waveform as \(\mathbf{R}\) received signal, consisting of \(N\) samples per period (in this case, \(N=10\). This received waveform contains the noise samples \(\mathbf{N} \sim \mathcal{N}(0, \sigma_n^2)\) plus the signal \(\mathbf{S}\) (a pulse of amplitude = 1) when it is present. Consequently, the hypothesis testing problem is:
\[
\begin{aligned}
& H_0: \mathbf{R} = \mathbf{N} \\
& H_1: \mathbf{R} = \mathbf{S} + \mathbf{N}
\end{aligned}
\]

The detector decision rule is:
\[
\Lambda(\mathbf{R}) = \frac{P(\mathbf{R}|H_1)}{P(\mathbf{R}|H_0)}  
\begin{tabular}{c @{\hspace{2\tabcolsep}} *{5}{c}}

     \(H_1\) \\
     $>$ \\
    $<$ \\
    \(H_0\)
\end{tabular}
\frac{P(H_0)}{P(H_1)} 
\]

We now need to evaluate the likelihood functions, \(P(\mathbf{R}|H_0)\) and \(P(\mathbf{R}|H_1)\).  Since we know that the noise samples are \(\sim \mathcal{N}(0, \sigma_n^2)\) and \emph{i.i.d}:

\[
P(\mathbf{R}|H_0) = \prod_{i=1}^N \left [ \frac{1}{\sqrt{2 \pi} \sigma_n} \exp \left ({-\frac{r_i^2}{2 \sigma_n^2}} \right )\right] =  \frac{1}{(2 \pi \sigma^2_n)^{N/2}} \exp \left ({-\sum_{i=1}^N \frac{r_i^2}{2 \sigma_n^2}} \right )
\]

\[
P(\mathbf{R}|H_1) = \prod_{i=1}^N \left [ \frac{1}{\sqrt{2 \pi} \sigma_n} \exp \left ({-\frac{(r_i-s_i)^2}{2 \sigma_n^2}} \right )\right] =  \frac{1}{(2 \pi \sigma^2_n)^{N/2}} \exp \left ({-\sum_{i=1}^N \frac{(r_i-s_i)^2}{2 \sigma_n^2}} \right )
\]

For \(P(\mathbf{R}|H_1)\) we make the distribution zero mean by removing the signal portion of the waveform: \(\mathbf{N=R-S}\). \\

Therefore, the likelihood ratio is:
\[
\Lambda(\mathbf{R}) = \frac{\exp \left ({-\sum_{i=1}^N \frac{(r_i-s_i)^2}{2 \sigma_n^2}} \right )}{\exp \left ({-\sum_{i=1}^N \frac{r_i^2}{2 \sigma_n^2}} \right )}  
\]

Taking the natural logarithm:
\[
\ln \{  \Lambda(\mathbf{R}) \} = \sum_{i=1}^N 2\frac{r_i s_i}{2 \sigma_n^2} - \sum_{i=1}^N \frac{(r_i-s_i)^2}{2 \sigma_n^2}  
\begin{tabular}{c @{\hspace{2\tabcolsep}} *{5}{c}}

     \(H_1\) \\
     $>$ \\
    $<$ \\
    \(H_0\)
\end{tabular}
\ln \left \{\frac{P(H_0)}{P(H_1)} \right \} 
\]

This can be rearranged as:
\[
\underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^N r_i s_i}_{\text{matched filter}}   
\begin{tabular}{c @{\hspace{2\tabcolsep}} *{5}{c}}

     \(H_1\) \\
     $>$ \\
    $<$ \\
    \(H_0\)
\end{tabular}
\underbrace{\sigma_n^2 \ln \left \{\frac{P(H_0)}{P(H_1)} \right \}}_{\text{a priori probabilities}}  - \underbrace{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^N s_i^2}_{\frac{1}{2}\text{ signal energy}}
\]

The left-hand side is a discrete-time \emph{matched filter}, with the decision threshold determined by the a priori probabilities and the signal energy.\\

Note: if the noise is not Gaussian, then the matched filter detector will not yield the minimum \(P(E)\), but it will yield the best SNR. \\ 

\begin{tcolorbox}
\begin{exmp}{Numerical Example of Binary Detector.}

    We now consider a random binary waveform with \(P(H_0) = P(H_1) = 0.5\). This corresponds to a log likelihood ratio of \(\Lambda(\mathbf{R}) = 0\). The waveform is subject to Gaussian white noise with $\sim \mathcal{N} (0, \sigma_n)$. The noisy waveform is sampled 10 times per period at the detector.

    \end{exmp}
\end{tcolorbox}

    The detector is implemented in \href{https://www.ece.unb.ca/kengleha/Textbook/Code/BinaryDetectorExample.py}{\texttt{BinaryDetectorExample.py}}. First, consider \(\sigma_n=0.1\). First, consider the likelihood functions \(P(\mathbf{R}|H_0)\) and \(P(\mathbf{R}|H_1)\), which are plotted in Figure \ref{fig:BinaryDetectorLikelihoods} (top). For this relatively low value of noise variance, it is clear that little overlap exists between a transmitted \(H_0\) or \(H_1\). Indeed, this can be observed in the samples from each of samples under \(H_0\) or \(H_1\), shown in the bottom plot of \ref{fig:BinaryDetectorLikelihoods}. \\
    

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.4]{figures/BinaryDetectorLikelihoods.png}
\includegraphics[scale=0.4]{figures/BinaryDetectorSamples.png}
\caption{A binary detector with noise variance \(\sigma_n=0.1\). Top: The likelihood functions of the binary detector. Bottom: samples of the noisy signal during transmission of \(H_0\) and \(H_1\).}
\label{fig:BinaryDetectorLikelihoods}
\end{figure}

In this case, we have:\\

\begin{tabular}{c @{\hspace{2\tabcolsep}} *{5}{c}}
 - Log-Likelihood Ratio (H0): & -13.71 & (choose $H_0$) \\
    - Log-Likelihood Ratio (H1): & 77.61 & (choose $H_1$) 
     
\end{tabular}

These are safely below and above the detection threshold of 0.\\

Increasing the noise variance to \(\sigma_n=1.0\) depicts a more challenging situation for the detector, illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:BinaryDetectorLikelihoods2}. In this case, we have:\\

\begin{tabular}{c @{\hspace{2\tabcolsep}} *{5}{c}}
 - Log-Likelihood Ratio (H0): & -6.43 & (choose $H_0$) \\
    - Log-Likelihood Ratio (H1): & 6.60 & (choose $H_1$) 
     
\end{tabular}
   

Even in the presence of substantial noise, and outliers that would be clearly misclassified on their own, the inclusion of 10 samples in the likelihood calculation builds in robustness. 

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.4]{figures/BinaryDetectorLikelihoods2.png}
\includegraphics[scale=0.4]{figures/BinaryDetectorSamples2.png}
\caption{A binary detector with noise variance \(\sigma_n=1.0\). Top: The likelihood functions of the binary detector. Bottom: samples of the noisy signal during transmission of \(H_0\) and \(H_1\).}
\label{fig:BinaryDetectorLikelihoods2}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Detector Performance Measures}

In the context of binary hypothesis testing, several performance measures are used to assess the performance of a detector. Consider first the possible outcomes of a detector:

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{True Negative} (\emph{TN}): The outcome is correctly identified as negative (belonging to \(H_0\)) by the detector when it is actually negative (belonging to \(H_0\)). 
    \item \textbf{True {Positive}} (\emph{TP}): The outcome is correctly identified as positive (belonging to \(H_1\)) by the detector when it is actually positive (belonging to \(H_1\)).
    \item \textbf{False Negative} (\emph{FN}): The outcome is incorrectly identified as negative (belonging to \(H_0\)) by the detector when it is actually positive (belonging to \(H_1\)).
    \item \textbf{False Positive} (\emph{FP}): The outcome is correctly identified as positive (belonging to \(H_1\)) by the detector when it is actually negative (belonging to \(H_0\)). 

\end{itemize}

Here,  are some of the key performance measures:

\begin{enumerate}

\item \textbf{True Negative Rate (TNR) or Specificity}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Measures the ability of the detector to correctly determine the absence of the signal when it is not present.
    \item \(P(\text{Decision} = H_0 | H_o \text{ is true})\)
    \item \(TNR = \frac{{TN}}{{TN + FP}}\)
\end{itemize}

\item \textbf{True Positive Rate (TPR) or Sensitivity}
\begin{itemize}
    \item  Measures the probability of the detector correctly detecting the presence of the signal when it is present.
    \item \(P(\text{Decision} = H_1 | H_1 \text{ is true})\)
    \item \(TPR = \frac{{FP}}{{FP + TN}}\)
\end{itemize}

\item \textbf{False Negative Rate (FNR) or Probability of False Negative (\(P_{FN}\))}
\begin{itemize}
    \item  Measures the probability of the detector failing to detect the signal when it is actually present.
    \item \(P(\text{Decision} = H_0 | H_1 \text{ is true})\)
    \item \(FNR = P_{FN} =  \frac{{FN}}{{FN + TP}}\)
\end{itemize}


\item \textbf{False Positive Rate (FPR) or Probability False Alarm (\(P_{FA}\))}
\begin{itemize}
    \item  Measures the probability of the detector incorrectly detecting the presence of the signal when it is not present.
    \item \(P(\text{Decision} = H_1 | H_0 \text{ is true})\).
    \item \(FPR = P_{FA} =  \frac{{FP}}{{FP + TN}}\)
\end{itemize}





\item \textbf{Positive Predictive Value (PPV) or Precision }
\begin{itemize}
    \item Measures the proportion of true positive decisions among all positive decisions made by the detector.
    \item \(PPV = \frac{{TP}}{{TP + FP}}\)
\end{itemize}


\item \textbf{F1 Score}
\begin{itemize}
    \item The F1 score is the harmonic mean of precision and sensitivity. It provides a balanced measure of a detector's accuracy when dealing with imbalanced datasets.
   \item \(F1 \text{ Score} = \frac{2}{PPV^{-1} + TPR^{-1}} = \frac{2 TP}{2 TP + FP + FN}\)
\end{itemize}

\end{enumerate}

\bigskip

\begin{exmp}{Binary Detector Metrics}
    \begin{tcolorbox}
        Suppose we have a binary detector that detects a digital signal (0 or 1) with additive noise. The detector outputs a probability of the signal being 1. We can use a threshold (e.g., 0.5) to make a binary decision (0 or 1). Show the effect of the noise noise on the binary detector metrics.  
    \end{tcolorbox}
\end{exmp}


The result is shown in Figure \ref{fig:BinaryDetectorMetrics}. The code \href{https://www.ece.unb.ca/kengleha/Textbook/Code/BinaryDetectorMetrics.py}{\texttt{BinaryDetectorMetrics.py}} implements this example.


\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.7]{figures/BinaryDetectorMetrics.png}
\caption{The effect of noise level on binary detector metrics.}
\label{fig:BinaryDetectorMetrics}
\end{figure}

\bigskip

As the noise becomes much larger than the signal, the detector's output becomes essentially random, and the detector is equally likely to detect a TP, TN, FP or FN (if the probability of transmitting true and false are equal). Consequently, the values of each of the metrics converges to 0.5, indicating a complete loss of signal detection capability. 




\chapter{Optimal Filtering}

Optimal filtering is a fundamental concept in signal processing and control theory, aiming to estimate the state of a dynamic system or the parameters of a signal from noisy or uncertain observations. It encompasses a diverse range of techniques designed to extract useful information from observed data while minimizing estimation errors or achieving certain performance criteria. \\

At the heart of optimal filtering lies the principle of optimality, which entails selecting the most suitable estimation algorithm or filter to minimize some measure of error or cost. The choice of optimality criterion depends on the specific application and the desired performance metrics. Common optimality criteria include minimizing mean squared error, maximizing likelihood, minimizing Bayesian risk, or achieving certain performance trade-offs.\\

An important optimal filtering technique is the Wiener filter, which is used for linear estimation problems with stationary stochastic inputs. The Wiener filter minimizes mean squared error by exploiting the statistical properties of the input signal and noise, providing an optimal linear estimate of the desired signal.\\

One of the most well-known and widely used optimal filtering techniques is the Kalman filter. The Kalman filter is particularly powerful for estimating the state of linear dynamic systems subject to Gaussian noise. It recursively updates the state estimate based on a dynamic model of the system and noisy observations, leveraging both past estimates and current measurements to produce an optimal estimate of the system state. \\




\section{Linear Time-Invariant Systems with Stochastic Inputs}

Understanding Linear Time-Invariant (LTI) systems with stochastic inputs is essential for study of optimal filtering. This is because LTI systems are prevalent in engineering applications due to their simplicity and analytical tractability, and many real-world signals are stochastic in nature.\\

When dealing with stochastic inputs, the theory of LTI systems can be extended to include random processes or stochastic signals. A block diagram of an LTI system \(h(t)\) with input process \(X(t)\) and output \(Y(t)\) is depicted in Figure \ref{fig:LTI_system}.

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.4]{figures/LTI_system.png}
\caption{A generalized block diagram of a linear time-invariant system.}
\label{fig:LTI_system}
\end{figure}

LTI systems with stochastic inputs have the following characteristics:

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Linearity:} An LTI system is linear if it satisfies the superposition principle. For stochastic inputs, linearity implies that the system responds to the sum of individual input realizations in the same way it would respond to the sum of the individual responses to each input.
    \item \textbf{Time-invariance:} A system is time-invariant if its behavior or response does not change with time. In the context of stochastic inputs, this means that the system's response characteristics remain constant over time, regardless of when the input is applied.
    \item \textbf{Convolution operation:} The response of an LTI system to a stochastic input is often characterized by the convolution operation. For instance, if \(X(t)\) is the input stochastic process and \(h(t)\) is the impulse response of the LTI system, then the output \(Y(t)\) is given by \(Y(t) = X(t) * h(t)\), where '*' denotes convolution.
    \item \textbf{Stationarity:} When dealing with stochastic processes, assumptions about the stationarity of the input process become important. Wide-sense stationarity or strict-sense stationarity conditions are generally assumed.
\end{itemize}

With stochastic inputs, an LTI system  may be thought of as providing a one-to-one mapping between corresponding sample functions, as depicted in Figure \ref{fig:LTI_mapping}.\\

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.5]{figures/LTI_mapping.png}
\caption{One-to-one mapping of input to output sample functions in an LTI system.}
\label{fig:LTI_mapping}
\end{figure}



\subsection{Cross-correlation and Auto-correlation}
Now, we turn to the first and second-order moments and PSD of the output process \(Y(t)\) in terms of \(h(t)\) and the moments of \(X(t)\). \\
\[
\begin{split}
    Y(t) &  = \int_{-\infty}^\infty X(\upsilon)h(t-\upsilon) d\upsilon\\
    & = \int_{-\infty}^\infty X(t-\upsilon)h(\upsilon) d\upsilon
\end{split}
\]

\[
    Y(t+\tau)  = \int_{-\infty}^\infty X(t+\tau-\upsilon)h(\upsilon) d\upsilon
\]

Consider first the cross-correlation function:
\[
\begin{split}
    \phi_\mathrm{xy}(\tau) & = E [ X(t) Y(t+ \tau)]\\
    & = E \left [ X(t) \int_{-\infty}^\infty X(t+\tau-\upsilon)h(\upsilon) d\upsilon \right ] \\
    & = \int_{-\infty}^\infty E \left [ X(t)  X(t+\tau-\upsilon) \right ] h(\upsilon) d\upsilon  \\
    & = \int_{-\infty}^\infty \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau-\upsilon) h(\upsilon) d \upsilon
\end{split}
\]

Therefore:
\[
\boxed{\phi_\mathrm{xy}(\tau) = \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) * h(\tau)}
\]
which is a convolution with \(h(t)\).\\

Now the autocorrelation function:
\[
\begin{split}
    \phi_\mathrm{yy}(\tau) & = E [ Y(t) Y(t+ \tau)]\\
    & = E \left [ \int_{-\infty}^\infty X(t-u)h(u) du \int_{-\infty}^\infty X(t+\tau-\upsilon)h(\upsilon) d\upsilon \right ] \\
    & = \int_{-\infty}^\infty \int_{-\infty}^\infty E \left [  X(t-u) X(t+\tau-\upsilon) \right ] h(u)   h(\upsilon) du d\upsilon  \\
    & = \int_{-\infty}^\infty \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau-\upsilon+u) h(u) h(\upsilon) d \upsilon
\end{split}
\]

Therefore:
\[
\boxed{\phi_\mathrm{yy}(\tau) = \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) * h(\tau) * h(-\tau)}
\]

which is a double convolution with \(h(t)\).\\

\subsection{Cross- and Auto-Spectral Density}
Consider the the cross-spectral density:
\[
\begin{split}
    \Phi_\mathrm{xy}(f) & = \mathcal{F} \left \{ \phi_\mathrm{xy}(\tau)\right \}\\
                & = \int_{-\infty}^\infty \phi_\mathrm{xy}(\tau) e^{-j2\pi f \tau} d \tau\\
                & = \int_{-\infty}^\infty \left [ \int_{-\infty}^\infty \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau - \upsilon) h(\upsilon) d \upsilon \right ] e^{-j2\pi f \tau} d \tau\ \\
                & = \int_{-\infty}^\infty \left [ \int_{-\infty}^\infty \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) e^{-j2\pi f \tau} e^{-j2\pi f \upsilon} d \tau\ \right ] h(\upsilon) d \upsilon \\
                 & = \int_{-\infty}^\infty \left [ \Phi_\mathrm{xx}(f) e^{-j2\pi f \upsilon} \, d f \right ] h(\upsilon) d \upsilon \\
                 & = \Phi_\mathrm{xx}(f)  \int_{-\infty}^\infty  h(\upsilon) e^{-j2\pi f \upsilon} d \upsilon
\end{split}
\]


Therefore:
\[
\boxed{\Phi_\mathrm{xy}(f)  = \Phi_\mathrm{xx}(f) H(f)}
\]

which is the Fourier transform pair of the cross-correlation \(\phi_\mathrm{xy}(\tau)  = \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) * h(\tau)\).\\

Now consider the the auto-spectral density:
\[
\begin{split}
    \Phi_\mathrm{xy}(f) & = \mathcal{F} \left \{ \phi_\mathrm{yy}(\tau)\right \}\\
                & = \int_{-\infty}^\infty \phi_\mathrm{yy}(\tau) e^{-j2\pi f \tau} d \tau\\
                & = \int_{-\infty}^\infty \left [ \int_{-\infty}^\infty \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau - \upsilon +u) h(u) h(\upsilon) du d \upsilon \right ] e^{-j2\pi f \tau} d \tau\ \\
\end{split}
\]

Using the same methods as above:
\[
\Phi_\mathrm{yy}(f)  = \Phi_\mathrm{xx}(f) H(f) H^{*}(f)
\]

\[
\boxed{\Phi_\mathrm{yy}(f)  = \Phi_\mathrm{xx}(f) \left |H(f) \right |^2}
\]

\subsection{The Mean}

\[
\begin{split}
    E[Y(t)] & = E \left [ \int_{-\infty}^\infty X(t-\upsilon) h(\upsilon) d \upsilon \right ]\\
                & =  \int_{-\infty}^\infty E \left [ X(t-\upsilon) \right ] h(\upsilon) d \upsilon \\
                & =  \mu_\mathrm{x} \int_{-\infty}^\infty  h(\upsilon) d \upsilon
\end{split}
\]

\[
\boxed{\mu_\mathrm{y}  = \mu_\mathrm{x} H(0)}
\]

where \(H(0)\) is the DC gain of \(H(f)\). \\


\textbf{Note:} If \(X(t)\) is WSS, then \(Y(t)\) is also WSS.\\

Observation:
\[
\begin{split}
    Y(t) & = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} X(t) h(t-u) du\\
        & \approx \sum_{i=-\infty}^\infty X(i \Delta) h(t-i \Delta) \Delta
\end{split}
\]

 If \(h(t)\) is lowpass with respect to \(X(t)\),

\[
Y(t) \approx h(t) \sum_{i=-\infty}^\infty X(i \Delta)  \Delta
\]

If the \(X(i \Delta)\) are roughly independent, the output will be Gaussian (central limit theorem).  

\section{Shaping Filters}

Given a process \(X(t)\) with some \(\Phi_\mathrm{xx}(f)\), generate a process
\(Y(t)\) with some \(\Phi_\mathrm{yy}(f)\).\\

Recall that:

\[ Y(t) = h(t) \ast X(t) \]

and 
\[ \Phi_\mathrm{yy}(f) = |H(f)|^2 \Phi_\mathrm{xx}(f) \]

so that
\[ |H(f)|^2 = \frac {\Phi_\mathrm{yy}(f)} {\Phi_\mathrm{xx}(f)} \]

\begin{tcolorbox}
    
\begin{exmp}{Shaping a white noise process.}\\
Suppose \(X(t)\) is white with \(\Phi_\mathrm{xx}(f) = K\) and we want

\[
\Phi_\mathrm{yy}(\omega) = \frac{1}{\omega^2 + \alpha^2}
\]

Find the required shaping filter \(h(t)\).

\end{exmp}
\end{tcolorbox}

Therefore,
\[ 
\begin{split}
    |H(f)|^2    & =   \frac{\Phi_\mathrm{yy}(f)}{\Phi_\mathrm{xx}(f) } \\
                & = \frac{1}{K(\omega^2 + \alpha^2)}
\end{split}
\]

To determine the transfer function, we  factor:
\[
|H(f)|^2 = \frac{1}{\sqrt{K}(\alpha+j \omega)} \cdot \frac{1}{\sqrt{K}(\alpha-j \omega)}
\]

Taking the causal part:
\[
H(\omega) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{K}(\alpha+j \omega)}
\]

Therefore:
\[
h(t) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}e^{-\alpha t} u(t)
\]

which is a $1^{st}$ order LPF, depicted in Figure \ref{fig:LPF}.

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.6]{figures/LPF.png}
\caption{The impulse response of a first-order LPF \(h(t) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{K}}e^{-\alpha t} u(t)\) for \(K = 2.0 \) and \(\alpha=0.5\).}
\label{fig:LPF}
\end{figure}


\section{Poles and Zeros of Random Spectra}
In the context of random processes, the terms "poles" and "zeros" are often used in relation to the power spectral density.\\

\textbf{Poles}
\begin{itemize} 
    \item Poles are locations where the denominator of PSD becomes zero (and the PSD approaches infinity).
    \item The presence of poles in the spectrum indicates the characteristic frequencies at which the process tends to oscillate or exhibit significant energy.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Zeros}
\begin{itemize}
    \item Zeros are locations where the numerator of the PSD becomes zero (and the the PSD becomes zero).
    \item The presence of zeros in the spectrum indicates frequencies at which certain components of the signal are absent or have minimal contribution.
\end{itemize}

Since the PSD of a random spectrum real and even, the poles and zeros must occur in groups of 4 or 2, as illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:poles_zeros}. \\

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.4]{figures/Poles_Zeros.png}
\caption{A typical pole-zero plot of the PSD of a random process.}
\label{fig:poles_zeros}
\end{figure}

For any \(\Phi_\mathrm{xx}(\omega)\) we can factor, grouping top-half plane (THP)  poles and zeros and lower-half plane (LHP) poles and zeros.
\[
\Phi_\mathrm{xx}(\omega) = \underbrace {\Phi_\mathrm{xx}^+(\omega)}_{\text{THP}} \underbrace {\Phi_\mathrm{xx}^-(\omega)}_{\text{LHP}}
\]

\begin{tcolorbox}
    \begin{exmp}{First-order lowpass system.}
    
Suppose that \(\phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) = e^{-a |\tau|}\) as illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:LP_phi}. Find $\Phi_\mathrm{xx}(\omega)$.
\end{exmp}
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.4]{figures/LP_phi.png}
\caption{Autocorrelation function \(\phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) = e^{-a |\tau|}\).}
\label{fig:LP_phi}
\end{figure}



The Fourier transform of $\phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau)$, denoted as $\Phi(\omega)$, is given by
\[
\Phi_\mathrm{xx}(\omega) = \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) e^{-j\omega\tau} \, d\tau.
\]

Substituting $\phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) = e^{-a|\tau|}$ into the integral, we get
\[
\begin{aligned}
\Phi_\mathrm{xx}(\omega) &= \int_{-\infty}^{\infty} e^{-a|\tau|} e^{-j\omega\tau} \, d\tau \\
&= \underbrace{\int_{-\infty}^{0} e^{(a-j\omega)\tau} \, d\tau}_{\text{-ve time}} + \underbrace{\int_{0}^{\infty} e^{(-a-j\omega)\tau} \, d\tau} _{\text{+ve time}}
\end{aligned}
\]

Integrating each term separately, we have
\[
\begin{aligned}
\Phi_\mathrm{xx}(\omega) &= \left. \frac{e^{(a-j\omega)\tau}}{a-j\omega} \right|_{-\infty}^{0} + \left. \frac{e^{(-a-j\omega)\tau}}{-a-j\omega} \right|_{0}^{\infty} \\
&= \underbrace{\frac{1}{a-j\omega}}_{\text{pole at }-ja} + \underbrace{\frac{1}{a+j\omega}}_{\text{pole at }+ja}.
\end{aligned}
\]

This is illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:LP_PZ}.\\

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.5]{figures/LP_PZ.png}
\caption{Pole-zero plot of $\Phi_\mathrm{xx}(\omega) = \frac{2a}{a^2 + \omega^2}$.}
\label{fig:LP_PZ}
\end{figure}

Note that positive time is mapped to THP poles and zeros, while negative time is mapped to LHP poles and zeros.\\

To simplify further, multiply the numerator and denominator of the second term by the complex conjugate of the denominator:
\[
\begin{aligned}
\Phi_\mathrm{xx}(\omega) &= \frac{a+j\omega}{a^2 - (j\omega)^2} + \frac{a-j\omega}{a^2 - (j\omega)^2} \\
&= \frac{2a}{a^2 + \omega^2}.
\end{aligned}
\]


This is illustrated in \ref{fig:PSD_omega} for \(a=2\).

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.5]{figures/PSD_omega.png}
\caption{The PSD of $\Phi_\mathrm{xx}(\omega) = \frac{2a}{a^2 + \omega^2}$ for \(a=2\).}
\label{fig:PSD_omega}
\end{figure}



\section{Wiener Filters}\label{Wiener}

\subsection{The General Case}

The general components of an optimal filtering system are shown in Figure 6.8. Let \(x(t)\) be the observed data and \(y(t)\) be the output of an LTI system. The desired signal is \(z(t)\), and the goal of designing a filter is to estimate \(z(t)\) that is optimum in some sense, that is, to apply some minimization criterion to the
error signal \(e(t)\). This criterion will depend on the application and its goals.

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.6
]{figures/Cont_LMMSE.png}
\caption{The general case for the optimal filtering. Here, \(x(t)\) is the input (observed data), \(z(t)\) is the desired output of the LTI system, \(y(t) = \hat{z(t)}\) is the actual output which is an estimate of \(z(t)\), and \(e(t)\) is the error.}
\label{fig:Cont_MMSE}
\end{figure}

In the most general sense, we have 
\[
y(t) = \hat{z}(t + \alpha)
\]

If $\alpha = 0$, we refer to this as \emph{filtering}. If $\alpha < 0$, it is  \emph{smoothing}. For $\alpha > 0$, we are performing  \emph{prediction}.\\

\begin{description}
    \item[Filtering ($\alpha = 0$)] \hfill \\
    Filtering is the process of estimating the \textit{current} state of a signal using all measurements available up to the present time $t$.
    \begin{itemize}
        \item \textbf{Goal:} To ``filter'' out noise and extract the best possible estimate of what is happening right now.
        \item \textbf{Example:} A noise-canceling headset estimating the current ambient noise to phase it out in real-time.
    \end{itemize}

    \item[Smoothing ($\alpha < 0$)] \hfill \\
    Smoothing is the process of estimating a \textit{past} state of a signal using data collected up to time $t$. Because you have access to data that occurred after the time point of interest ($t + \alpha$), you can create a much cleaner, ``smoother'' estimate than is possible in real-time.
    \begin{itemize}
        \item \textbf{Goal:} To obtain a high-accuracy historical record by utilizing ``future'' information (relative to the point being estimated).
        \item \textbf{Example:} Post-processing GPS data from a hike to create a clean map of where you walked, removing the ``zig-zags'' caused by momentary signal drops.
    \end{itemize}

    \item [Prediction ($\alpha > 0$)] \hfill \\
    Prediction is the process of estimating a future state of a signal using only the measurements available up to the present time $t$. Since you are trying to guess what hasn't happened yet, this is typically the "noisiest" and most difficult form of estimation.
    \begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{Goal:} To anticipate future values or trends to allow for proactive adjustments or "look-ahead" logic.
    \item \textbf{Example:} A thermostat that "predicts" the outdoor temperature drop to start heating the house early, or a radar system predicting where a plane will be in 5 seconds to help with tracking.
    \end{itemize}
\end{description}

The Wiener filter is essentially the "optimal engine" that makes all three processes (filtering, smoothing, and prediction) work. While the terms we just discussed describe what you are trying to do with time, the Wiener filter provides the mathematical recipe for how to do it perfectly—specifically by minimizing the Mean Square Error (MSE).

\subsection{The Wiener filter}
Consider again that the general components of an optimal filtering system are shown in Figure 6.8. Let \(x(t)\) be the observed data and \(y(t)\) be the output of an LTI system. The desired signal is \(z(t)\), and the goal of designing a filter is to estimate \(z(t)\) that is optimum in some sense, that is, to apply some minimization criterion to the
error signal \(e(t)\). \\

If all signals are sample functions from stochastic processes and are Wide-Sense Stationary, and the optimization crtirion is mean squared error, then the optimum filter is the \emph{Wiener filter}.\\

As an example, consider a signal \(s(t)\) that we want to estimate from a measurable input \(x(t)\). It might be necessary to estimate \(s(t)\) if, for example, the input \(x(t)\) is composed of the signal \(s(t)\) corrupted by additive white noise \(\omega(t)\), as illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:Noise_cancel}.


\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.6
]{figures/Noise_cancel.png}
\caption{The general case for noise cancellation.}
\label{fig:Noise_cancel}
\end{figure}

In this case, 
\[
z(t) = s(t)
\]
and
\[
y(t) = \hat{s(t)}
\]





\subsubsection*{Derivation}


First, we start with the orthogonality principle:

\[
\begin{aligned}
    E[e(t) x(u)] & = 0 \quad \forall u \\
    E[(z(t)-y(t))x(u)] & = 0 \quad \forall u\\
    E[z(t)x(u)] - E[y(t)x(u)] & = 0 \quad \forall u\\
    \phi_\mathrm{zx}(u-t) - \phi_\mathrm{yx}(u-t) & = 0  \quad \forall u
\end{aligned}
\]
Therefore,

\[
\phi_\mathrm{zx}(u-t) - \int_{-\infty}^\infty \phi_\mathrm{xx}(u-t-\nu)h(-\nu)\, d \nu = 0 \quad \forall u
\]

\[
\phi_\mathrm{xz}(t-u) - \int_{-\infty}^\infty \phi_\mathrm{xx}(t-u-\nu)h(\nu)\, d \nu = 0 \quad \forall u
\]

Let \(\tau = t-u\),

\[
\phi_\mathrm{xz}(\tau) - \int_{-\infty}^\infty \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau-\nu)h(\nu)\, d \nu = 0 \quad \forall \tau
\]
Therefore,
\[
\boxed{\phi_\mathrm{xz}(\tau) = \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau)*h_o(\tau)}
\]

This is the \emph{continuous Wiener-Hopf equation} where $h_o(t)$ is the optimal Wiener filter. Note also that since the cross correlation function is an odd function:
\[
\phi_\mathrm{zx}(\tau) = \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau)*h_o(-\tau)
\]

Also, since \(z(t) = y(t) - e(t)\):
\[
\phi_\mathrm{xz}(\tau) = \phi_\mathrm{xy}(\tau)
\]

This is because the error \(e(t)\) is orthogonal to the data \(x(t)\). It means that in an optimal system, the input $x(t)$ "sees" the hidden signal $z(t)$ exactly the same way it "sees" the estimate $y(t)$. If $\phi_{xz}(\tau) \neq \phi_{xy}(\tau)$, it would mean there is still some information about the signal $z(t)$ left in the error $e(t)$ that the filter failed to extract. By making these correlations equal, the filter has extracted every bit of useful information from the input $x(t)$ to create the estimate $y(t)$. 

\subsection{Non-Causal Solution to the Wiener-Hopf Equation}

Taking the Fourier transform of the continuous Wiener-Hopf equation:
\[
\mathcal{F} \left \{ \phi_\mathrm{xz}(\tau) - 
 \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau)*h_o(\tau)\right \}=  0
\]
Now,
\[
\Phi_\mathrm{xz}(\omega) - \Phi_\mathrm{xx}(\omega)\cdot H_o(\omega)=  0
\]
Therefore, 
\[
\boxed{H_o(\omega) = \frac{\Phi_\mathrm{xz}(\omega)}{\Phi_\mathrm{xx}(\omega)}}
\]

We will see that this is impractical in most cases, but a useful performance reference for causal systems.

\subsection{Causal Solution to the Wiener-Hopf Equation}

The causality constraint is defined as:

\[
h(t) = \begin{cases}
    0, & t<0 \\
    h(t) & t\ge 0
\end{cases}
\]

Without causality constraints, 
\[
\begin{aligned}
    E[e(t) x(u)] & = 0\\
    E[(z(t)-y(t))x(u)] & = 0 \\
    E \left [z(t)x(u) - \left \{ \int x(t-\nu)h(\nu) \, d \nu \right \} x(u) \right ] & = 0 \quad \forall u
\end{aligned}
\]

Now, introduce the causality constraints. The orthogonality condition $E[e(t)x(u)] = 0$ only needs to hold for the data we have actually observed. In a real-time (causal) system, we only have access to the input $x(u)$ up to the current time $t$. 
\[
 E \left [z(t)x(u) - \left \{ \int x(t-\nu)h(\nu) \, d \nu \right \} x(u) \right ] = 0 \quad \forall u \le t
\]


From before, this is:
\[
\phi_\mathrm{xz} (t-u) - \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\phi_\mathrm{xx}(t-u-\nu)h(\nu) \, d \nu = 0 \quad \forall u \le t 
\]

If we define the lag as $\tau = t - u$, the condition must hold for all $\tau \ge 0$.

\[
\phi_\mathrm{xz} (\tau) - \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau-\nu)h(\nu) \, d \nu = 0 \quad \forall \tau \ge 0 
\]

But, we cannot simply take the Fourier transform of this expression, since the RHS=0 \emph{only} for \(\tau \ge 0\). For \(\tau < 0\), RHS is undefined.\\

Consider a function:
\[
f(\tau) = 
\begin{cases}
    0, & \tau \ge 0 \\
    f(\tau), & \tau < 0
\end{cases}
\]

Therefore we can set the RHS=0:
\[
\phi_\mathrm{xz} (\tau) - \int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau-\nu)h(\nu) \, d \nu = f(\tau) \quad \forall \tau  
\]

Now, take the Fourier transform:

\[
\Phi_\mathrm{xz}(\omega) - H(\omega)\Phi_\mathrm{xx}(\omega) = F(\omega)
\]

Since \(f(\tau)=0\) for \(\tau \ge 0\), the poles and zeros of \(F(\omega)\) are all BHP (negative time).\\

Assume that $\Phi_\mathrm{xz}(\omega)$ and  $H(\omega)\Phi_\mathrm{xx}(\omega)$ are ratios of polynomials in $\omega$:

\[
\Phi_\mathrm{xx}(\omega) =  \underbrace{\Phi_\mathrm{xx}^-(\omega)}_{\text{BHP}}\underbrace{\Phi_\mathrm{xx}^+(\omega)}_{\text{THP}}
\]

where BHP and THP designates bottom and top half plane poles and zeros, respectively.\\

Therefore,
\[
\underbrace{\Phi_\mathrm{xx}^-(\omega)}_\text{BHP } \left [\underbrace{\frac{\Phi_\mathrm{xz}(\omega)}{\Phi_\mathrm{xx}^-(\omega)}}_\text{THP, BHP} - \underbrace{\Phi_\mathrm{xx}^+(\omega) H(\omega)}_\text{THP}\right ] = \underbrace{F(\omega)}_\text{BHP } 
\]

Since the RHS has only BHP poles and zeros, so does the left. This means that the THP poles and zeros of $\frac{\Phi_\mathrm{xz}(\omega)}{\Phi_\mathrm{xx}^-(\omega)}$ must cancel those of $\Phi_\mathrm{xx}^+(\omega) H(\omega)$ to leave only BHP poles and zeros\\

To do this we expand $\frac{\Phi_\mathrm{xz}(\omega)}{\Phi_\mathrm{xx}^-(\omega)}$ into partial fractions:

\[
\frac{\Phi_\mathrm{xz}(\omega)}{\Phi_\mathrm{xx}^-(\omega)} = \left [\frac{\Phi_\mathrm{xz}(\omega)}{\Phi_\mathrm{xx}^-(\omega)} \right ]^- +  \left [\frac{\Phi_\mathrm{xz}(\omega)}{\Phi_\mathrm{xx}^-(\omega)} \right ]^+
\]

In order for the THP poles and zeros in $\frac{\Phi_\mathrm{xz}(\omega)}{\Phi_\mathrm{xx}^-(\omega)}$ to cancel those of $\Phi_\mathrm{xx}^+(\omega) H(\omega)$, 
\[
\Phi_\mathrm{xx}^+(\omega) H(\omega) = \left [\frac{\Phi_\mathrm{xz}(\omega)}{\Phi_\mathrm{xx}^-(\omega)} \right ]^+
\]

Solving for the filter:

\[
\boxed{H_o(\omega) = \frac{1}{\Phi_\mathrm{xx}^+(\omega) }\left [\frac{\Phi_\mathrm{xz}(\omega)}{\Phi_\mathrm{xx}^-(\omega)} \right ]^+}
\]

This is the solution for the \emph{causal Wiener filter}.

\bigskip

\begin{tcolorbox}
\begin{exmp}{A Simple Wiener Filter.}
\end{exmp}

Consider a signal \(s(t)\) defined by its PSD:
\[\Phi_\mathrm{ss}(\omega) = \frac{3}{1+\omega^2}
\]
The observed data \(x(t)\) consists of the signal \(s(t)\) corrupted by additive zero-mean white noise \(w(t)\):
\[
\Phi_\mathrm{ww}(\omega) = 1
\]

Find the causal Wiener filter to recover \(s(t)\) from \(x(t)\).
\end{tcolorbox}

\bigskip
We assume that the signal and the noise are uncorrelated:
\[
E[s(t)w(t)] = E[s(t)]E[w(t)]
\]

Since $E[w(t)] = 0$ then 
\[
E[s(t)w(t)] = 0
\]

Since \(x(t) = s(t) + w(t)\), then 
\[
\Phi_\mathrm{xx}(\omega) = \Phi_\mathrm{ss}(\omega) + \Phi_\mathrm{sw}(\omega) + \Phi_\mathrm{ws}(\omega) + \Phi_\mathrm{ww}(\omega)
\]

Knowing that $E[s(t)w(t)] = 0$:

\[
\Phi_\mathrm{xx}(\omega) = \Phi_\mathrm{ss}(\omega) + \Phi_\mathrm{ww}(\omega)
\]

From the known signal and noise characteristics:
\[
\begin{aligned}
   \Phi_\mathrm{xx}(\omega) & = \frac{1}{1+\omega^2}+ 1\\
                        & = \frac{4+\omega^2}{1+\omega^2}\\
                        & = \frac{(2+j\omega)(2-j\omega)}{(1+j\omega)(1-j\omega)}
\end{aligned}
\]

Decomposing into THP and BHP:
\[
\Phi_\mathrm{xx}^+(\omega) = \frac{2+j\omega}{1+j\omega}
\]

\[
\Phi_\mathrm{xx}^-(\omega) = \frac{2-j\omega}{1-j\omega}
\]

Since $s(t)$ and $w(t)$ are uncorrelated:
\[
\Phi_\mathrm{xz}(\omega) \equiv \Phi_\mathrm{xs}(\omega) = \Phi_\mathrm{ss}(\omega) = \frac{3}{1+\omega^2}
\]
Here, \[
\Phi_\mathrm{xs}(\omega) = \Phi_\mathrm{ss}(\omega)
\]




\[
\begin{aligned}
   \text{Proof: \,\,\,\,} & \\
   \Phi_\mathrm{xs}(\omega) & = \mathcal{F} \left \{ E[x(t)s(t+\tau)] \right \} \\
   & = \mathcal{F} \left \{ E[(s(t) + w(t))s(t+\tau)] \right \}\\
   & = \mathcal{F} \left \{ E[(s(t) + s(t+ \tau)] + E[s(t+\tau) + w(t)] \right \}\\
   & = \Phi_\mathrm{ss}(\omega)
\end{aligned}
\]


\bigskip
Now,

\[
\begin{aligned}
    H(\omega)   & = \frac{1+j\omega}{2+j \omega} \left [ \frac {\frac{3}{1+ \omega^2}}{\frac{2-j \omega}{1-j \omega}} \right ]^+\\
            & = \frac{1+j\omega}{2+j \omega} \left [ \frac{3}{(2-j \omega)(1 + j \omega)} \right ]^+\\
            \text{partial fraction expansion} & \\
            & = \frac{1+j\omega}{2+j \omega} \left [ \underbrace{\frac{1}{2 - j \omega}}_{\text{BHP}} + \underbrace{\frac{1}{1 + j \omega}}_{\text{THP}} \right ] ^+\\
            & = \left (\frac{1+j\omega}{2+j \omega} \right ) \left (\frac{1}{1+j\omega} \right ) \\
            & = \frac{1}{2+j \omega}
\end{aligned}
\]

Therefore, the causal Wiener filter is 
\[
\begin{aligned}
H_o(\omega) & = \frac{1}{2+j \omega}  \\
h_o(t) & = e^{-2t} u(t)
\end{aligned}
\]
Which is a first-order lowpass filter, with an impulse function shown in Figure \ref{fig:LPF_impulse}.
\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.3]{figures/LPF_impulse.png}
\caption{The impulse response of the optimal Wiener filter.}
\label{fig:LPF_impulse}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Evaluating the Error for a Wiener Filter}

Consider the mean square error:
\[
\begin{aligned}
    E[e^2(t)] &= E[\{z(t) - y(t) \}^2 ]\\
        & = \phi_\mathrm{zz}(0) + \phi_\mathrm{yy}(0) + 2\phi_\mathrm{zy}(0) \\
\end{aligned}
\]

But we know that 
\[
\begin{aligned}
\phi_\mathrm{yy}(0) & = \int \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau - u -\nu) h(u) h(\nu) \, du \, d \nu |_{\tau = 0} \\
    & = \int \int \phi_\mathrm{xx}(u -\nu) h(u) h(\nu) \, du \, d \nu \\
    & = \int \phi{_\mathrm{xy}}(u) h(u)\, du
\end{aligned}
\]
and also that 

\[
\begin{aligned}
\phi_\mathrm{zy}(\tau) & = \phi_\mathrm{zx}(\tau)* h(\tau)\\
        & = \int \phi_\mathrm{zx}(\tau - u) h(u) \, du \\
\phi_\mathrm{zy}(0) & = \int \phi_\mathrm{zx}(- u) h(u) \, du\\
        & = \int \phi_\mathrm{xz}(u) h(u) 
\end{aligned}
\]

Substituting these results:
\begin{align*}
E[e^2(t)] & = \phi_\mathrm{zz}(0)
              + \int\!\!\int \phi_\mathrm{xx}(u-\nu)\, h(u)\, h(\nu)\, du\, d\nu \\
           & \qquad - 2 \int \phi_\mathrm{xz}(u)\, h(u)\, du \\
           & = \phi_\mathrm{zz}(0)
              + \int h(u) \left[\int \phi_\mathrm{xx}(u-\nu)\, h(\nu)\, d\nu \right] du \\
           & \qquad - 2 \int \phi_\mathrm{xz}(u)\, h(u)\, du \\
           & = \phi_\mathrm{zz}(0)
              + \int \phi_\mathrm{xy}(u)\, h(u)\, du
              - 2 \int \phi_\mathrm{xz}(u)\, h(u)\, du \\
\intertext{\quad since $h(t) = h_0(t)$, then
           $\phi_\mathrm{xz}(\tau) = \phi_\mathrm{xy}(\tau)$, and}
           & = \phi_\mathrm{zz}(0)
              + \int \phi_\mathrm{xz}(u)\, h(u)\, du
              - 2 \int \phi_\mathrm{xz}(u)\, h(u)\, du \\
           & = \phi_\mathrm{zz}(0) - \int \phi_\mathrm{xz}(u)\, h(u)\, du \\
           & = \phi_\mathrm{zz}(0)
              - \int\!\!\int \phi_\mathrm{xx}(u-\nu)\, h(u)\, h(\nu)\, du\, d\nu \\
           & = \phi_\mathrm{zz}(0)
              - \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) * h(\tau) * h(-\tau)\big|_{\tau=0}
\end{align*}

Taking the Fourier transform:

\[
\boxed{\text{MSE} = \frac{1}{2 \pi} \int_{-\infty}^\infty \Phi_\mathrm{zz}(\omega) - \Phi_\mathrm{xx}(\omega)|H_o(\omega)|^2  \, d \omega}
\]

\bigskip

\begin{tcolorbox}
\begin{exmp}{Continue with the previous example and evaluate the MSE.}
\end{exmp}
\end{tcolorbox}

\[
H_o(\omega) = \frac{1}{2+j \omega}, \quad \Phi_\mathrm{xx}(\omega) = \frac{4+\omega^2}{1+\omega^2}, \quad \Phi_\mathrm{zz}(\omega) = \Phi_\mathrm{ss}(\omega) = \frac{3}{1 + \omega^2}
\]

\[
\begin{aligned}    
\text{MSE} & = \frac{1}{2 \pi} \int_{-\infty}^\infty \left (\frac{3}{1 + \omega^2} \right )  - \left ( \frac{4+\omega^2}{1+\omega^2} \right) \left | \frac{1}{2+j \omega} \right |^2  \, d \omega \\
        & = \frac{1}{2 \pi} \int_{-\infty}^\infty \left (\frac{3}{1 + \omega^2} \right )  - \left ( \frac{4+\omega^2}{1+\omega^2} \right) \left | \frac{1}{4 + \omega^2} \right |  \, d \omega \\
        & = \frac{1}{2 \pi} \int_{-\infty}^\infty \frac{1}{1 + \omega^2 }\, d \omega\\
        & = 1
\end{aligned}
\]

Therefore, the \emph{percentage} error is the MSE normalized by the energy (mean square value) in the signal:
\[
\begin{aligned}
    \phi_\mathrm{ss}(0) & = \mathcal{F}^{-1}{\Phi_\mathrm{ss}}(\omega)|_{\tau=0}\\
            & = \frac{1}{2 \pi} \int_{-\infty}^\infty \frac{3}{1 + \omega^2} \, d \omega \\
            & = \frac{3}{2}
\end{aligned}
\]

Therefore:
\[
\frac{\text{MSE}}{\phi_\mathrm{ss}(0)} = \frac{2}{3} = 66\%
\]

\bigskip
For the non-causal filter:

\[
H_o(\omega) = \frac{\Phi_\mathrm{xz}(\omega)}{\Phi_\mathrm{xx}(\omega)} = \frac{3}{4 + \omega^2}
\]

\[
h_o(t) = \mathcal{F}^{-1} \left \{ \frac{3}{4 + \omega^2} \right \} = \mathcal{F}^{-1} \left \{ \frac{3/4}{2 + j\omega} + \frac{3/4}{2 - j\omega} \right \} = \frac{3}{4}e^{-2|t|}
\]

\[
\begin{aligned}    
\text{MSE} & = \frac{1}{2 \pi} \int_{-\infty}^\infty \left (\frac{3}{1 + \omega^2} \right )  - \left ( \frac{4+\omega^2}{1+\omega^2} \right) \left | \frac{3}{4 + \omega^2} \right |^2  \, d \omega \\
        & = \frac{3}{4}
\end{aligned}
\]

Therefore:
Therefore:
\[
\frac{\text{MSE}}{\phi_\mathrm{ss}(0)} = \frac{3}{4}\cdot \frac{2}{3} = \frac{1}{2}= 50\%
\]

This is an intuitive result, as the non-causal filter should always outperform a causal filter.

\bigskip

\subsection{The General Causal Case}
\label{sec:general-causal}

% ── Shorthand used throughout this section ─────────────────────────────────
% \providecommand is safe even if \F is already defined in the preamble.
\providecommand{\F}{\mathcal{F}}      % Fourier-transform operator

The general causal Wiener filter estimates a \emph{shifted} version of the
signal:
\[
  z(t) = s(t+\alpha), \qquad \alpha \in \mathbb{R}.
\]
The parameter $\alpha$ determines which of three classical estimation problems
is being solved, as illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:alpha_timeline}.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{alpha_timeline.png}
  \caption{The role of the shift parameter $\alpha$ in the causal Wiener
    filter.  When $\alpha=0$ the filter estimates the signal at the current
    instant (\emph{filtering}); when $\alpha<0$ the desired signal
    $z(t)=s(t+\alpha)$ lies in the past (\emph{smoothing}); when $\alpha>0$
    it lies in the future (\emph{prediction}).  In every case the filter
    $h(t)$ must be causal, i.e.\ $h(t)=0$ for $t<0$.}
  \label{fig:alpha_timeline}
\end{figure}

The general filter structure is shown in Figure~\ref{fig:general_block}.
The causal filter $h(t)$ processes the noisy observation $x(t)$ to produce
the estimate $y(t)\approx z(t)=s(t+\alpha)$; the error
$e(t)=z(t)-y(t)$ is minimised in the mean-square sense.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
  \centering
  \begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth, node distance=1.8cm, auto,
      block/.style={draw, rectangle, rounded corners=2pt,
                    minimum width=2.0cm, minimum height=0.9cm,
                    fill=boxbg, font=\small},
      sum/.style  ={draw, circle, minimum size=0.75cm, fill=boxbg},
      wire/.style ={->, thick}]

    % Nodes
    \node (src)  [font=\small]                    {$x(t)$};
    \node (H)    [block,  right=1.6cm of src]     {$H(\omega)$};
    \node (plus) [sum,    right=1.8cm of H]       {$+$};
    \node (out)  [font=\small, right=1.6cm of plus] {$e(t)$};
    \node (zref) [font=\small, above=1.1cm of plus] {$z(t)=s(t+\alpha)$};

    % Wires
    \draw[wire] (src) -- (H);
    \draw[wire] (H)   -- node[midway, above, font=\small] {$y(t)$} (plus);
    \draw[wire] (zref) -- node[right, font=\small] {$-$} (plus);
    \draw[wire] (plus) -- (out);

    % Annotation below filter block
    \node [below=0.15cm of H, font=\footnotesize, text=gray]
          {causal: $h(t)=0,\ t<0$};
  \end{tikzpicture}
  \caption{Block diagram of the general causal Wiener filter.
    The causal filter $H(\omega)$ processes the noisy observation
    $x(t)=s(t)+n(t)$ to form the estimate $y(t)$ of the desired signal
    $z(t)=s(t+\alpha)$.  The error $e(t)=z(t)-y(t)$ is minimised in the
    mean-square sense subject to the causality constraint $h(t)=0$ for
    $t<0$.}
  \label{fig:general_block}
\end{figure}

% ── Spectral setup ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────

\medskip\noindent
\textit{Continuing the running example} with
\[
  \Phi_{ss}(\omega) = \frac{3}{1+\omega^2}, \qquad
  \Phi_{nn}(\omega) = 1 \quad \text{(white noise)},
\]
the observed-data PSD and its spectral factors are
\[
  \Phi_{xx}(\omega)
    = \Phi_{ss}(\omega)+\Phi_{nn}(\omega)
    = \frac{4+\omega^2}{1+\omega^2}
    = \underbrace{\frac{2+j\omega}{1+j\omega}}_{\Phi_{xx}^+(\omega)}
      \underbrace{\frac{2-j\omega}{1-j\omega}}_{\Phi_{xx}^-(\omega)}.
\]

The cross-spectrum between the observation and the desired signal is
\begin{align*}
  \phi_{xz}(\tau)
    &= E\!\bigl[x(t)\,z(t+\tau)\bigr]
     = E\!\bigl[(s(t)+n(t))\,s(t+\tau+\alpha)\bigr]
     = \phi_{ss}(\tau+\alpha),\\[4pt]
  \Phi_{xz}(\omega)
    &= \F\!\bigl[\phi_{ss}(\tau+\alpha)\bigr]
     = \Phi_{ss}(\omega)\,e^{j\omega\alpha}
     = \frac{3\,e^{j\omega\alpha}}{1+\omega^2}.
\end{align*}

The causal Wiener--Hopf solution is therefore
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{H(\omega)
    = \frac{1}{\Phi_{xx}^{+}(\omega)}
      \left[\frac{\Phi_{xz}(\omega)}{\Phi_{xx}^{-}(\omega)}\right]^{\!+}}
  \label{eq:wiener-hopf}
\end{equation}
where $[\,\cdot\,]^+$ denotes the \emph{causal part} (inverse Fourier
transform, truncate to $t\ge 0$, then transform back).

Substituting the spectral factors:
\[
  \frac{\Phi_{xz}(\omega)}{\Phi_{xx}^{-}(\omega)}
    = \frac{3\,e^{j\omega\alpha}}{1+\omega^2}\cdot\frac{1-j\omega}{2-j\omega}
    = \frac{3\,e^{j\omega\alpha}}{(1+j\omega)(2-j\omega)}.
\]

There are three distinct cases depending on the sign of $\alpha$, treated below.

% ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
\subsection*{Case 1: $\alpha = 0$ \quad (Filtering Problem)}
\label{ssec:filtering}

When $\alpha=0$ the desired signal is the \emph{current} signal value,
$z(t)=s(t)$.  The causal-part operation is straightforward because no shift
complicates the partial-fraction structure.  A partial-fraction expansion gives
\[
  \frac{\Phi_{xz}(\omega)}{\Phi_{xx}^{-}(\omega)}\bigg|_{\alpha=0}
  = \frac{3}{(1+j\omega)(2-j\omega)}
  = \underbrace{\frac{1}{2-j\omega}}_{\text{anti-causal (BHP)}}
    +\underbrace{\frac{1}{1+j\omega}}_{\text{causal (THP)}},
\]
so $[\cdots]^+ = 1/(1+j\omega)$, and from~\eqref{eq:wiener-hopf}:
\[
  H_o(\omega) = \frac{1+j\omega}{2+j\omega}\cdot\frac{1}{1+j\omega}
              = \frac{1}{2+j\omega}
  \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
  h_o(t) = e^{-2t}\,u(t).
\]
This is a single-pole low-pass filter: it attenuates high-frequency noise
while passing the signal.  The impulse response is shown in
Figure~\ref{fig:case1_impulse}.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
  \centering
  \begin{tikzpicture}[xscale=1.6, yscale=1.4]
    % Axes
    \draw[->] (-0.3,0) -- (3.0,0) node[right] {$t$};
    \draw[->] (0,-0.12) -- (0,1.35) node[above] {$h_o(t)$};
    % Curve  h(t) = exp(-2t), t >= 0
    \draw[thick, color=accent, domain=0:2.6, samples=100]
          plot (\x, {exp(-2*\x)});
    % Initial value marker
    \filldraw[accent] (0,1) circle (2pt);
    \draw[dashed, gray] (0,1) node[left, black, font=\small] {$1$} -- (0.05,1);
    % Time-constant marker at t = 0.5 (time constant = 1/2)
    \draw[dashed, gray] (0.5, 0) node[below, font=\footnotesize] {$\tfrac{1}{2}$}
                                 -- (0.5, {exp(-1)});
    \draw[dashed, gray] (0, {exp(-1)}) node[left, font=\footnotesize] {$e^{-1}$}
                                       -- (0.5, {exp(-1)});
    % Label
    \node[accent, font=\small, right] at (1.1, 0.35) {$h_o(t)=e^{-2t}u(t)$};
    % t<0 region: h=0
    \draw[thick, color=accent] (-0.28,0) -- (0,0);
    \filldraw[accent] (0,1) circle (2pt);
    \filldraw[white]  (0,0) circle (2pt);
    \draw[accent,thick] (0,0) circle (2pt);
  \end{tikzpicture}
  \caption{Case~1 ($\alpha=0$, filtering): impulse response of the optimal
    causal Wiener filter $H_o(\omega)=1/(2+j\omega)$.  The filter is a
    first-order low-pass system with time constant $\tau=1/2$~s and unity
    gain at $\omega=0$.  For $t<0$, $h_o(t)=0$ (causality).}
  \label{fig:case1_impulse}
\end{figure}

% ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
\subsection*{Case 2: $\alpha < 0$ \quad (Smoothing Problem)}
\label{ssec:smoothing}

When $\alpha<0$ the desired signal $z(t)=s(t+\alpha)=s(t-|\alpha|)$ is a
\emph{past} value of the signal.  Even though the signal of interest lies in
the past, the causality constraint $h(t)=0,\ t<0$ still applies to the
filter — only past values of the observation $x(\cdot)$ may be used.

The exponential shift $e^{j\omega\alpha}$ prevents an immediate partial-fraction
factorisation, so the causal-part operation is performed in the time domain.
Using the inverse transform of $3/[(1+j\omega)(2-j\omega)]$:
\[
  \F^{-1}\!\left\{\frac{3}{(1+j\omega)(2-j\omega)}\right\}
  = e^{-t}u(t)+e^{2t}u(-t),
\]
delaying by $|\alpha|$ (i.e.\ replacing $t$ by $t+\alpha$) and retaining only
$t\ge 0$:

\begin{align}
  \left[\frac{\Phi_{xz}(\omega)}{\Phi_{xx}^{-}(\omega)}\right]^{\!+}
  &= \int_0^{-\alpha}e^{2(t+\alpha)}\,e^{-j\omega t}\,dt
   + \int_{-\alpha}^{\infty}e^{-(t+\alpha)}\,e^{-j\omega t}\,dt \notag\\[4pt]
  &= \frac{3\,e^{j\omega\alpha}}{(2-j\omega)(1+j\omega)}
   - \frac{e^{2\alpha}}{2-j\omega}.
   \label{eq:causal-part-smooth}
\end{align}

Substituting into~\eqref{eq:wiener-hopf}:
\begin{align}
  H_o(\omega)
  &= \frac{1+j\omega}{2+j\omega}\left[
       \frac{3\,e^{j\omega\alpha}}{(1+j\omega)(2-j\omega)}
      -\frac{e^{2\alpha}}{2-j\omega}\right] \notag\\[4pt]
  &= \frac{3\,e^{j\omega\alpha}}{4+\omega^2}
   - \frac{(1+j\omega)\,e^{2\alpha}}{4+\omega^2}.
   \label{eq:Ho-smooth}
\end{align}

Inverting~\eqref{eq:Ho-smooth} via standard Fourier-transform pairs yields the
time-domain impulse response:
\begin{equation}
  \boxed{h_o(t)
    = \frac{e^{-2|t+\alpha|}}{4}
      \Bigl[3 - e^{2\alpha} + 2\,e^{2\alpha}\operatorname{sgn}(t+\alpha)\Bigr]\,u(t)}
  \label{eq:ho-smooth-time}
\end{equation}
The structure of~\eqref{eq:ho-smooth-time} reflects the smoothing geometry:
for $0\le t < -\alpha$ (i.e.\ $t+\alpha<0$) the filter weight grows as
$e^{2(t+\alpha)}$, reaching a peak at $t=-\alpha$, then decays as
$e^{-(t+\alpha)}$ for $t>-\alpha$.  A jump discontinuity of magnitude
$e^{2\alpha}$ occurs at $t=-\alpha$ as a result of the causality constraint.

\medskip\noindent
\textbf{Sanity checks.}
\begin{itemize}
  \item $\alpha=0$: $h_o(t)=(e^{-2|t|}/4)[3-1+2\operatorname{sgn}(t)]
        = e^{-2t}u(t)$, consistent with Case~1. \checkmark
  \item $\alpha\to-\infty$: $e^{2\alpha}\to 0$, so
        $h_o(t)\to\tfrac{3}{4}e^{-2|t+\alpha|}$ — the bilateral
        (non-causal) filter shifted by $|\alpha|$ seconds, which becomes
        entirely causal as $|\alpha|\to\infty$. \checkmark
\end{itemize}

The impulse responses for three smoothing delays are shown in
Figure~\ref{fig:smoothing_impulse}.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{smoothing_wiener.png}
  \caption{Case~2 (smoothing, $\alpha<0$): impulse response
    $h_o(t)$ given by~\eqref{eq:ho-smooth-time} for three smoothing delays
    $\alpha\in\{-0.5,-1,-2\}$.
    Each response is zero for $t<0$ (causality) and peaks at $t=-\alpha$.
    The left branch ($0\le t < -\alpha$) grows as $e^{2(t+\alpha)}$; the
    right branch ($t>-\alpha$) decays as $e^{-(t+\alpha)}$.  Open circles
    mark the left-hand limit at the peak; filled circles mark the right-hand
    (adopted) value.  The jump $\Delta=e^{2\alpha}$ diminishes as the delay
    $|\alpha|$ increases, and as $\alpha\to-\infty$ the response converges
    to the non-causal bilateral exponential $\tfrac{3}{4}e^{-2|t+\alpha|}$.}
  \label{fig:smoothing_impulse}
\end{figure}

% ── Limit: infinite smoothing ──────────────────────────────────────────────
\subsubsection*{Limit: infinite smoothing delay}

As $\alpha\to-\infty$, equation~\eqref{eq:Ho-smooth} gives
\[
  H_o(\omega) \;\xrightarrow{\alpha\to-\infty}\; \frac{3\,e^{j\omega\alpha}}{4+\omega^2}
  \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
  h_o(t) = \tfrac{3}{4}\,e^{-2|t+\alpha|}.
\]
This is the \emph{non-causal} bilateral Wiener filter (Section~6.4.4)
shifted $|\alpha|$ seconds into the future.  Because every point of the
bilateral filter eventually falls in the causal half-line as
$|\alpha|\to\infty$, the smoother asymptotically achieves the non-causal MSE
performance.  The conclusion is:

\begin{quote}
  \itshape Introducing a sufficiently long smoothing delay allows a causal
  filter to approach the performance of an ideal non-causal filter.
\end{quote}

% ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
\subsection*{Case 3: $\alpha > 0$ \quad (Prediction Problem)}
\label{ssec:prediction}

When $\alpha>0$ the desired signal $z(t)=s(t+\alpha)$ is a \emph{future}
value of the signal.  The prediction geometry is illustrated in the left
panel of Figure~\ref{fig:prediction}.

The shifted time-domain function is $e^{-(t+\alpha)}u(t+\alpha)$; for $\alpha>0$
this function is positive for all $t>-\alpha$, so the portion with $t\ge 0$
is entirely contained in the causal half-line:
\[
  \left[\frac{\Phi_{xz}(\omega)}{\Phi_{xx}^{-}(\omega)}\right]^{\!+}
  = \F\!\Bigl[\bigl\{e^{-(t+\alpha)}\,u(t+\alpha)\bigr\}u(t)\Bigr]
  = \F\!\Bigl[e^{-\alpha}\,e^{-t}\,u(t)\Bigr]
  = \frac{e^{-\alpha}}{1+j\omega}.
\]

Substituting into~\eqref{eq:wiener-hopf}:
\[
  H_o(\omega)
    = \frac{1+j\omega}{2+j\omega}\cdot\frac{e^{-\alpha}}{1+j\omega}
    = \frac{e^{-\alpha}}{2+j\omega}
  \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
  \boxed{h_o(t) = e^{-\alpha}\,e^{-2t}\,u(t)}.
\]

The predictor has the same \emph{shape} as the filtering solution (Case~1)
but with its gain scaled down by the factor $e^{-\alpha}<1$.  This is
equivalent to running the filtering solution and attenuating the output:

\begin{center}
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth, node distance=2.4cm,
    block/.style={draw, rectangle, rounded corners=2pt,
                  minimum width=2.6cm, minimum height=0.9cm,
                  fill=boxbg, font=\small, align=center}]
  \node (in)   [font=\small] {$x(t)$};
  \node (filt) [block, right=of in]
               {$h(t)=e^{-2t}u(t)$\\[1pt]\footnotesize Causal filter\\[-2pt]\footnotesize ($\alpha=0$)};
  \node (gain) [block, right=of filt]
               {$\times\,e^{-\alpha}$\\[1pt]\footnotesize Prediction\\[-2pt]\footnotesize gain};
  \node (out)  [font=\small, right=of gain] {$\hat{s}(t+\alpha)$};
  \draw[->, thick] (in)   -- (filt);
  \draw[->, thick] (filt) -- (gain);
  \draw[->, thick] (gain) -- (out);
\end{tikzpicture}
\end{center}

The impulse responses for several prediction horizons $\alpha$ are shown in
the right panel of Figure~\ref{fig:prediction}.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{prediction_shift_diagram.png}
  \caption{Case~3 (prediction, $\alpha>0$).
    \emph{Left:} Timeline of the prediction problem.  At current time $t$
    only the shaded (past) observations $x(\tau),\ \tau\le t$ are available;
    the goal is to estimate the signal $s(t+\alpha)$ that will occur $\alpha$
    seconds in the future (red star).
    \emph{Right:} Impulse responses $h_o(t)=e^{-\alpha}e^{-2t}u(t)$ for
    $\alpha\in\{0,\,0.5,\,1.0,\,2.0\}$.  All responses share the same
    exponential decay shape but the initial amplitude is scaled by
    $e^{-\alpha}$: as the prediction horizon grows the filter trusts the
    observation less and its output tends to zero.  As
    $\alpha\to\infty,\;e^{-\alpha}\to 0$ and $\hat{s}(t+\alpha)\to 0$,
    consistent with the fact that the AR(1)-type process $s(t)$ becomes
    unpredictable over long horizons.}
  \label{fig:prediction}
\end{figure}

% ════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
\subsection*{Summary: All Three Cases}

Figure~\ref{fig:three_cases} collects the impulse responses for a
representative value of $|\alpha|=1$ in each case.

\begin{figure}[htbp]
  \centering
  \includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{wiener_three_cases.png}
  \caption{Comparison of the optimal causal Wiener filter impulse responses
    for the three estimation problems (same signal and noise spectra in each
    case).
    \emph{Left} (filtering, $\alpha=0$): purely decaying exponential
    $h_o(t)=e^{-2t}u(t)$ starting at unity.
    \emph{Centre} (smoothing, $\alpha=-1$): two-sided exponential starting
    at a reduced amplitude, growing to its peak at $t=1=-\alpha$, then
    decaying; the jump discontinuity at the peak is $\Delta=e^{2\alpha}$.
    \emph{Right} (prediction, $\alpha=1$): purely decaying exponential with
    reduced initial amplitude $e^{-\alpha}=e^{-1}\approx 0.368$.
    In all cases the filter is causal ($h_o(t)=0$ for $t<0$).}
  \label{fig:three_cases}
\end{figure}

The key qualitative conclusions are:
\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Filtering} ($\alpha=0$): the filter optimally trades off
        signal preservation against noise rejection via a first-order
        low-pass structure.

  \item \textbf{Smoothing} ($\alpha<0$): the delay $|\alpha|$ allows the
        filter to ``look ahead'' in its buffer; it uses data \emph{after}
        the desired time instant (but still in its past relative to the
        output) to form a better estimate.  Performance approaches the
        non-causal limit as $|\alpha|\to\infty$.

  \item \textbf{Prediction} ($\alpha>0$): the filter shape is identical to
        the filtering case, but the overall gain decreases exponentially with
        horizon $\alpha$.  For a process with finite spectral bandwidth,
        long-range prediction is inherently limited.
\end{itemize}



% ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════



\subsection{The Discrete Wiener Filter}
The discrete Wiener filter is realized as a finite impulse response (FIR) filter. The filter tap weights are optimized using the discrete realization of the Wiener-Hopf equation, evaluated using time-series estimate of the input autocorrelation and input/output cross-correlation functions. \\

Consider some observed data \( \mathbf{x} = \{x(1), x(2), \ldots x(N)\} \) consisting of a  discrete signal \(\mathbf{s} = \{s(1), s(2), \ldots s(n) \} \) corrupted by additive white noise \(\mathbf{w} = \{w(1), w(2), \ldots w(n) \} \). For convenience, we will use the symbols \(\mathbf{x} = x(n)\), \(\mathbf{s} = s(n)\), \(\mathbf{w} = w(n)\) and \(\mathbf{z} = z(n)\) interchangeably.\\


As with the continuous Wiener the desired signal \(z(n)\) is the signal \(s(n)\), as illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:Noise_cancel_discrete}. \\

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.6
]{figures/Noise_cancel_discrete.png}
\caption{The discrete Wiener filter.}
\label{fig:Noise_cancel_discrete}
\end{figure}

Since the noise is white, it is uncorrelated with the signal:
\[
E[s(n)w(n)] = 0
\]

The output of the filter is:
\[
y(n) = \hat{s}(n) = \sum_{k = 0}^\infty h(k)x(n-k)
\]


Now, consider the orthogonality principle:
\[
\begin{aligned}
    E[e(n) x(n-i)] & = 0 \quad \forall i \ge 0,\\
    E \left [\left \{ s(n) - \sum_{k=0}^\infty h(k) x(n-k) \right \} x(n-i) \right ] & = 0 \quad \forall i \ge 0,
\end{aligned}
\]

Therefore:
\[
\boxed{  \phi_\mathrm{xs}(i) - \sum_{k=0}^\infty h(k) \phi_\mathrm{xx}(i-k) = 0, \quad \forall i \ge 0}
\]

This is the \emph{discrete Wiener-Hopf equation}.\\


Now consider a finite data sequence \( \mathbf{x} = \{x(1), x(2), \ldots x(N)\} \). Using the finite data sequence means that the discrete Wiener-Hopf equation is an \emph{estimate}:
\[
\phi_\mathrm{xs}(i) - \sum_{k=0}^N h(k) \phi_\mathrm{xx}(i-k) \approx 0, \quad \forall i \ge 0
\]

In matrix form this is:
\[
\underbrace{\boldsymbol{\phi}_\mathrm{xs}}_{N\times 1} -   \underbrace{\boldsymbol{\phi}_\mathrm{xx}}_{N\times  N} \underbrace{\boldsymbol{h}^T(k)}_{N \times 1} =  \underbrace{\varnothing}_{\text{null}}
\]

Note that $\boldsymbol{\phi}_\mathrm{xx}$ is formed as a Toeplitz matrix. Let's expand the matrices:

\begin{equation*}
    \begin{bmatrix}
        \phi_\mathrm{xs}(0) \\
        \phi_\mathrm{xs}(1) \\
        \vdots \\
        \phi_\mathrm{xs}(N-1)
    \end{bmatrix}
    =
    \begin{bmatrix}
        \phi_\mathrm{xx}}(0) & \phi_\mathrm{xx}(1) & \cdots & \phi_\mathrm{xx}(N-1) \\
       \phi_\mathrm{xx}(1) & \phi_\mathrm{xx}(0) & \cdots & \phi_\mathrm{xx}(N-2)  \\
        \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\
        \phi_\mathrm{xx}(N-1) & \phi_\mathrm{xx}(N-2) & \cdots & \phi_\mathrm{xx}(0) \\
    \end{bmatrix}
    \begin{bmatrix}
        h(0)\\
        h(1)\\
        \vdots \\
        h(N-1)
    \end{bmatrix}
\end{equation*}

Note that what would normally be negative indices are positive, since \(\phi_\mathrm{xx}(i) \) is defined only for \(i\ge 0\). \\

Now, consider limiting the length of the correlations to \(M\) lags:  

\begin{equation*}
    \begin{bmatrix}
        \phi_\mathrm{xs}(0) \\
        \phi_\mathrm{xs}(1) \\
        \vdots \\
        \phi_\mathrm{xs}(M)
    \end{bmatrix}
    =
    \begin{bmatrix}
        \phi_\mathrm{xx}(0) & \phi_\mathrm{xx}(1) & \cdots & \phi_\mathrm{xx}(M) \\
       \phi_\mathrm{xx}(1) & \phi_\mathrm{xx}(0) & \cdots & \phi_\mathrm{xx}(M-1)  \\
        \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \vdots \\
        \phi_\mathrm{xx}(M) & \phi_\mathrm{xx}(M-1) & \cdots & \phi_\mathrm{xx}(0) \\
    \end{bmatrix}
    \begin{bmatrix}
        h(0)\\
        h(1)\\
        \vdots \\
        h(M)
    \end{bmatrix}
\end{equation*}

To solve this, we rearrange the discrete Wiener-Hopf equation:
\[
\boldsymbol{h}^T(k)= \boldsymbol{\phi}_\mathrm{xx}^{-1} \boldsymbol{\phi}_\mathrm{xs}
\]

\begin{tcolorbox}
    \begin{exmp}{Simple Example of a Discrete Wiener Filter.}
    
    Consider observed data corrupted by additive white noise:
    \[
    x(n) = s(n) + w(n)
    \]
    
    The signal's autocorrelation function is defined as
\[
\boldsymbol{\phi}_\mathrm{ss} = 
    \begin{bmatrix}
        \phi_\mathrm{ss}(0) \\
        \phi_\mathrm{ss}(1) \\
        \phi_\mathrm{ss}(2)
    \end{bmatrix}
    = 
    \begin{bmatrix}
        \rfrac{3}{4}\\
        \rfrac{1}{2}\\
        \rfrac{1}{4}
    \end{bmatrix}
\]
and \(\phi\mathrm{ss}(i) = 0 \) for \(i \ge 3\). The additive white noise has:

\[
\boldsymbol{\phi}_\mathrm{ww} = 
    = 
    \begin{bmatrix}
        \rfrac{1}{4}\\
        0\\
        0
    \end{bmatrix}
\]
and 0 otherwise.

    \end{exmp}

Find the discrete Wiener filter to recover \(s(n)\) from \(x(n)\).

\end{tcolorbox}

We'll assume that the signal is uncorrelated with the noise:
\[
E[s(n)w(k)] = E[s]E[w] = 0
\]

Therefore
\[
\boldsymbol{\phi}_\mathrm{xx} =  \boldsymbol{\phi}_\mathrm{ss}+ \boldsymbol{\phi}_\mathrm{ww} = 
    \begin{bmatrix}
        1\\
        \rfrac{1}{2}\\
        \rfrac{1}{4}
    \end{bmatrix}
\]
and
\[
\boldsymbol{\phi}_\mathrm{xs} =  \boldsymbol{\phi}_\mathrm{ss}
\]

\[
\boldsymbol{\phi}_\mathrm{xs} = \boldsymbol{\phi}_\mathrm{xx} \boldsymbol{h}^T(k)= 
\]

\begin{equation}
  \begin{bmatrix}
        \rfrac{3}{4}\\
        \rfrac{1}{2}\\
        \rfrac{1}{4}
    \end{bmatrix}
    =
    \begin{bmatrix}
        1 & \rfrac{1}{2} & \rfrac{1}{4} \\
       \rfrac{1}{2} & 1 & \rfrac{1}{2}  \\
        \rfrac{1}{4} & \rfrac{1}{2}  & 1 \\
    \end{bmatrix}
    \begin{bmatrix}
        h(0)\\
        h(1)\\
        h(2)
    \end{bmatrix}
\end{equation}

\[
\boldsymbol{h}^T(k)= \boldsymbol{\phi}_\mathrm{xx}^{-1} \boldsymbol{\phi}_\mathrm{xs} =      = 
       \begin{bmatrix}
        \rfrac{2}{3}\\
        \rfrac{1}{6}\\
        0
    \end{bmatrix}
\]

This is illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:Tapped_delay}.
\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.15]{figures/Tapped_delay2.png}
\caption{The tapped delay line representation of the resulting FIR Wiener filter.}
\label{fig:Tapped_delay}
\end{figure}


\begin{tcolorbox}
    \begin{exmp}{Recovery of a Noisy Sinusoid.} 
    
    Consider a sinuoisal signal of length \(N=1000\) corrupted by white Gaussian noise. Construct a discrete Wiener filter that will recover the sinusoid with the least mean square error.   

    \end{exmp}


 \end{tcolorbox}

The code \href{https://www.ece.unb.ca/kengleha/Textbook/Code/FIRWienerFilter.py}{\texttt{FIRWienerFilter.py}} implements this filter. The key components are:

\begin{itemize}
    \item Assign the length of the data window and the filter length.\\
    \texttt{\# Parameters\\
    N = 1000 \quad \# Number of samples\\
    M = 200  \quad \,   \# Filter order (one less than the matrix size)\\
    }
    \item Compute the autocorrelation matrix Rxx with dimensions 21x21\\
    \texttt{Rxx = toeplitz(np.correlate(x, x, mode='full')[:M+1])}
    \item Compute the cross-correlation vector Rxy\\
    \texttt{Rxy = np.correlate(x, y, mode='full')[:M+1]}
    \item Solve the Wiener-Hopf equations to find the filter coefficients\\
    \texttt{h = np.dot(pinv(Rxx), Rxy)}
    \item Apply the Wiener filter to the noisy signal\\
    \texttt{filtered\_signal = filtfilt(h, 1.0, y)}
\end{itemize}

The noisy sinusoid and the estimated signal using the Wiener filter are shown in \ref{fig:FIR_sinsusoid}.
\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.4]{figures/FIRSinusoid.png}
\caption{The noisy sinusoid and the estimated signal using the Wiener filter.}
\label{fig:FIR_sinsusoid}
\end{figure}



    
\subsection{Wiener Filters: Fixed Plant Component}
Consider the scenario where an LTI system exists in the system that cannot be modified (fixed plants). In this case, the Wiener filter be be optimized with regard to the output of the fixed plant, as illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:Fixed_plant}.

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.5]{figures/Fixed_plant.png}
\caption{Optimal filter design with a fixed plant $h_F(t)$.}
\label{fig:Fixed_plant}
\end{figure}

That is, given $h_F(t)$, find $h(t)$ such that the overall system is optimal.

\[
H_o(\omega) = \frac{1}{\Phi_\mathrm{x_1 x_1}^+(\omega) }\left [\frac{\Phi_\mathrm{zx_1}(\omega)}{\Phi_\mathrm{x_1 x_1}^-(\omega)} \right ]^+
\]

In terms of the input $X(t)$:
\[
\Phi_\mathrm{x_1 x_1}(\omega) = \Phi_\mathrm{xx}(\omega)\left | H_F(\omega) \right |^2
\]
\[
\Phi_\mathrm{z_1x_1}(\omega) = \Phi_{zx}(\omega) H_F^*(\omega)
\]

Therefore,

\[
H_o(\omega) = \frac{1}{\left [ \Phi_\mathrm{xx}(\omega)\left | H_F(\omega) \right |^2 \right ]^+ } \left [\frac{\Phi_\mathrm{zx}(\omega) H_F^*(\omega)}{\left [ \Phi_\mathrm{xx}(\omega)\left | H_F(\omega) \right |^2 \right ]^- }  \right ]^+
\]
\section{Kalman Filter}
Kalman filtering uses a system's dynamic model (e.g., physical laws of motion), known control inputs to that system, and multiple sequential measurements (such as from sensors) to form an estimate of the system's varying quantities (its state) that is better than the estimate obtained by using only one measurement alone. As such, it is a common sensor fusion and data fusion algorithm.\\


Imagine we have a car equipped with GPS and an inertial measurement unit (IMU) consisting of accelerometers and gyroscopes. The GPS provides periodic but somewhat noisy measurements of the car's position, while the IMU provides continuous but subject to drift measurements of the car's velocity and orientation.\\

Now, let's say we want to accurately estimate the car's position, velocity, and orientation at all times. We can use a Kalman filter to fuse the information from the GPS and IMU to obtain a more accurate estimate than using either sensor alone. \\

Here's how the Kalman filter would work in this scenario:

\begin{itemize}
    \item \textbf{System Model}: We define a dynamic model of the car's motion based on the laws of physics, taking into account factors like acceleration, velocity, and orientation changes. This model predicts how the car's state (position, velocity, orientation) evolves over time given control inputs such as throttle, steering angle, and brake.
    \item \textbf{Control Inputs}: We incorporate information about the control inputs to the car, such as throttle position and steering angle. These inputs affect the predicted state of the car according to the dynamic model.
    \item \textbf{Measurements:} We receive periodic GPS measurements of the car's position and potentially noisy continuous measurements from the IMU about its velocity and orientation.
    \item \textbf{Kalman Filtering:} We use the Kalman filter to combine the predictions from the dynamic model with the measurements from the GPS and IMU. The filter calculates a weighted average of the predicted state (based on the dynamic model) and the measured state (from the sensors), with the weights determined by the relative uncertainties of the predictions and measurements. This process provides an optimal estimate of the car's state that minimizes the mean squared error.
    \item \textbf{State Estimation:} The output of the Kalman filter is an estimate of the car's position, velocity, and orientation that is more accurate than using just the GPS or IMU alone. The filter continually updates this estimate as new measurements arrive, effectively filtering out noise and correcting for drift in the IMU measurements.
\end{itemize}


By fusing information from multiple sensors using the Kalman filter, we obtain a more reliable and accurate estimate of the car's state.\\

The Kalman filter produces an estimate of the state of the system as an average of the system's predicted state and of the new measurement using a weighted average. The purpose of the weights is that values with better (i.e., smaller) estimated uncertainty are "trusted" more. This process is repeated at every time step, with the new estimate and its covariance informing the prediction used in the following iteration. This means that Kalman filter works recursively and requires only the last "best guess", rather than the entire history, of a system's state to calculate a new state.\\


\subsection{The System Dynamic Model}
Kalman filtering is based on linear dynamic systems discretized in the time domain. The state of the target system refers to the ground truth (yet hidden) system configuration of interest, which is represented as a vector of real numbers. At each discrete time increment, a linear operator is applied to the state to generate the new state, with some noise mixed in, and optionally some information from the controls on the system. Then, another linear operator mixed with more noise generates the measurable outputs (i.e., observation) from the true ("hidden") state.\\ 

In order to use the Kalman filter to estimate the internal state \(\mathbf{x}_k\) of a process at time step \(k\) given only a sequence of noisy observations \(\{\mathbf{z}_k, \mathbf{z}_{k-1}\ldots \}\), one must model the process in accordance with the following framework. This means specifying the matrices, for each time-step k:

\begin{itemize}
    \item $F_k$: the state-transition model;
    \item $H_k$: the observation model;
    \item $Q_k$: the covariance of the process noise;
    \item $R_k$: the covariance of the observation noise;
    \item Sometimes $B_k$: the control-input model as described below; if $B_k$ is included, then there is also
    \item $u_k$: the control vector, representing the controlling input into the control-input model.
\end{itemize}

This model is illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:Kalman_model}.\\

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.7]{figures/Kalman_filter_model_2.png}
\caption{The model underlying the Kalman filter. Squares represent matrices. Ellipses represent multivariate normal distributions (with the mean and covariance matrix enclosed). Unenclosed values are vectors.}
\label{fig:Kalman_model}
\end{figure}


The Kalman filter model assumes the true state at time $k$ is evolved from the state at $(k - 1)$ according to:
\[
\mathbf{x}_k = \mathbf{F}_k \mathbf{x}_{k-1} + \mathbf{B}_k \mathbf{u}_k + \mathbf{w}_k
\]
where:
\begin{itemize}
    \item $\mathbf{F}_k$: the state transition model applied to the previous state $\mathbf{x}_{k-1}$;
    \item $\mathbf{B}_k$ is the control-input model which is applied to the control vector $\mathbf{u}_k$;
    \item $\mathbf{w}_k$ is the process noise, which is assumed to be drawn from a zero-mean multivariate normal distribution with zero mean and covariance $\mathbf{Q}_k$. That is: $\mathbf{w}_k \sim \mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{Q}_k)$.
\end{itemize}


At time $k$, an observation (or measurement) $\mathbf{z}_k$ of the true state $\mathbf{x}_k$ is made. The relationship between the measurement and the true state space is: 

\[
\mathbf{z}_k = \mathbf{H}_k \mathbf{x}_k + \mathbf{v}_k
\]

where
\begin{itemize}
    \item $\mathbf{H}_k$ is the observation model, which maps the true state space into the observed space; and
    \item $\mathbf{v}_k$ is the observation noise, which is assumed to be zero-mean Gaussian white noise with covariance $\mathbf{R}_k$. That is: $\mathbf{v}_k \sim \mathcal{N}(0,\mathbf{R}_k)$.

\end{itemize}
The initial state, and the noise vectors at each step $\{\mathbf{x}_0, \mathbf{w}_1, \ldots, \mathbf{w}_k, \mathbf{v}_1, \ldots, \mathbf{v}_k\}$ are all assumed to be mutually independent.

\subsection{Formulation}
The Kalman filter is a recursive estimator. This means that only the estimated state from the previous time step and the current measurement are needed to compute the estimate for the current state. In contrast to batch estimation techniques, no history of observations and/or estimates is required. In what follows, the notation $\hat{\mathbf{x}}_{n\mid m}$ represents the estimate of $\mathbf{x}$ at time $n$ given observations up to and including time $m \leq n$.

The state of the filter is represented by two variables:
\begin{itemize}
    \item $\mathbf{x}_{k\mid k}$, the \emph{a posteriori} state estimate mean at time $k$ given observations up to and including time $k$;
    \item $\mathbf{P}_{k\mid k}$, the \emph{a posteriori} state estimate covariance matrix (a measure of the estimated accuracy of the state estimate).

\end{itemize}

The Kalman filter  is most often conceptualized as two distinct phases: `Predict' and `Update'.

\begin{itemize}
    \item The \textbf{predict phase} uses the state estimate from the previous timestep \(k-1\) to produce an estimate of the state at the current timestep \(k\): $\mathbf{\hat{x}}_{k\mid k-1}$. This predicted state estimate is also known as the \emph{a priori} state estimate because, although it is an estimate of the state at the current timestep, it does \emph{not} include observation information from the current timestep ($\mathbf{z}_{k}$).
    \item In the \textbf{update phase}, the \emph{innovation} is computed. This is the difference between the current \emph{a priori} prediction of the observation and the current observation: \(\mathbf{\Tilde{y}}_k = \mathbf{z_k} - \mathbf{H_k}\mathbf{\hat{x}}_{k\mid k-1}\). This is multiplied by the optimal Kalman gain $\mathbf{K}_k$ and combined with the previous state estimate to refine the state estimate: $\mathbf{x}_{k\mid k} = \mathbf{\hat{x}}_{k\mid k-1} + \mathbf{K}_k \mathbf{\Tilde{y}}_k$. This improved estimate based on the current observation is termed the \emph{a posteriori} state estimate.
\end{itemize} 

Typically, the two phases alternate, with the prediction advancing the state until the next scheduled observation, and the update incorporating the observation. \\

The complete set up update and predict equations are as follows: \\

\begin{tabular}{ll}
\textbf{Predict} & \\
Predicted (\emph{a priori}) state estimate      & $\mathbf{\hat{x}}_{k\mid k-1}  = \mathbf{F}_k \mathbf{{x}}_{k-1\mid k-1} + \mathbf{B}_k \mathbf{u}_k$\\
Predicted (\emph{a priori}) estimate covariance     & $\mathbf{\hat{P}}_{k\mid k-1} =  \mathbf{F}_k \mathbf{{P}}_{k-1\mid k-1} \mathbf{F}^{\text{T}}_k + \mathbf{Q}_k$\\
    &   \\
\textbf{Update} & \\   
{Innovation} & \(\mathbf{\Tilde{y}}_k = \mathbf{z_k} - \mathbf{H_k}\mathbf{\hat{x}}_{k\mid k-1}\) \\
{Innovation covariance} & $\mathbf{S}_k = \mathbf{H_k}\mathbf{\hat{P}}_{k\mid k-1} \mathbf{H}^{\text{T}}_k + \mathbf{R}_k$\\
{Optimal Kalman gain} & $\mathbf{K}_k = \mathbf{\hat{P}}_{k\mid k-1} \mathbf{H}^{\text{T}}_k \mathbf{S}^{-1}_k$\\
{Updated (\emph{a posteriori}) state estimate} & $\mathbf{\hat{x}}_{k\mid k}  = \mathbf{\hat{x}}_{k\mid k-1} + \mathbf{K}_k \mathbf{\Tilde{y}}_k$\\
{Updated (\emph{a posteriori}) estimate covariance} & $\mathbf{P}_{k\mid k} = (\mathbf{I - \mathbf{K}_k \mathbf{H}_k)}\mathbf{\hat{P}}_{k\mid k-1}$\\
{Measurement post-fit residual} & $\mathbf{\Tilde {y}}_{k \mid k} =  \mathbf{z}_k - \mathbf{H}_k \mathbf{\Tilde{x}}_{k \mid k }$
\end{tabular}


\bigskip
The formula for the the optimal Kalman gain \(\mathbf{K}_k \) is that which minimizes the residual error \(\Tilde{y}_{k \mid k}\). Derivation of the optimal Kalman gain and the \emph{a posteriori} estimate covariance matrix is provided in Appendix A.\\

An alternative (intuitive) way to express the updated state estimate (\(\mathbf{x}_{k\mid k}\)) is:

\[
\mathbf{x}_{k\mid k}  = ( \mathbf{I} - \mathbf{K}_k \mathbf{H}_k ) \mathbf{\hat{x}}_{k\mid k-1} + \mathbf{K}_k \mathbf{z}_k
\]

This expression reminds us of a linear interpolation, \(y = (1-\alpha) a + \alpha b \), for \(\alpha\) between 0 and 1. In our case:

\begin{itemize}

    \item \(a \rightarrow \mathbf{\hat{x}}_{k\mid k-1}\), the internal state estimated from the model.
    \item \(b \rightarrow \mathbf{H}^{^1}_k \mathbf{z}_k\) is the internal state estimated from the measurement.
    \item \(\alpha \rightarrow \mathbf{K}_k \mathbf{H}_k\) can range from 0 (high error in the measurement) \(\mathbf{z}_k\) to 1 (low error).
\end{itemize}

Therefore, the Kalman gain (proportional to \(\alpha \)) can be seen to provide a weighted average of the model and the measurement, depending on their relative uncertainty (error).


\bigskip

\begin{tcolorbox}
    \begin{exmp}{Intuitive Illustration of  Kalman Update and Predict.}

    By definition, the values (and distribution) of the state \(\mathbf{x}\) are hidden (and thus unobservable). If we assume that the state and the measurements are normally distributed, we can illustrate the influence of the predict and update stages for a single step.  We show the effect of sensor measurement that has low and high variance (uncertainty), relative to the uncertainty in the state model. Note that for this example, there is no control signal \(\mathbf{u}_k\).
        
    \end{exmp}
\end{tcolorbox}

We consider a system with the prior state \(\mathbf{x}_{k-1 \mid k-1} \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_{p},\sigma_p)\) We will assume that the state transition model \(\mathbf{F}_k\) is a constant velocity (per step) with inherent noise \( \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_{w},\sigma_w) \). This yields a  predicted or \emph{a priori} state estimate \( \mathbf{\hat{x}}_{k\mid k-1} \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_{ap},\sigma_{ap})\) with 

\[
\begin{aligned}
   \mu_{ap} & = \mu_p + \mu_w \\
   \sigma_{ap} & = \sqrt{\sigma_p^2 + \sigma_w^2}
\end{aligned}
\]

Next, the measurement \(\mathbf{z}_k 
 \sim \mathcal{N}(\mu_z,\sigma_z)\) is used to update the state estimate via the product of the innovation and the Kalman gain: $\mathbf{\hat{x}}_{k\mid k}  = \mathbf{\hat{x}}_{k\mid k-1} + \mathbf{K}_k \mathbf{\Tilde{y}}_k$.\\

Recall that the Kalman gain can range from 0 (high error in the measurement, relative to the process)  to 1 (low error).
 Figure \ref{fig:Kalman__pdf_example} illustrates two cases: one where the measurement error is low (thus having a marked effect on the  \emph{a posteriori} state estimate), and one where measurement error is high (having relatively little effect). Note that the noisy measurement not only introduces a minimal shift in the mean from the \emph{a priori} state model to the \emph{a posteriori} state estimate but also incorporates a significant level of uncertainty into the \emph{a posteriori} state estimate.

 
\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.6]{figures/Kalman_good_sensor.png}
\includegraphics[scale=0.6]{figures/Kalman_noisy_sensor.png}
\caption{An illustration of the state and measurement variable distributions for a single  Kalman predict/update step. Here, the fixed variables are \(\mu_p=5.0, \, \sigma_p=1.0, \, \mu_{ap} = \mu_{p} + 1.0, \, \sigma_w = 0.5, \) and  \(\mu_z = 5.2\). The figures depicts two cases. Top: measurement noise that is low relative to the noise inherent in the state model ($\sigma_z=0.4$); this yields $\mathbf{K}_k=0.9$.  Bottom: measurement noise that is high ($\sigma_z=1.4$); this yields $\mathbf{K}_k=0.4$.}
\label{fig:Kalman__pdf_example}
\end{figure}

\begin{tcolorbox}
    \begin{exmp}{The Kalman filter's application in allowing GPS to be robust.}\\

\textbf{Problem Statement:}
