Suppose we have a GPS receiver in a moving vehicle, and you want to accurately estimate the vehicle's position and velocity while driving through urban canyons with tall buildings that can block or reflect GPS signals, leading to noisy and unreliable measurements.\\

\textbf{Kalman Filter Application:}

A Kalman filter can be employed to improve the accuracy of position and velocity estimates by fusing noisy GPS measurements with a dynamic motion model of the vehicle.

    \end{exmp}
\end{tcolorbox}

\textbf{System Model:}

\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{State Variables:} The state vector includes the position (latitude and longitude) and velocity (speed and direction) of the vehicle:\\ 
   - State Vector, $\mathbf{x}_k$ = [latitude, longitude, speed, direction]
   \item \textbf{State Transition Model (Predict):} The state evolves with time based on the vehicle's dynamics (e.g., constant velocity model). This is the prediction step.\\
      - Predicted State, $\mathbf{\hat{x}}_{k|k-1} = \mathbf{F}_k \mathbf{x}_{k-1|k-1}$\\
   - $\mathbf{F}_k$: State transition model
   
    \item \textbf{Observation Model (Update):} GPS provides measurements of the vehicle's position (latitude and longitude) with some noise.\\
   - Measurement: \(\mathbf{z}_k\) = [measured\_latitude, measured\_longitude]\\
   - Measurement Equation: $\mathbf{z}_k = \mathbf{H}_k \mathbf{x}|_k + \mathbf{v}_k$\\
   - $\mathbf{H}_k$: Observation model\\
   - $\mathbf{v}_k$: Measurement noise (GPS measurement error)

\end{enumerate}

\bigskip


\textbf{Kalman Filter Steps:}

\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Initialization:} Initialize the Kalman filter with an initial state estimate and covariance matrix.

    \item \textbf{Prediction} (Time Update):
\begin{align*}
\text{Predicted State, } \mathbf{\hat{x}}_{k|k-1} &= \mathbf{F}_k \cdot \mathbf{x}_{k-1 \mid k-1} \\
\text{Predicted Covariance, } \mathbf{P}_{k|k-1} &= \mathbf{F}_k \mathbf{P}_{k-1} \mathbf{F}_k^T + \mathbf{Q}_k
\end{align*}
Where:
\(\mathbf{Q}_k\): Process noise covariance

\item \textbf{Measurement Update} (Correction): Update the state estimate using GPS measurements.

\begin{align*}
\text{Kalman Gain, } \mathbf{K}_k &= \mathbf{P}_{k|k-1} \mathbf{H}_k^T  (\mathbf{H}_k \mathbf{P}_{k|k-1} \mathbf{H}_k^T + \mathbf{R}_k)^{-1} \\
\text{Updated State Estimate, } \hat{\mathbf{x}}_{k|k} &= \hat{\mathbf{x}}_{k|k-1} + \mathbf{K}_k  (\mathbf{z}_k - \mathbf{H}_k  \hat{\mathbf{x}}_{k|k-1}) \\
\text{Updated Covariance, } \mathbf{P}_{k|k} &= (\mathbf{I} - \mathbf{K}_k \mathbf{H}_k)  \mathbf{P}_{k|k-1}
\end{align*}
Where:
\(\mathbf{R}_k\): Measurement noise covariance (GPS measurement error)

\end{enumerate}

\begin{tcolorbox}
    \begin{exmp}{Numerical (1D) Example of Kalman filter with GPS }
        In this example, we'll create a simple 1D Kalman filter to estimate the position of a moving vehicle using noisy GPS measurements. In this  this example we assume constant velocity motion along a straight path.
    \end{exmp}
\end{tcolorbox}
For the first 20 time steps, the GPS has not triangulated and so no measurements are available. Whereas the vehicle is moving at constant velocity, assume the transition model of the vehicle's position is subject to a constant drift. This drift can be seen in the first 20 time steps of Figure \ref{fig:Kalman_GPS}. No measurement updates are available during this time.  Once the GPS location data are available (with some inherent noise), the drift is corrected. It is also clear that the Kalman filter allows the GPS measurements to reduce the variance in the process model (the \emph{a priori} estimate of position). 
\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.5]{figures/Kalman_GPS_position.png}
\includegraphics[scale=0.5]{figures/Kalman_GPS_offset.png}
\caption{The position (top) and the offset from the true position (bottom).}
\label{fig:Kalman_GPS}
\end{figure}

The code for this example is available as \href{https://www.ece.unb.ca/kengleha/Textbook/Code/Kalman_Example_GPS.py}{\texttt{Kalman\_Example\_GPS.py}}.\\

A second example compares a GPS with a Kalman filter to a state estimate using only a system model (using IMUs), and in the case where the IMU sensors drift away from the true position (a constant velocity profile). Figure \ref{fig:KalmanDrift} illustrates the position estimation with and without the use of GPS data. Clearly, even though the GPS measurements are noisy, they correct the offset in position due to drift in the system model.\\

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.6]{figures/KalmanDrift.png}
\caption{The effect of estimate drift without a Kalman filter.}
\label{fig:KalmanDrift}
\end{figure}

The code for this example is available as \href{https://www.ece.unb.ca/kengleha/Textbook/Code/KalmanDrift.py}{\texttt{KalmanDrift.py}}.\\

\chapter{Analysis of Time-Series Data}
In most real-world scenarios, the estimation of ensemble statistics is not practical as an ensemble of sample functions is not available. Rather, only a single sample function exists. Having only one sample function. we must make \emph{time-averages} rather than \emph{ensemble averages}. Therefore, it is required that the process be wide-sense ergodic (which implies wide-sense stationarity).\\

\section{Tests of Self-Stationarity}
The assertion of true wide-sense stationarity requires ensemble statistics. If we have only one sample function \(x_i(t)\), we must consider self-stationarity of that sample function.\\

\subsubsection{Trend Testing}
To assess self-stationarity in the wide-sense, we must consider the stationarity of the time-average mean and time-average autocorrelation function. From a single sample function \(x_i(t)\) of length \(T\) seconds, we can divide the entire time record into \(N\) intervals of length $\Delta$. This gives \(N\) epochs \(\{ 0, T_1, \ldots T_N\), where \(T_N = T\). Figure \ref{fig:Time_stationarity} depicts this scenario for \(N=5\). \\

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.4]{figures/Time_stationarity.png}
\caption{The use of epochs to assess self-stationarity.}
\label{fig:Time_stationarity}
\end{figure}

Using these epochs of length $\Delta$, we can compute an ensemble of \(N\) time-average means and autocorrelation functions. \\

First, the ensemble of time-average means:    

\[
\begin{aligned}
    \hat{\mu}_{1} & = \frac{1}{\Delta}\int_{0}^{T_1}x_i(t) \, dt \\
    \vdots \\
    \hat{\mu}_{N} & = \frac{1}{\Delta}\int_{T_{N-1}}^{T_N}x_i(t) \, dt 
\end{aligned}
\]

And, the ensemble of time-average autocorrelation functions:    

\[
\begin{aligned}
    R_\mathrm{xx_1}(\tau) & = \frac{1}{\Delta}\int_{0}^{T_1}x_i(t) x_i(t + \tau)\, dt \\
    \vdots \\
    R_{\mathrm{xx_1}}(\tau) & = \frac{1}{\Delta}\int_{T_{N-1}}^{T_N}x_i(t) x_i(t+\tau)\, dt 
\end{aligned}
\]

To test for trends in time-series data, one can use statistical tests or visual inspection. Common methods include:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Augmented Dickey-Fuller Test: It is a common test for unit roots in time series data. If the null hypothesis is rejected, it suggests the presence of a trend.
    \item Kendall's Tau or Spearman's Rank Correlation: These non-parametric tests can identify trends in data.
    \item Visual Inspection: Plot the time series data and look for patterns that indicate trends, such as linear or nonlinear trends.
\end{itemize}

\bigskip
\textbf{Challenge: The choice of the epoch length, $\Delta$}
\begin{itemize}
    \item \(\Delta << T \) to get a suffucnet number if epochs in the ensemble to test for trends.
    \item $\Delta$ must be large enough to give a time-average estimate with sufficiently low variance. 
\end{itemize}

\section{Criteria for Ergodicity}
To demonstrate WS ergodicity we must show that as \(T \rightarrow \infty\):

\begin{itemize}
    \item \(E[\hat{\mu}_\mathrm{x}] \rightarrow \mu_\mathrm{x} \)
    \item \(Var[\hat{\mu}_\mathrm{x}] \rightarrow 0\)
     \item \(E[R_\mathrm{xx}](\tau) \rightarrow \mu_\mathrm{x} \)
    \item \(Var[R_\mathrm{xx}(\tau)] \rightarrow 0\)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Theorem 6.1}: if $X(t)$ is a Gaussian, WSS process with $\mu_\mathrm{x}$ and $C_\mathrm{xx}(\tau)$, a sufficient condition for \(X(t)\) to be WS ergodic is:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item \( \int_{-\infty}^\infty C_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) \, d \tau < \infty \)
    \item \( \int_{-\infty}^\infty \ | \tau | C_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) \, d \tau < \infty \)
        \item \( \int_{-\infty}^\infty C^2_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) \, d \tau < \infty \)
    \item \( \int_{-\infty}^\infty \ | \tau | C^2_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) \, d \tau < \infty \)
\end{enumerate}

\section{Estimation Errors}

As with ensemble estimates, estimation errors exist in time-average estimates. Consider the estimate of a true parameter \(\theta\) given by  \(\hat{\theta}\). The relevant estimation errors are:

\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Bias}.  \(\text{b}[\hat{\theta}] = E[\hat{\theta}] - \theta\). 
    \begin{itemize}
        \item If \(\text{b}[\hat{\theta}] = 0\) then the estimator is said to be \emph{unbiased}.
    \end{itemize}
    

    \item \textbf{Variance}.  \(\text{Var}[\hat{\theta}] = E[(\hat{\theta} - E[\hat{\theta}])^2]\).

    \item \textbf{Mean square error}. \(\text{MSE}[\hat{\theta}] = E[(\hat{\theta} - \theta)^2]\).
    \begin{itemize}
        \item  An estimator is said to be \emph{consistent} if $\text{MSE} \rightarrow$ as \(T\rightarrow \infty\).
        \item This can also be expressed as  \(\text{MSE}(\hat{\theta}) = \text{Var}[\hat{\theta}] + \text{b}^2[\hat{\theta}]\) where $\text{Var}[\hat{\theta}]$ is the AC component of the MSE and \(\text{b}^2[\hat{\theta}]\) is the DC component.
    \end{itemize}
   
    \item \textbf{Normalized standard error}. (for \(\text{b}(\hat{\theta}) = 0)\)
    \[
    \varepsilon = \frac{\sqrt{\text{Var}[\hat{\theta}]}}{\theta}
    \]

    This is often expressed as a percentage.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Estimate of the Mean, $\hat{\mu}_\mathrm{x}$}

\[
\hat{\mu}_\mathrm{x} = \frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^T
X(t) \, dt
\]
\subsubsection*{Bias error}

\[
\text{b}[\hat{\mu}_\mathrm{x}] = E[\hat{\mu}_\mathrm{x}] -  \mu_\mathrm{x}
\]
\[
\begin{aligned}
    E[\hat{\mu}_\mathrm{x}]  & = E \left [ \frac{1}{T} \int_0^T X(t) \, dt \right ] \\
    & =  \frac{1}{T} \int_0^T E \left [ X(t) \right ] \, dt \\ 
    & = \mu_\mathrm{x}
\end{aligned}
\]

Therefore
\[
\text{b}[\hat{\mu}_\mathrm{x}] = 0
\]

and the estimator is \emph{unbiased}.

\subsubsection{Variance of the estimate of the mean}
\[
\begin{aligned}
 \text{Var}[\hat{\mu}_\mathrm{x}] = & E \left [ (\hat{\mu}_\mathrm{x} - \mu_\mathrm{x})^2 \right ] \\
        & = E [ \hat{\mu}^2_\mathrm{x}] - E^2[\hat{\mu}_\mathrm{x}] \\
        & = E^2[\hat{\mu}_\mathrm{x}] - {\mu}^2_\mathrm{x}\\
        & = E \left [ \frac{1}{T} \int_0^T x(u) \, du \cdot \frac{1}{T} \int_0^T x(\nu) \, d \nu \right ] - {\mu}^2_\mathrm{x}\\
        & = \frac{1}{T^2}  \int_0^T  \int_0^T E \left [  x(u) x(\nu) \right ] \, du \, d \nu   - {\mu}^2_\mathrm{x}\\
        & = \frac{1}{T^2}  \int_0^T  \int_0^T \phi_\mathrm{xx}(\nu-u) \, du \, d \nu   - {\mu}^2_\mathrm{x}
\end{aligned}
\]

Since \[
\mathrm{C}_\mathrm{xx}(\nu-u) = \mathrm{C}_\mathrm{xx}(\nu-u) - \mu^2_\mathrm{x}
\]

Then,

\[
\text{Var}[\hat{\mu}_\mathrm{x}] = \frac{1}{T^2}  \int_0^T  \int_0^T \mathrm{C}_\mathrm{xx}(\nu-u) \, du \, d \nu
\]

Let \( \tau = \nu - u\):
\[
\text{Var}[\hat{\mu}_\mathrm{x}] = \frac{1}{T^2}  \int_{-T}^T \mathrm{C}_\mathrm{xx} \left ( T - |\tau| \right ) \, d \tau  \quad \text{(Papoulis, p. 222)}
\]

Rearranging:
\[
\text{Var}[\hat{\mu}_\mathrm{x}] = \frac{1}{T}  \int_{-T}^T \mathrm{C}_\mathrm{xx} \left (1 - \frac{|\tau|}{T}  \right ) \, d \tau
\]

Now \emph{iff}:

\[
\int_{-\infty}^\infty \mathrm{C}_\mathrm{xx} (\tau) \, d \tau < \infty
\]

and

\[
\int_{-\infty}^\infty |\tau| \mathrm{C}_\mathrm{xx} (\tau) \, d \tau < \infty
\]

then $\text{Var}[\hat{\mu}_\mathrm{x}] \rightarrow 0$ as \(T \rightarrow \infty\), and the estimator is \emph{consistent}. These two results correspond to criteria 1 and 2 in Theorem 6.1.\\

\emph{Note:} for \(T\) large (compared to values of \(\tau\) over which $\mathrm{C}_\mathrm{xx} (\tau)$ is significant), then:
\[
\boxed{\text{Var}[\hat{\mu}_\mathrm{x}] \approx \frac{1}{T}  \int_{-T}^T \mathrm{C}_\mathrm{xx}  \, d \tau}
\]

The normalized standard error is:
\[
\varepsilon \% = \frac{\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}[\hat{\mu}_\mathrm{x}]}}{\mu_\mathrm{x}} \times 100\%
\]
This does not reduce to any convenient form. 
\begin{tcolorbox}
    \begin{exmp}{The Estimate the Mean for Bandlimited White Noise.}
    
    Consider The autocorrelation function and the power spectral density of pure bandlimited white noise, as illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:BLWN}. Find the analytic form of the variance of the estimate of the mean.
    \end{exmp}
\end{tcolorbox}
\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.6]{figures/BLWN_t.png}
\includegraphics[scale=0.6]{figures/BLWN_f.png}
\caption{The autocorrelation function (top) and the power spectral density (bottom) of pure bandlimited white noise.}
\label{fig:BLWN}
\end{figure}

\[
\phi_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) = B N_o \frac{\sin(2 \pi B \tau)}{2 \pi B \tau} + \mu^2_\mathrm{x} = B N_o \, \mathrm{sinc}( 2 B N_o) + \mu^2_\mathrm{x}
\]

\[
\mathrm{C}_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) =  B N_o \frac{\sin(2 \pi B \tau)}{2 \pi B \tau} 
\]

For the estimator
\[
\hat{\mu}_\mathrm{x} = \frac{1}{T}\int_0^T x(t) \, dt \quad \rightarrow \quad  \text{choose } T>> \frac{1}{2B}
\]

Therefore,
\[
\begin{aligned}
    \text{Var}[\hat{\mu}_\mathrm{x}] & \approx \frac{1}{T}  \int_{-\infty}^\infty \mathrm{C}_\mathrm{xx}  \, d \tau \\
        & \approx \frac{1}{T}  \int_{-\infty}^\infty B N_o \frac{\sin(2 \pi B \tau)}{2 \pi B \tau}  \, d \tau \\
        & \approx \frac{\sigma^2_\mathrm{x}}{2B} \, \mathrm{V^2}
\end{aligned}
\]

Therefore, 
$\text{Var}[\hat{\mu}_\mathrm{x}] \rightarrow 0$ as \(T \rightarrow \infty\), and the estimator is \emph{consistent}.\\

Consider the standard error for this estimator:
\[
\varepsilon \% = \frac{\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^2_\mathrm{x}}{2 B T}}}{\mu_\mathrm{x}} \times 100\%
\]

\subsection{Estimate of the Variance, \(\hat{\sigma^2_\mathrm{x}}\)}


First, assume that \(\mu_\mathrm{x} = 0.\) Consider the estimator:
\[
\hat{\sigma}^2_\mathrm{x} = \frac{1}{T}\int_0^T x^2(t) \, dt
\]

\subsubsection*{Bias Error}
First, investigate the bias of the estimator:
\[
\mathrm{b}[\hat{\sigma}^2_\mathrm{x}] = E[\hat{\sigma}^2_\mathrm{x}] - \sigma^2_\mathrm{x}
\]

\[
E[\hat{\sigma}^2_\mathrm{x}] = E \left [  \frac{1}{T} \int_0^T x^2(t) \, dt \right ] = \frac{1}{T} \int_0^T E [x^2(t)] \, dt = \sigma^2_\mathrm{x}
\]

Therefore, the estimate of the variance is unbiased.\\

\subsubsection{Variance of the estimate of the variance}
Now consider the variance of \(\hat{\sigma}^2_\mathrm{x}\):

\[
\begin{aligned}
    \mathrm{Var}[\hat{\sigma}^2_\mathrm{x}] & = E [ (\hat{\sigma}^2_\mathrm{x} - {\sigma}^2_\mathrm{x})^2] \\
    & = E [\hat{\sigma}^4_\mathrm{x}] - 2\hat{\sigma}^4_\mathrm{x} + {\sigma}^4_\mathrm{x} \\
    & = E [\hat{\sigma}^4_\mathrm{x}] - {\sigma}^4_\mathrm{x} \\
    & = E \left [  \frac{1}{T} \int_0^T x^2(u) \, du \cdot \frac{1}{T} \int_0^T x^2(\nu) \, d \nu\right ] -{\sigma}^4_\mathrm{x} \\
    & = \frac{1}{T^2} \int_0^T \int_0^T E[x^2(u) x^2(\nu) ] \, du \,d \nu - {\sigma}^4_\mathrm{x} 
\end{aligned}
\]
To go any further, we must assume some distribution for \(X(t)\). Assume it is Gaussian with \(\mu_\mathrm{x} = 0\):
\[
\begin{aligned}
    E[x^2(u) x^2(\nu)] & = 2 \phi^2_\mathrm{xx}(u-\nu) + \sigma^4_\mathrm{x} \quad \text{from Piersol, p. 94}\\
    & = 2 \mathrm{C}^2_\mathrm{xx}(u-\nu) + \sigma^4_\mathrm{x} 
\end{aligned}
\]

\[
\begin{aligned}
    \mathrm{Var}(\hat{\sigma}^2_\mathrm{x}) & = \frac{1}{T^2} \int_0^T \int_0^T 2 \mathrm{C}^2_\mathrm{xx}(u-\nu) + \sigma^4_\mathrm{x}  \, du \, d \nu - \sigma^4_\mathrm{x} \\
    & = \frac{1}{T^2} \int_0^T \int_0^T 2 \mathrm{C}^2_\mathrm{xx}(u-\nu) \, du \, d \nu
\end{aligned}
\]

Let \(\tau = u - \nu\):
\[
\mathrm{Var}[\hat{\sigma}^2_\mathrm{x}] = \frac{2}{T}\int_{-T}^T \left ( 1 - \frac{|\tau|}{T}\right ) \mathrm{C}^2_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) \, d \tau
\]

Now \emph{iff}:

\[
\int_{-\infty}^\infty \mathrm{C}^2_\mathrm{xx} (\tau) \, d \tau < \infty
\]

and

\[
\int_{-\infty}^\infty \mathrm{C}^2_\mathrm{xx} |\tau|(\tau) \, d \tau < \infty
\]

then \(\text{Var}[\hat{\sigma}^2_\mathrm{x}] \rightarrow 0\) as \(T \rightarrow \infty\), and the estimator is \emph{consistent}. These two results correspond to criteria 3 and 4 in Theorem 6.1.\\

\emph{Note:} for \(T\) large (compared to values of \(\tau\) over which $\mathrm{C}_\mathrm{xx} (\tau)$ is significant), then: 

\[
\boxed{\mathrm{Var} [\hat{\sigma}^2_\mathrm{x}] \approx \frac{2}{T}\int_{-\infty}^\infty \mathrm{C}^2_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) \, d \tau}
\]

The normalized standard error is:
\[
\varepsilon \% = \frac{\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}[\hat{\sigma}^2_\mathrm{x}]}}{\sigma^2_\mathrm{x}} \times 100\%
\]
which does not reduce to any convenient form.

\begin{tcolorbox}
    \begin{exmp}{Continue the bandlimited white noise example for the estimate of the variance.}
   
    \end{exmp}
\end{tcolorbox}

For our process \(X(t)\):
\[
\mathrm{C}_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) =  B N_o \frac{\sin(2 \pi B \tau)}{2 \pi B \tau} 
\]

Note that the magnitude of the peak of the autocorrelation function is \(N_0 B\), which is also equal to the MSV ( and since \(\mu_\mathrm{x} = 0\), is equal to the variance \(\sigma^2_\mathrm{x}\).

Therefore:
\[
N_o B = \sigma^2_\mathrm{x}
\]

So 
\[
\mathrm{C}_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) =  \sigma^2_\mathrm{x} \frac{\sin(2 \pi B \tau)}{2 \pi B \tau} 
\]

and the magnitude of \(\Phi_\mathrm{xx} (\omega) = \frac{\sigma^2_\mathrm{x} }{2 B}\).\\

Using
\[
{\mathrm{Var} [\hat{\sigma}^2_\mathrm{x}] \approx \frac{2}{T}\int_{-\infty}^\infty \mathrm{C}^2_\mathrm{xx}(\tau) \, d \tau}
\]

We have
\[
\begin{aligned}
    \text{Var}[\hat{\sigma}^2_\mathrm{x}] & \approx \frac{2}{T}  \int_{-\infty}^\infty \mathrm{C}^2_\mathrm{xx}  \, d \tau \\
        & \approx \frac{2}{T}  \int_{-\infty}^\infty \sigma^4_\mathrm{x} \left ( \frac{\sin(2 \pi B \tau)}{2 \pi B \tau} \right ) ^2 \, d \tau \\
        & \approx \frac{\sigma^4_\mathrm{x}}{BT} 
\end{aligned}
\]

Consider the standard error for this estimator:
\[
\begin{aligned}
    \varepsilon \% & = \frac{\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^4_\mathrm{x}}{B T}}}{\sigma^2_\mathrm{x}} \times 100\% \\
        & = \frac{1}{\sqrt{BT}} \times 100\% 
\end{aligned}
\
\]

If \(T\) is a parameter over which we have control, choose 
\[
T = \frac{1}{B \varepsilon^2}
\]

to achieve a desired error performance.

\subsection{Estimate of the Autocorrelation Function, \(\hat{\phi}_\mathrm{xx} (\tau)\)}


Consider the estimator:
\[
\mathrm{R}_\mathrm{xx} (\tau) = \hat{\phi}_\mathrm{xx} (\tau) = \frac{1}{T}\int_0^T x(t) x(t + \tau)\, dt
\]

\subsubsection*{Bias Error}

\[
E \left [ \hat{\phi}_\mathrm{xx} (\tau) \right ] = \frac{1}{T} \int_0^T x(t) x(t+\tau) \, dt = \phi_\mathrm{xx} (\tau)
\]
Therefore, the estimator is unbiased.

\subsubsection*{Variance of the estimate \( \hat{\phi}_\mathrm{xx} (\tau) \)}
\[
\begin{aligned}
    \text{Var}[\hat{\phi}_\mathrm{xx} (\tau)] & = E \left [ \hat{\phi}^2_\mathrm{xx} (\tau) \right ] - \phi^2_\mathrm{xx} (\tau) \quad \text{since } E\left [ \hat{\phi}_\mathrm{xx} (\tau) \right ] = \phi_\mathrm{xx}  (\tau) \\
    & = E \left [ \frac{1}{T} \int_0^T x(u) x(u+\tau) \, du \cdot \frac{1}{T} \int_0^T x(\nu) x(\nu+\tau) \, d \nu\right ] - \phi^2_\mathrm{xx} (\tau) \\
    & = \frac{1}{T^2} \int_0^T \int_0^T x(u) x(u+\tau) x(\nu) x(\nu+\tau) \, du \, d \nu - \phi^2_\mathrm{xx} (\tau)
\end{aligned}
\]

To go any further, we must assume some distribution for \(X(t)\). Assume it is Gaussian with \(\mu_\mathrm{x} = 0\):

\[
    \text{Var}[\hat{\phi}_\mathrm{xx} (\tau)] = \frac{1}{T^2} \int_0^T \int_0^T \left \{ \phi^2_\mathrm{xx} (\tau) + \phi^2_\mathrm{xx} (u - \nu) + \phi_\mathrm{xx} (u - \nu + \tau) \phi_\mathrm{xx} (u - \nu - \tau) \right \}   \, du \, d \nu  - \phi^2_\mathrm{xx} (\tau)
\]

Let \(\gamma = u - \nu\):

\[
    \text{Var}[\hat{\phi}_\mathrm{xx} (\tau)] = \frac{1}{T} \int_{-T}^T \left \{ \phi^2_\mathrm{xx} (\gamma) + \phi_\mathrm{xx} (\gamma + \tau) \phi_\mathrm{xx} (\gamma - \tau) \right \} \left \{ 1 - \frac{|\gamma|}{T}\right \} \, d \gamma
\]

Note that as \(T \rightarrow \infty, \text{Var}[\hat{\phi}_\mathrm{xx} (\tau)] \rightarrow 0\) \emph{iff} the integrals are finite, in which case the estimator is consistent.\\

For \(T\) large (with respect to the values of \(\phi_\mathrm{xx} (\tau) \) that are significant): 

\[
    \text{Var}[\hat{\phi}_\mathrm{xx} (\tau)] \approx \frac{1}{T} \int_{-\infty}^\infty  \phi^2_\mathrm{xx} (\gamma) + \phi_\mathrm{xx} (\gamma + \tau) \phi_\mathrm{xx} (\gamma - \tau)  \, d \gamma
\]

\begin{tcolorbox}
    \begin{exmp}{Continue the bandlimited white noise example for the estimate of the autocorrelation function.}
   
    \end{exmp}
\end{tcolorbox}

\[
\begin{aligned}
    \text{Var}[\hat{\phi}_\mathrm{xx} (\tau)]  & = \left (\frac{\sigma^4_\mathrm{x}}{2 B T} \right )\left (  1 + \frac{\sin^2 2 \pi B \tau}{(2 \pi B \tau)^2}\right ) \\
    & = 
    \begin{cases}
        \frac{\sigma^4_\mathrm{x}}{B T} & , \tau = 0 \\
        \frac{\sigma^4_\mathrm{x}}{2 B T} & , \tau \rightarrow \infty 
    \end{cases}
\end{aligned}
\]

Note that at \( \tau = 0\) the expression for \(\text{Var}[\hat{\phi}_\mathrm{xx} (\tau)] \) is that of \(\text{Var}[\hat{\sigma}_\mathrm{x}] \) and at \( \tau \rightarrow \infty\) it is that of \( \text{Var}[\hat{\mu}^2_\mathrm{x} ] \).

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.6]{figures/Var_ACF.png}
\caption{The variance of $\hat{\phi}_\mathrm{xx} (\tau)$ as a function of \(\tau\). Here, two examples are shown, both with  $B = 2 Hz$ and $T = 10 s$, but with $\sigma_\mathrm{x} = 1.0 $ and $\sigma_\mathrm{x} = 1.1$}
\label{fig:Var_ACF}
\end{figure}

The normalized standard error is:

\[
\begin{aligned}
\varepsilon \% & = \frac{\sqrt{\mathrm{Var}[\hat{\phi}_\mathrm{xx}]}}{{\phi}_\mathrm{xx}} \\
    & = \frac{1}{2 B T} \left ( 1 + \frac{\phi^2_\mathrm{xx}(0)}{{\phi^2}_\mathrm{xx}(\tau)} \right )
\end{aligned}
\]

which equals \(\frac{1}{BT}\) at \(\tau=0\) and goes to \(\infty\) as \(\tau \rightarrow \infty\).

\section{Power Density Spectrum Estimation}

We will consider the implementation and performance of a few types of PSD estimators. 

\subsection{Analog PSD Estimator}

Analog estimation of the PSD may be considered as a sliding narrowband bandpass filter of width \(B_e\), providing estimates of the power density at each centre frequency. The filter width \(B_e\) can be interpreted as the resolutio of teh PSD estimator. At each frequency, we can consider the amplitude of the PSD as an estimate of the MSV of the signal, as illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:PSD_analog}.

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.6]{figures/PSD_analog.png}
\caption{An illustration of the stages of an analog PSD estimator.}
\label{fig:PSD_analog}
\end{figure}

\[
\hat{\mathrm{MSV}}(f,B_e) = \frac{1}{T}\int_0^T x^2(t,f,B_e) \, dt
\]

\subsubsection*{Estimate of the Mean of \( \hat{\Phi}_\mathrm{xx} (f) \)}
To generate a power density spectrum estimate, we divide the estimate of the MSV by the area under the narrowband filter, \(2 B_e\) (accounting for both sides of the PSD):

\[
\begin{aligned}
    \hat{\Phi}_\mathrm{xx} (f) & = \frac{1}{2 B_e T} \int_0^T x^2(t,f,B_e) \, dt \\
    & = \frac{\hat{\mathrm{MSV}}(f,B_e)}{2 B_e}
\end{aligned}
\]

We also know that 
\[
\mathrm{MSV}(f,B_e) = 2 \int_{f-\frac{B_e}{2}}^{f+\frac{B_e}{2}} \Phi_\mathrm{xx} (f) \, df
\]

Consider now the expected value of the PSD estimate:

\[
\begin{aligned}
 E \left [ \hat{\Phi}_\mathrm{xx} (f) \right ] & = \frac{1}{2 B_e} \underbrace{\frac{1}{T}\int_0^T E [x^2(t,f,B_e)] \, dt}_{\mathrm{MSV}(f,B_e)} \\   
 & = \frac{1}{2 B_e}  \int_{f-\frac{B_e}{2}}^{f+\frac{B_e}{2}} \Phi_\mathrm{xx} (f) \, df
\end{aligned}
\]

We can see that in general, 
\[
E \left [ \hat{\Phi}_\mathrm{xx} (f) \right ] \ne \Phi_\mathrm{xx} (f)
\]

unless 
\[
\frac{1}{2 B_e}  \int_{f-\frac{B_e}{2}}^{f+\frac{B_e}{2}} \Phi_\mathrm{xx} (f) \, df \, = \, \frac{\hat{\mathrm{MSV}}(f,B_e)}{2 B_e}
\]

That is, the area under \(\Phi_\mathrm{xx} (f)\) between \(f \pm \frac{B_e}{2}\) is equal to the rectangle of height \(\hat{\mathrm{MSV}}(f,B_e)\). This is is true if \(\Phi_\mathrm{xx} (f)\) is relatively constant over \(f \pm \frac{B_e}{2}\). The larger the value of \(B_e\), the less likely this is true.

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.4]{figures/PSD_analog_bias.png}
\caption{The bias inherent in an analog PSD estimate. Small filter bandwidth (\(Be_1\)) introduces smaller bias than larger filter bandwidth (\(Be_2\)).}
\label{fig:PSD_analog_bias}
\end{figure}


\subsubsection*{Estimate of the Variance of \( \hat{\Phi}_\mathrm{xx} (f) \)}

We will neglecting the bias error to simplify the derivation:


\[
\begin{aligned}
    \hat{\Phi}_\mathrm{xx} (f) & = \frac{\hat{\mathrm{MSV}}(f,B_e)}{2 B_e}
\end{aligned}
\]

\[
\begin{aligned}
    \mathrm{Var}[\hat{\Phi}_\mathrm{xx} (f)] & = \frac{\mathrm{Var}[\hat{\mathrm{MSV}}(f,B_e)]}{4 B_e^2}
\end{aligned}
\]

\[
\begin{aligned}
    \mathrm{Var}[\hat{\mathrm{MSV}}(f,B_e)] & =  \frac{\sigma^4_\mathrm{x}(f,B_e)}{B_e T} \quad \text{from the proof of } \mathrm{Var}[ \hat{\sigma^2_\mathrm{x}}]\\
\end{aligned}
\]

Therefore,
\[
\begin{aligned}
    \mathrm{Var}[\hat{\Phi}_\mathrm{xx} (f)] & = \frac{\sigma^4_\mathrm{x}(f,B_e)}{4 B_e^3 T} \\
\end{aligned}
\]

Since \(\sigma^2(f,B_e) = 2 B_e \Phi_\mathrm{xx} (f)\):
\[
\boxed{
    \mathrm{Var}[\hat{\Phi}_\mathrm{xx} (f)] = \frac{\Phi^2_\mathrm{xx} (f)}{B_e T} \\
}
\]

and the normalized standard error is:
\[
\varepsilon = \frac{1}{\sqrt{B_e T}}
\]

\subsection{PSD Estimator using the Autocorrelation Function}

We know that 
\[
{\Phi}_\mathrm{xx} (f) = \mathcal{F} \left \{{\phi}_\mathrm{xx} (f)\right \}
\]

Estimating the autocorrelation function requires using a finite data window. Let's assume that the autocorrelation function is truncated at \(\tau = [-\tau_m,\tau_m]\). This is equivalent to multiplying the true autocorrelation function by a rectangular window \(p(\tau) = \mathrm{rect}[-\tau, \tau]\):
\[
\phi_\mathrm{xx_T} (\tau) = \phi_\mathrm{xx_T} (\tau) \cdot p(\tau)
\]
and 

\[
\Phi_\mathrm{xx_T} (f) = \Phi_\mathrm{xx_T} (f) \ast  P(f)
\]

The frequency spectrum \(P(f)\) of the truncation window takes the form a a sinc function with zero-crossing of the main lobe at \(\frac{1}{2 \tau_m}\), as illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:P(f)}.

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.6]{figures/P(f).png}
\caption{The frequency spectrum of the autocorrelation function truncation window.}
\label{fig:P(f)}
\end{figure}

Due to the fact that the spectrum \(\phi_\mathrm{xx_T} (\tau)\) is convolved with this since function, \(P(f)\) effectively "smears" the true spectrum \(\phi_\mathrm{xx} (\tau)\). Also, it introduces a spectral resolution roughly equal to the wide of the main lobe:
\[
B_e \approx \frac{1}{\tau_m}
\]

If we use all of the data toward the estimatio of the autocorrelation function, then \(t_m = T\) and 
\[
B_e \approx \frac{1}{T}
\]

A spectral estimate \(\hat{\Phi}_\mathrm{xx_T} (f) \) taken from an estimate of the autocorrelation function \(\hat{\phi}_\mathrm{xx_T} (\tau) \) will be subject to this smearing and resolution limitation.\\

\subsection{FFT Estimate}
The FFT operates on a finite data sequence \(x_T(t)\) on \([0,T]\) as illustrated in Figure \ref{fig:FFT_rand}.


\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.6]{figures/FFT_rand.png}
\caption{The data window \(x_T(t)\) used in the FFT estimate of the PSD.}
\label{fig:FFT_rand}
\end{figure}

The PSD estimate is:

\[
\hat{\Phi}_\mathrm{xx_T} (f) = \frac{|X_T(f)|^2}{T}
\]

where \[
X_T(f) = \mathrm{FFT}[x_{_T}(t)]
\]

Consider now the expected value of the PSD estimate:
\[
\begin{aligned}
    E \left [ \hat{\Phi}_\mathrm{xx_T} (f) \right ] & =\frac{1}{T} E \left [ |X_T(f)|^2 \right ]\\
        & = \frac{1}{T} E \left [ \left |\int_0^T x_{_T}(t) e^{-j 2 \pi f t} \, dt \right |^2 \right ]\\
        & = \frac{1}{T} \int_0^T \int_0^T E \left [ x_{_T}(u) x_{_T}(\nu) \right ] e^{-j 2 \pi f u} e^{-j 2 \pi f \nu} \, du \, d \nu  \\
        & = \frac{1}{T} \int_0^T \int_0^T  {\phi}_\mathrm{xx} (\nu - u) e^{-j 2 \pi f u} e^{-j 2 \pi f \nu} \, du \, d \nu  \\
        & = \int_{-T}^T  {\phi}_\mathrm{xx} (\tau) e^{-j 2 \pi f \tau} \left [ 1 - \frac{|\tau|}{T}\right ] d \tau
\end{aligned}
\]
Thus, in general:
\[
E \left [ \hat{\Phi}_\mathrm{xx_T} (f) \right ] \ne \Phi_\mathrm{xx} (f)
\]

However, for \(T\) large with respect to values of \( \tau \) for which \(\phi_\mathrm{xx}\) is significant:
\[
E \left [ \hat{\Phi}_\mathrm{xx_T} (f) \right ] \approx \int_{-\infty}^\infty  {\phi}_\mathrm{xx} (\tau) e^{-j 2 \pi f \tau} \, d \tau = \Phi_\mathrm{xx} (f)
\]

We assert the general form of the normalized standard error:
\[
\varepsilon  = \frac{1}{\sqrt{B_e T}}
\]



Considering now that the data window can be considered as 
\[
x_{_T} = x(t) \times p(t)
\]
where \(p(t) = \mathrm{rect[0,T]}\), then the spectrum of \(p(t)\) is a sinc function of width \(\frac{2}{T}\) as depicted in Figure \ref{fig:FFT_P(f)}.

\begin{figure}[h!]
\centering
\includegraphics[scale=0.6]{figures/FFT_P(f).png}
\caption{The frequency spectrum of the data truncation window.}
\label{fig:FFT_P(f)}
\end{figure}

This suggests a frequency resolution \(B_e \approx \frac{2}{T}\). 

Therefore,
\[
\varepsilon  = \frac{1}{\sqrt{B_e T}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
\]

This is an interesting result, as the normalized standard error is fixed, \emph{independent of the data length}, \(T\). \\

To reduce the variance of the FFT PSD estimate, the general approach is to choose a relatively large \(T\), and divide it into segments. The PSD estimates of the segments are then ensemble averaged to provide a lower-variance PSD estimate. This is referred to as the Welch method. There are two drawbacks to this approach:
\begin{itemize}
    \item The resolution of the PSD estimate of each segemnt is \(B_e = \frac{2}{T_{seg}}\). If the data record \(T\) is divided into \(M\) segments, then \(T_{seg} = \frac{T}{M} \) and the effective frequency resolution will be degraded to \(M B_e\).
    \item Choosing a larger \(T\) may increase the likelihood that the data used violate the assumption of stationarity.
\end{itemize}
